=== PAGE: Agent Evaluation ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Agent-Evaluation-3d5fca3384fc80feaac2d743eaa88e60

LLM as a judge 
Human Annotation
Code-based Evals
1. LLM-as-a-Judge
Use another LLM to evaluate the agent's output.
Give the input + agent response + evaluation criteria to a judge LLM.
Judge scores things like:
Correctness
Relevance
Helpfulness
Reasoning quality
Tool usage
Can use a 1–5 score, pass/fail, or pairwise comparison.
Example:
Agent answers: “The capital of Australia is Sydney.”
Judge LLM checks → Incorrect → Expected answer: Canberra.
Pros: Scalable, fast, good for subjective qualities
Cons: Judge can be biased or make mistakes; needs a good evaluation rubric.
2. Human Annotation
A human reviews the agent's output and labels/scores it.
For example:
Humans can evaluate things that are difficult to capture with code, such as tone, usefulness, safety, and overall quality.
Pros: High-quality ground truth
Cons: Expensive, slow, and potentially subjective.
3. Code-Based Evals
Use deterministic code/rules to check whether the agent did what it was supposed to do.
For example:
assertresponse.status_code==200assert"Canberra"inresponse.textasserttool_called=="weather_api"assertlatency<2
For an agent, you can evaluate:
Did it call the correct tool?
Did it call the tool the correct number of times?
Were the parameters correct?
Is the output in the required format?
Did it return the expected value?
Did it violate any constraints?
Latency / token usage / cost
Pros: Fast, cheap, objective, highly reproducible
Cons: Only works well when you can define deterministic criteria.
The key difference
Code-based → “Did it follow this exact rule?”
LLM judge → “How good/correct was this response?”
Human annotation → “Would a real person consider this good?”
Agent evaluation
Router →Determines which skill to call the function
Skill evaluation → Did we call the right function with the right parameter is passed
Memory
Path planning and reflection evals 
Planning → evaluate the plan generated by the agent before action → LLM as a judge
Reflection → decide whether to repeat the previous steps → LLM as a judge, code-based
Trajectory → Determine whether the optimal path was taken by the agent overall

Prompt Engineering
Prompt engineering is the process of creating clear, effective prompts that guide the AI model to generate accurate responses. 
Zero short prompting: Perform a task without specific examples
One short: Prompt is given with one example
Few short: Provides multiple examples of the task
Iterative prompting: Basically, telling what to avoid in the response
Negative prompting: Refining the model output through feedback or follow-up prompts
Fine Tuning: 
It is a process where LLM is further trained on a smaller, specific dataset to specialise its knowledge for a  particular task
Benefits:
Improved performance
Customisation
Reduced cost
eg: healthcare, finance, legal, customer service
eg: med-PaLM, Bloomberg, Llamma
Rag
It is a technique that connects LLM with external knowledge sources like databases, documents, or the web to provide accurate, up-to-date, and domain-specific answers by retrieving the relevant info before generating a response and reducing hallucinations 
AI Agent:
A software system that understands and 
Responsible AI principles
https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/principles-and-approach
RAG Steps
data processing and indexing
Load, split, embed, store
retrieval and response generation
question, retrive, prompt, llm, response
Fine Tuning/RAG/Prompt
user query → prompt → response
User query → retiver →llm → response
large data → base llm → fine tuned llm (from documents) → response

What is LangChain?
LangChain is an open-source framework used to build applications powered by Large Language Models (LLMs).
It helps developers with:
Data loading
Text splitting
Embeddings
Retrieval
Chains
Agents
Memory
Output parsing
LangChain simplifies the development of:
Chatbots
RAG applications
AI assistants
Search systems
Workflow automation
What are Document Loaders?
Document Loaders are components in LangChain that:
Load data from different sources
Convert them into standardized Document objects
Supported sources:
PDF
CSV
HTML
Markdown
JSON
YouTube
Wikipedia
GitHub
MS Office Docs
Purpose:
Standardize data before sending it to LLM pipelines
Document Object Structure
A LangChain Document contains:
Creating a Document Object
from langchain_core.documents impor tDocument

data=Document(
page_content="This is a LangChain document",
id=1,
metadata={"source":"AV"}
)

print(data)
Output:
Document(
id='1',
metadata={'source':'AV'},
page_content='This is a LangChain document'
)
Types of Document Loaders
1. File-Based Loaders
Load data based on file type.
Examples:
CSV
PDF
HTML
Markdown
JSON
2. Source-Based Loaders
Load data from external platforms.
Examples:
YouTube
Wikipedia
GitHub
AWS
Azure
Categories
CSV Loader
Used to load CSV files row-by-row as Documents.
Example
from langchain_community.document_loaders.csv_loader import CSVLoader

loader=CSVLoader(
file_path="./iris.csv",
metadata_columns=['species'],
csv_args={"delimiter":","}
)

data=loader.load()
CSV Metadata Example
data[0].metadata
Output:
{
'source':'./iris.csv',
'row':0,
'species':'setosa'
}
CSV Page Content Example
print(data[0])
Output:
page_content='
sepal_length:5.1
sepal_width:3.5
petal_length:1.4
petal_width:0.2
'

Text Splitting
Large documents are split into smaller chunks before embeddings.
Why?
LLMs have token/input limitations
Smaller chunks improve retrieval accuracy
CharacterTextSplitter Example
fromlangchain.text_splitterimportCharacterTextSplitter

text_splitter=CharacterTextSplitter(
chunk_size=100,
chunk_overlap=20,
separator="\n"
)

chunks=text_splitter.split_text(page_content)

Vector Stores in LangChain
After splitting:
Generate embeddings
Store embeddings in vector databases
Purpose:
Fast similarity search
Retrieval for RAG systems
Chroma Vector Store Example
fromlangchain.vectorstoresimportChroma
fromlangchain.embeddingsimportOpenAIEmbeddings

embeddings=OpenAIEmbeddings()

db=Chroma.from_documents(
documents,
embeddings
)

query="What is transformer?"

docs=db.similarity_search(query)
RAG Pipeline Flow
Steps
1. Load
Load documents using Document Loaders.
2. Split
Split large text into chunks.
3. Embed
Convert chunks into vectors.
4. Store
Store embeddings in vector DB.
5. Retrieve
Retrieve relevant chunks.
6. Generate
LLM generates final response.
LangChain Chains
Chains combine multiple operations together.
Example:
Prompt → LLM → Output
LCEL (LangChain Expression Language)
Modern way to build chains in LangChain.
Example
fromlangchain_core.promptsimportPromptTemplate
fromlangchain_openaiimportOpenAI

prompt_template="Tell me a {adjective} joke"

prompt=PromptTemplate(
input_variables=["adjective"],
template=prompt_template
)

llm=OpenAI()

chain=prompt|llm

result=chain.invoke("funny")
LangChain Core Components
Vector Database

A Vector Database is a specialized database designed to store, manage, and search high-dimensional vectors.
Vectors are numerical representations of data such as:
Text
Images
Audio
Videos
User behavior
Unlike traditional databases that search exact values using rows and columns, vector databases search based on semantic similarity.
Example:
Searching for "red dress" can also return images/products related to red gowns even if the exact words are not present.
Vector databases are widely used in:
AI applications
Recommendation systems
Semantic search
Chatbots
Image retrieval
NLP systems
How Vector Databases Work
1. Data Ingestion
Convert Data into Vectors
Machine learning embedding models convert data into vectors.
Examples:
Text → Word embeddings
Images → Image embeddings
Store the Vectors
The generated vectors are stored along with metadata.
Example metadata:
Product name
User ID
Timestamp
2. Indexing
Vector databases use advanced indexing methods for fast retrieval.
Indexing in a vd creates a map or guide, pointing to where each vector is located in a list
I
Common indexing techniques:
HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World)
ANN (Approximate Nearest Neighbor)
PQ (Product Quantization)
Goal:
Balance speed and accuracy
Efficient search on huge datasets
3. Querying
Similarity Search
Instead of exact matching, vector databases find the most similar vectors.
Common similarity metrics:
Retrieval Process
Convert query into vector
Compare with stored vectors
Return nearest matches
4. Integration with Applications
Vector databases provide:
APIs
SDKs
ML integrations
Used in:
Search engines
Recommendation systems
AI assistants
Real-time analytics
5. Scalability & Performance
To handle large-scale AI workloads, vector databases support:
Distributed architecture
Parallel processing
Sharding
Hardware optimization
This enables:
Real-time querying
Low latency
High throughput
Key Features of Vector Databases
High-Dimensional Data Handling
Efficiently manages vectors with hundreds or thousands of dimensions.
Used for:
Images
Text embeddings
Audio embeddings
Efficient Similarity Search
Quickly retrieves semantically similar items.
Supports:
Cosine distance
Euclidean distance
Hamming distance
Advanced Indexing
Uses optimized indexing techniques like:
HNSW
ANN
PQ
Real-Time Querying
Provides low-latency responses for:
Search systems
Recommendations
AI assistants
AI & ML Integration
Works seamlessly with:
LLMs
Embedding models
ML pipelines
Metadata Support
Stores metadata alongside vectors for better filtering and querying.
Example:
{
  "product":"Shoes",
  "category":"Sports",
  "vector": [0.12,0.45, ...]
}
Applications of Vector Databases
Recommendation Systems
Used by platforms like:
Netflix
Amazon
Spotify
Example:
Recommend movies/products similar to user interests.
Semantic Search Engines
Search based on meaning instead of keywords.
Example:
Searching "best phone for photography" Returns camera-focused smartphones.
Natural Language Processing (NLP)
Supports:
Chatbots
Language translation
Sentiment analysis
Semantic search
Image & Video Retrieval
Find visually similar images/videos.
Example:
Upload an outfit image → find similar clothes.
Biometrics & Security
Used in:
Face recognition
Authentication systems
Airport verification systems

Efficient for:

What is Data Division (Data Splitting)?
Definition:
Data division is the process of splitting a dataset into multiple parts so that a machine learning model can be trained, validated, and tested properly.
Purpose:
Evaluate model performance on unseen data
Prevent overfitting
Estimate real-world performance
🔹 1. Train–Test Split
Definition:
The dataset is split into two parts:
Training set → used to train the model
Test set → used to evaluate the final model
Common Split Ratios:
80% Train – 20% Test
70% Train – 30% Test
Diagram (conceptual):
[ Training Data | Test Data ]


Advantages:
Simple and fast
Good for large datasets
Disadvantages:
Performance depends on how the split is done
Can be unreliable for small datasets
🔹 2. Train–Validation–Test Split
Definition:
The dataset is divided into three parts:
Training set → trains the model
Validation set → tunes hyperparameters
Test set → final evaluation
Common Ratio:
70% Train – 15% Validation – 15% Test
Why Validation Set?
Helps choose best model without touching test data
🔹 3. K-Fold Cross Validation
Definition:
The dataset is split into K equal parts (folds).
The model is trained K times, each time using:
K−1 folds for training
1 fold for validation
Final performance = average of all K results
Diagram (K=5):
Fold1 →Test|Fold2–5 →Train
Fold2 →Test|Fold1,3–5 →Train
...


Common Values of K:
K = 5
K = 10
Advantages:
Uses entire dataset efficiently
More reliable performance estimate
Disadvantages:
Computationally expensive
🔹 4. Stratified Train–Test Split
Definition:
A train–test split where class proportions are preserved in both training and test sets.
Used When:
Classification problems
Imbalanced datasets
Example:
If dataset has:
70% Class A
30% Class B
Both train and test sets keep the same ratio.
🔹 5. Leave-One-Out Cross Validation (LOOCV)
Definition:
A special case of K-Fold where:
K = number of data points


Each iteration:
1 sample → test
remaining → train
Pros:
Maximum data usage
Cons:
Very slow for large datasets
StandardScaler
What is StandardScaler?
Definition:
StandardScaler is a feature scaling technique that transforms data so that:
Mean = 0
Standard Deviation = 1
It standardizes features using Z-score normalization.
📐 Formula
For each feature:
z = (x − μ) / σ


Where:
x = original value
μ = mean of the feature
σ = standard deviation of the feature
📊 What Does It Do?
Before scaling:
Feature 1:  [10,20,30]
Feature 2:  [1000,2000,3000]


After StandardScaler:
Feature1:[-1.22, 0, 1.22]
Feature2:[-1.22, 0, 1.22]


➡️ Both features now have equal importance.
⭐ Why is StandardScaler Important?
🔹 1. Prevents Dominance of Large Values
Models that use distance or gradients get biased if one feature has larger values.
🔹 2. Improves Model Performance
Helps algorithms converge faster and give better accuracy.
🔹 3. Required for Many Algorithms
Classification evaluation model
A confusion matrix is a NxN table that helps evaluate a machine learning model's performance by
comparing its predictions to actual values, with rows and columns representing classes.
TP: Correctly predicted positive
TN: Correctly predicted negative
FP: Predicted positive, but actually negative (Type I error)
FN: Predicted negative, but actually positive (Type II error)
🔹 1. Accuracy
✅ Definition
Accuracy measures how many predictions the model got correct overall.
📐 Formula
Accuracy=TP+TNTP+TN+FP+FN\text{Accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}
Accuracy=TP+TN+FP+FNTP+TN
🧠 When to use
Classes are balanced
Cost of FP and FN is similar
🔹 2. Precision
✅ Definition
Precision measures how many predicted positives are actually positive.
📐 Formula
Precision=TP/FP+TP
🧠 When to use
False positives are costly
Example: Spam detection
🔹 3. Recall (Sensitivity / True Positive Rate)
✅ Definition
Recall measures how many actual positives were correctly identified.
📐 Formula
Recall=TP/FN+TP
🧠 When to use
False negatives are costly
Example: Disease detection
🔹 4. F1 Score
✅ Definition
F1 Score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall, balancing both.
📐 Formula
F1 Score = 2*(recall * precision/(recall+ precision))
🧠 When to use
Imbalanced datasets
Need a balance between precision & recall
🔹 Quick Summary Table
ROC
ROC Curve plots TPR against FPR at various thresholds, separating signal from noise.
X-axis: False Positive Rate - FP/FP + TN
Y-axis: True Positive Rate (TPR) - TP/TP+FN
Each point on the curve corresponds to a different classification threshold.
AUC (Area Under the Curve) is the area under the ROC curve.
📐 Value Range
0.0 – 1.0
🧠 Interpretation
1.0 → Perfect classifier
0.5 → Random guessing
< 0.5 → Worse than random (labels likely flipped)
👉 Probabilistic meaning:
AUC is the probability that the model ranks a random positive example higher than a random negative one.
Regression - Evaluation methods
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
 Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
 R2 Score
 Adjusted R2 Score
Any predictive model can be evaluated based on correctness and the reliability
Mean Absolute error: 
sum(Abs(actual values - predicted values))/n
Take the predicted values from your model for each data point
Subtract the corresponding actual values from the predicted values
Convert the differences in absolute errors
Calculate the sum of absolute errors
Find the mean of absolute error
R² measures the proportion of variance in the dependent variable that is explained by the independent variables.
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
Definition:
Mean Absolute Error measures the average absolute difference between actual values and predicted values.
It tells you how far predictions are from real values, on average, without considering direction.
MAE = (1 / n) × Σ | yᵢ − ŷᵢ |

n = number of observations
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
Definition:
Root Mean Squared Error measures the square root of the average squared differences between actual and predicted values.
It penalizes large errors more heavily than MAE.
Formula:
RMSE = √[ (1 / n) × Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² ]


Where:
n = number of observations
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
📌 R² Score (Coefficient of Determination)
Definition:
R² Score explains how much variance in the target variable is explained by the model.
Values range from 0 to 1, where higher is better.
Note:
Variance measures how spread out the data points are from the mean.
It tells you how much the values vary in a dataset.
Low variance → data points are close to the mean
High variance → data points are widely spread
Formula:
R² =1 − [ Σ (yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² / Σ (yᵢ − ȳ )² ]


Where:
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
ȳ = mean of actual values
📌 Adjusted R² Score
Definition:
Adjusted R² adjusts the R² score by penalizing unnecessary features.
It is more reliable when comparing models with different numbers of predictors.
Formula:
AdjustedR² =1 − [ (1 −R²) × (n −1) / (n −p −1) ]


Where:
n = number of observations
p = number of independent variables
R² = R² score
KNN
Definition:
K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is a supervised, non-parametric algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It makes predictions based on the K closest data points to a new input.
How Does KNN Work?
Step-by-Step Process
Choose K
Number of nearest neighbors (e.g., K = 3, 5)
Calculate Distance
Compute distance between the new point and all training points
(commonly Euclidean distance)
Select K Nearest Neighbors
Pick the K points with the smallest distances
Make Prediction
Classification → majority vote
Regression → average of values
Distance Formula (Most Common)
Euclidean Distance
d = √[ (x₁ − y₁)² + (x₂ − y₂)² + ... + (xₙ − yₙ)² ]


📊 KNN for Classification
Example:
K = 5
3 neighbors → Class A
2 neighbors → Class B
➡️ Predicted class = A
📈 KNN for Regression
Example:
K = 4
Neighbor values:
10, 12, 14, 16


➡️ Prediction = (10 + 12 + 14 + 16) / 4 = 13
🔢 Choosing the Value of K
👉 Usually found using cross-validation
⭐ Advantages of KNN
Simple to understand
No training phase
Works well with small datasets
⚠️ Disadvantages of KNN
Slow for large datasets
Sensitive to noise
Requires feature scaling
High memory usage
❗ Why Scaling is Important in KNN
KNN is distance-based, so features with large values dominate.
➡️ Always use StandardScaler or MinMaxScaler
🧠 When to Use KNN
✅ Small to medium datasets
✅ Low-dimensional data
❌ Very large datasets
❌ High-dimensional data (curse of dimensionality)
Logistic Regression:
Used for a classification problem based on linear regression
odds ratio: 
log loss function -? perfect model will be 0
Logistic Regression (statsmodels) — Interpretation & Diagnostics
Logistic Regression is used when the target variable is categorical (usually binary: 0/1, Yes/No).
Instead of predicting a value, it predicts a probability using the log-odds (logit) function.
📌 Logistic Regression Equation
log(p / (1 − p)) = β₀ + β₁X₁ + β₂X₂ + ... + βₙXₙ


Where:
p = probability of class 1
β = coefficients
X = features
🔍 statsmodels Logistic Regression Summary — Key Metrics
When you run:
model = sm.Logit(y, X).fit()


You get the following diagnostics 👇
📌 Coefficients (β)
Meaning:
Represents the change in log-odds for a 1-unit increase in the feature.
Interpretation Tip:
Positive β → increases probability of class 1
Negative β → decreases probability of class 1
📌 Odds Ratio (exp(β))
Meaning:
Exponentiating coefficients gives odds ratios.
OddsRatio= e^β


Interpretation:
OR > 1 → feature increases odds
OR < 1 → feature decreases odds
📌 p-value (Very Important)
Meaning:
Tests whether a feature is statistically significant in predicting the target class.
Null Hypothesis:
Feature has no effect on the outcome.
Desired Value:
p-value <0.05  ✅


📌 Log-Likelihood (LL)
Meaning:
Measures how well the model fits the data.
Higher (less negative) → better fit
Used for model comparison, not absolute interpretation.
📌 Log-Likelihood Ratio (LLR)
Meaning:
Compares the fitted model against a null model (no predictors).
Desired:
Large value + smallp-value


📌 LLR p-value
Meaning:
Tests whether at least one predictor significantly improves the model.
Desired Value:
LLRp-value <0.05  ✅


➡️ Confirms the model is better than guessing.
📌 Pseudo R² (McFadden’s R²)
Meaning:
Indicates how well the model explains the outcome
(Not the same as linear regression R²)
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
👉 Even 0.3 is considered very good in logistic regression.
📌 Convergence
Meaning:
Indicates whether the optimization algorithm successfully found optimal coefficients.
Desired:
converged:True  ✅


❌ False → model may be unreliable.
🧪 Assumption Checks for Logistic Regression
📌 Multicollinearity (VIF)
Same as Linear Regression
High correlation between features inflates variance
Check using VIF
Desired:
VIF <5 (preferably <3)


📌 Linearity of Log-Odds
Meaning:
Logistic regression assumes a linear relationship between predictors and log-odds, not the target itself.
❌ Violations → poor predictions
📌 Independence of Observations
Each data point must be independent
No repeated or grouped observations
🧠 What Logistic Regression Does NOT Use
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics (After Training)
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Logistic Regression:
Predicts probabilities using log-odds for classification problems.
p-value:
Tells whether a feature significantly affects the probability of the outcome.
Pseudo R²:
Measures model fit but is not interpreted like linear regression R².
Decision Tree
A Decision Tree is a supervised learning algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It works by splitting data into smaller subsets based on feature conditions, forming a tree-like structure.
🧠 Core Idea (Intuition)
“Ask a sequence of yes/no questions to reach a decision.”
Each internal node = question
Each branch = answer
Each leaf node = final prediction
⚙️ How Decision Tree Works
Choose the best feature to split the data
Split data to maximize purity
Repeat recursively
Stop when:
Node is pure
Max depth reached
Minimum samples reached
📐 Splitting Criteria (Very Important)
📌 1. Gini Impurity (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures how impure a node is.
Formula:
Gini =1 − Σ (pᵢ)²


Desired Value:
Lower isbetter (0 = pure node)


📌 2. Entropy (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures disorder or randomness in a node.
Formula:
Entropy = − Σ pᵢ log₂(pᵢ)


Desired Value:
Loweris better


📌 3. Information Gain
Meaning:
Measures how much entropy is reduced after a split.
Formula:
InformationGain=Entropy(parent) −WeightedEntropy(children)


Desired Value:
Higheris better


📌 4. Variance Reduction (Regression Trees)
Meaning:
Used in Decision Tree Regression.
Tree splits data to minimize variance in target values.
Desired:
Lower variance in leaf nodes


📊 Example (Classification)
Dataset
Tree Rule
IFStudent=Yes→Buy=Yes
ELSE→Buy=No


➡️ Final prediction comes from leaf node
📌 Important Hyperparameters (Control Overfitting)
🔹 Max Depth
Maximum depth of tree
Desired:
Small–moderate (e.g.,3–10)


🔹 Min Samples Split
Minimum samples required to split a node
Higher value → simpler tree
🔹 Min Samples Leaf
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node
Helps smooth predictions
🔹 Max Features
Number of features considered at each split
⚠️ Overfitting in Decision Trees
Problem:
Trees can memorize data
Very deep trees → poor generalization
Solution:
Pruning
Limiting depth
Ensemble methods (Random Forest)
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics
📌 For Classification
📌 For Regression
🧠 Advantages of Decision Tree
Easy to interpret
No feature scaling required
Handles non-linear data
Works with categorical & numerical data
❌ Disadvantages
Overfitting
Sensitive to small data changes
Lower accuracy than ensembles
📌 Assumptions (Minimal!)
👉 Decision Trees are assumption-light
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Decision Tree:
A tree-based model that splits data using feature conditions to make predictions.
Gini / Entropy:
Measures node impurity; lower values mean purer splits.
Overfitting:
Deep trees overfit; pruning and depth control fix this.
🔁 Comparison (Quick)
Decision Tree Hyperparameters
Hyperparameters control how the tree grows and help prevent overfitting.
📌 random_state
What it is:
Controls the randomness in the tree-building process.
Why it matters:
Ensures reproducible results
Same data + same random_state → same tree
Desired Practice:
Settoany fixed number (e.g.,42)


👉 Does not affect accuracy directly — only reproducibility.
📌 criterion
What it is:
Metric used to decide how to split nodes.
Classification:
gini (default)
entropy
Regression:
squared_error
Desired:
gini → faster
entropy → more information-theoretic
📌 max_depth (Most Important)
What it is:
Maximum depth of the tree.
Effect:
Large depth → overfitting
Small depth → underfitting
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
3 – 10


📌 min_samples_split
What it is:
Minimum number of samples required to split a node.
Effect:
Higher value → simpler tree
Lower value → complex tree
Typical Values:
2 (default),5,10


📌 min_samples_leaf
What it is:
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node.
Why important:
Smooths predictions
Reduces overfitting
Typical Values:
1, 2, 5


📌 max_features
What it is:
Number of features considered when looking for the best split.
Options:
None → all features
sqrt → √features (common for classification)
log2
Integer or float
Effect:
Smaller value → more randomness
Helps reduce overfitting
📌 max_leaf_nodes
What it is:
Maximum number of leaf nodes.
Effect:
Controls model complexity
Smaller value → simpler tree
✂️ Pruning (Very Important)
📌 What is Pruning?
Definition:
Pruning removes unnecessary branches to prevent overfitting.
Two types:
Pre-pruning
Post-pruning
🔹 Pre-Pruning (Early Stopping)
Done while building the tree using hyperparameters:
🔹 Post-Pruning (Cost Complexity Pruning)
📌 ccp_alpha
What it is:
Controls cost-complexity pruning.
How it works:
Penalizes complex trees
Removes branches that add little value
Effect:
Larger ccp_alpha → more pruning
Smaller ccp_alpha → deeper tree
Desired Practice:
Tuneusing cross-validation


📊 Example (Intuition)
DecisionTreeClassifier(
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Balanced tree
➡️ Less overfitting
➡️ Reproducible results
🧠 Key Takeaways
max_depth is the most important hyperparameter
Pruning controls overfitting
Decision Trees do not need scaling
Always tune hyperparameters using cross-validation
🎯 Interview One-Liners
random_state:
Ensures reproducibility of results.
Pruning:
Reduces overfitting by removing unnecessary branches.
max_depth:
Controls the complexity of the tree.
Handling Imbalanced Data in Decision Trees
An imbalanced dataset occurs when one class appears much more frequently than the other(s).
Example:
Class0:95%
Class1:5%


➡️ A naive model may always predict the majority class and still get high accuracy.
🚨 Why Imbalance Is a Problem
Model becomes biased toward majority class
Minority class is poorly predicted
Accuracy becomes misleading
🛠️ Techniques to Handle Imbalance in Decision Trees
📌 1. class_weight (Most Important)
What it is:
Assigns higher importance (penalty) to the minority class during splitting.
📐 How It Works
Misclassifying minority class is penalized more
Tree learns splits that focus on minority samples
✅ Common Options
class_weight="balanced"


Automatically assigns:
weight = total_samples / (n_classes × class_frequency)


🧠 When to Use
Strong class imbalance
Classification problems
When recall on minority class matters
📌 2. Frequency / Sample Weights (sample_weight)
What it is:
Assigns a weight to each training sample.
Minority class samples get higher weights
Similar effect to class_weight, but more flexible
📐 Example
sample_weight = [5if y==1else1for yin y_train]
model.fit(X_train, y_train, sample_weight=sample_weight)


🧠 When to Use
You want custom control
Unequal importance per observation
📌 3. Resampling Techniques
🔹 Oversampling (Increase Minority Class)
Duplicate minority samples
Or generate synthetic samples (SMOTE)
Pros:
Improves recall
Cons:
Risk of overfitting
🔹 Undersampling (Reduce Majority Class)
Remove majority samples
Pros:
Faster training
Cons:
Loss of information
📌 4. Adjust Tree Hyperparameters
🔹 min_samples_leaf
Larger value prevents tree from ignoring minority class
min_samples_leaf =5or10


🔹 max_depth
Restricts overfitting to majority class patterns
max_depth =4 –8


📌 5. Change Evaluation Metrics (Very Important)
❌ Avoid accuracy
✅ Use:
Recall
Precision
F1-score
ROC–AUC
PR–AUC (very good for imbalance)
📌 6. Threshold Tuning
Instead of default:
0.5


Use:
Lower threshold → higher recallfor minorityclass


📊 Example (Decision Tree with Imbalance Handling)
from sklearn.treeimport DecisionTreeClassifier

model = DecisionTreeClassifier(
    class_weight="balanced",
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Better minority class detection
➡️ Reduced bias
🧠 Comparison: class_weight vs Resampling
🎯 Interview One-Liners
class_weight:
Assigns higher penalty to minority class misclassification.
sample_weight:
Adjusts importance of individual samples.
Best practice:
Combine class weighting with proper evaluation metrics.
✅ Best Strategy (Real Projects)
Start with class_weight="balanced"
Tune tree depth & leaf size
Use Recall / F1 instead of Accuracy
Try SMOTE if needed
Validate with cross-validation

Imbalanced data in Random Forest
SMOTE explained simply
Confusion matrix interpretation
End-to-end example

tell about over and under sampling with SMOTE algo
