Reflex Patterns: 
The Reflection Pattern focuses on improving AI’s ability to evaluate and refine its own outputs. Imagine an LLM reviewing its generated content or code as if it were a human reviewer, identifying errors, gaps, or areas that need improvement and then offering suggestions for how to improve.
Agent reviews its own output and identifies mistakes.
Uses feedback to improve future responses.
Example: Generate answer → Critique answer → Regenerate better answer.
Question
↓
Generate Answer
↓
Self-Critique
↓
Improve Answer
Tool Use Pattern
The Tool Use Pattern significantly broadens an LLM’s capability by allowing it to interact with external tools and resources to enhance its problem-solving abilities. Instead of relying solely on internal computations or knowledge, an AI following this pattern can access databases, search the web, or even execute complex functions via programming languages like Python.

Agent can call external tools/APIs when needed.
Examples: Search, Calculator, Database, Code Executor, RAG Retriever.
Question
↓
Need Tool?
↓
Call Tool
↓
Observe Result
↓
Generate Final Answer
Planning Pattern
The Planning Pattern enables an LLM to break down large, complicated tasks into smaller, more manageable components. Planning equips an agent with the ability to react to requests and strategically structure the steps needed to achieve a goal.
Agent breaks a complex task into smaller steps before execution.
Similar to how humans create a plan before acting.
Goal
↓
Create Plan
↓
Execute Step 1
↓
Execute Step 2
↓
...
↓
Final Result
Multi-Agent Pattern
The Multi-Agent Pattern builds upon the concept of delegation.
This pattern involves assigning different agents (which are instances of an LLM with specific roles or functions) to handle various subtasks. These agents can work independently on their assignments while also communicating and collaborating to achieve a unified outcome.
Multiple specialized agents collaborate.
Each agent has a specific role.
      Orchestrator
           ↓
┌───────────┼───────────┐
↓           ↓           ↓
Research    Coding     Review
Agent      Agent      Agent
└───────────┼───────────┘
↓
Final Answer
LLM as a judge 
G-eval → uses the cot prompting to stabilize and make judges more reliable and accurate
Relextion patterns 
Key Components of the Reflection Pattern
Generation Step: The process begins when a user provides an initial prompt, which could be a request to generate text, write code, or solve a complex problem. For example, a prompt might ask the AI to generate an essay on a historical figure or to implement an algorithm in a specific programming language.
Reflection Step: The reflection step is a critical phase where the AI model reviews its own generated content. This step involves:
Self-Critique: The model critiques its own work, identifying areas for improvement, such as factual errors, stylistic issues, or logical inconsistencies.
Feedback Generation: The AI generates specific feedback, which can include suggestions for restructuring content, adding details, or correcting mistakes.
Evaluation Criteria: The critique may be based on predefined criteria such as grammatical accuracy, coherence, relevance to the prompt, or adherence to specific formatting guidelines.
Iteration and Refinement: The feedback generated during the reflection step is used to guide the next generation of output. The AI incorporates the suggested changes and improvements into a new version of the content.
Adaptive Learning: Through this iterative process, the AI learns to recognize patterns in its own mistakes and refines its understanding of the task requirements.
Multiple Iterations: The process can be repeated for a fixed number of steps (e.g., 10 iterations) or until a specific stopping condition is met, such as achieving a certain level of content quality or encountering a “stop” keyword.
Flow
Step 1 – Generate: The user starts by providing a Prompt to the AI model. For example, the prompt might be: “Write a short story about a cat that travels to space.”
Step 2 – Output Text: The model generates a response based on the prompt, such as: “Once upon a time, there was a cat named Whiskers who found a magical rocket ship in his backyard. Whiskers hopped inside and launched into space, where he met alien cats from the planet Meowtar.”
Step 3 – Reflect: You review the generated output for quality at this stage. You might notice that the story lacks detail about Whiskers’ emotions or the challenges faced during the journey.
Step 4 – Reflected Text: You revise the text or make suggestions for improvement based on the reflection. The reflected version might include additional details: “Whiskers, feeling both excited and scared, stepped into the rocket ship. As the engines roared to life, he gripped the seat tightly, wondering if he would ever see home again. The journey through space was filled with strange sights and dangers, like meteor showers and cosmic storms, which tested Whiskers’ bravery.”
Step 5 – Iterate: This refined text can now be fed back into the generation process, potentially serving as a new prompt or an improved foundation for further text generation. Based on the reflected text, the model can generate a more polished version of the story.
Tool Based Patterns:
This pattern allows the LLM to interact with external systems 
Core Idea
Modularization of Tasks: Instead of relying on a monolithic AI model that tries to handle everything, the system breaks down user prompts and assigns them to specific tools (represented as Tool A, Tool B, Tool C). Each tool specializes in a distinct capability, which makes the overall system more efficient and scalable.
Specialized Tools for Diverse Tasks:
Tool A: This could be, for example, a fact-checker tool that queries databases or the internet to validate information.
Tool B: This might be a mathematical solver or a code execution environment designed to handle calculations or run simulations.
Tool C: Another specialized tool, possibly for language translation or image recognition.
Each tool is capable of querying information sources (e.g., databases, web APIs, etc.) as needed, suggesting a modular architecture where different sub-agents or specialized components handle different tasks.
Sequential Processing: The model likely runs sequential queries through the tools, which means that multiple prompts can be processed one by one, and each tool independently queries its respective data sources. This allows for fast, responsive results, especially when combined with tools that excel in their specific domain.
End-to-End Process:
Input: User asks “What is 2 times 3?”
Interpretation: The LLM recognizes this as a mathematical operation.
Tool Selection: The LLM selects the multiply tool.
Payload Creation: It extracts relevant arguments (a: 2 and b: 3), prepares a payload, and calls the tool.
Execution: The tool operates (2 * 3 = 6), and the result is passed back to the LLM to present to the user.
Supervisor Architecture
Network Architecture
Hierarchical architecture
LLM fine-tuning is the process of taking a pre-trained language model and training it further on a smaller, task-specific dataset so that it performs better for a particular domain or task.
A base LLM is trained on general internet data.
It knows a lot about many topics, but it doesn't know:
your company's policies
your writing style
medical terminology
legal documents
how you want responses formatted
Fine-tuning teaches the model these things
Types of Fine-Tuning
1. Full Fine-Tuning
The entire model is updated.
All weights
↓

Updated
Example:
70 billion parameters
All 70B parameters are trained.
Pros
Highest accuracy
Learns deeply
Best customization
Cons
Extremely expensive
Needs many GPUs
Large storage
Slow
2. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT)
Instead of updating every parameter...
Only a small number of parameters are updated.
Popular methods:
LoRA
QLoRA
Adapters
Prefix Tuning
Most companies use PEFT.
LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation)
This is the most common interview topic.
Instead of changing the original weights...
LoRA adds small trainable matrices.
Original weights stay frozen.
Original Model (Frozen)

      +
Small LoRA Layers

↓

Combined Output
Imagine a 7B model.
Without LoRA:
Train all 7 billion parameters.
With LoRA:
Train maybe only 10–50 million parameters.
Huge savings.
QLoRA
QLoRA combines:
Quantization (compressing model weights, e.g., to 4-bit)
LoRA adapters
Benefits:
Lower memory usage
Faster training
Can fine-tune large models on a single high-memory GPU
Fine-Tuning Process
Collect Dataset

↓

Clean Dataset

↓

Create Prompt-Response pairs

↓

Tokenize

↓

Train

↓

Evaluate

↓

Deploy
Pre-training teaches general language and knowledge.
Fine-tuning specializes a model for a domain, task, or behavior by updating model parameters.
RAG adds up-to-date external knowledge without changing the model.
LoRA/QLoRA are efficient fine-tuning methods widely used in production because they require much less compute than full fine-tuning.
LLM Fine-Tuning Process (Interview Notes)
Step 1: Dataset Collection
The first step in fine-tuning is collecting a high-quality dataset relevant to the target task.
The dataset typically consists of input-output (prompt-response) pairs.
Examples
Customer Support
Prompt: How do I reset my password?
Response: Click "Forgot Password" and follow the instructions sent to your email.
SQL Generation
Prompt: Show all employees with salary > 50000.
Response: SELECT * FROM employees WHERE salary > 50000;
Medical Assistant
Prompt: What are the symptoms of diabetes?
Response: Common symptoms include increased thirst, frequent urination...
Good Dataset Characteristics
High-quality and accurate
Domain-specific
Diverse examples
Properly labeled
Free from duplicates and noise
Rule: A model is only as good as the data it is trained on.
Step 2: Training
During training, the model learns by comparing its predictions with the expected answers and gradually updating its weights.
Training Pipeline
Dataset
    ↓
Forward Pass
    ↓
Prediction
    ↓
Loss Computation
    ↓
Backward Propagation
    ↓
Weight Update
    ↓
Repeat
What Happens in Each Step?
1. Forward Pass
The model receives the input and predicts an output.
Example:
Input:
"What is Kubernetes?"

Prediction:
"Kubernetes is a container."
2. Loss Computation
The prediction is compared with the correct answer.
Example:
Expected:
"Kubernetes is a container orchestration platform."

Predicted:
"Kubernetes is a container."
A loss function measures how wrong the prediction is.
High loss → Poor prediction
Low loss → Good prediction
The objective is to minimize the loss over time.
3. Backpropagation
The model calculates how much each parameter (weight) contributed to the error.
Using gradients, it determines:
"Which weights should change, and by how much?"
4. Weight Update
An optimizer (e.g., AdamW) updates the model's weights.
The updated weights help the model make better predictions in the next iteration.
This cycle repeats for thousands of batches across multiple epochs until the model converges.
How Do We Train?
There are multiple ways to fine-tune an LLM.
1. PyTorch Training Loop
You manually implement the training process.
Typical flow:
for batch in dataloader:
	outputs=model(batch)
	loss=compute_loss(outputs)
	loss.backward()
	optimizer.step()
Pros
Maximum flexibility
Full control over training
Cons
More code
Easier to make mistakes
2. Hugging Face Transformers Trainer API
The Trainer API abstracts away most of the training loop.
You mainly configure:
Model
Dataset
Training arguments
Evaluation strategy
The library handles:
Training loop
Optimizer
Checkpointing
Logging
Evaluation
Pros
Very easy to use
Less boilerplate
Industry standard
3. Hugging Face Accelerate
Accelerate simplifies training and inference across different hardware setups.
It enables:
Multi-GPU training
TPU support
Mixed precision (FP16/BF16)
Distributed training
without requiring major code changes.
Advantages
Faster training
Better GPU utilization
Scales from one GPU to many GPUs
Step 3: Evaluation
After training, the model must be evaluated to determine how well it performs.
Evaluation measures whether the model has learned the desired behavior and generalizes to unseen data.
There are two main approaches:
1. Automatic Evaluation (Benchmarks)
The model is tested on standardized datasets using predefined metrics.
Examples include:
MMLU
GSM8K
HumanEval
TruthfulQA
HellaSwag
These benchmarks provide quantitative scores that make it easier to compare different models.
Advantages
Fast
Reproducible
Easy to compare models
Limitation
LLM benchmarks are a very noisy proxy. They are useful for measuring generic capabilities but should not be treated as the final indicator of model quality.
A high benchmark score does not always translate to better real-world performance.
2. Human Evaluation
Humans manually review model outputs and judge their quality.
Typical evaluation criteria include:
Correctness
Helpfulness
Accuracy
Safety
Fluency
Relevance
Coherence
Human evaluation remains the gold standard because it captures qualities that automatic metrics often miss.
Interview Tip: Human evaluation is considered the most reliable way to assess an LLM's real-world usefulness.
Why Can Model Evaluations Be Misleading?
A common issue is benchmark contamination or evaluation bias.
Sometimes, models are trained on data that overlaps with the benchmark datasets.
As a result, the model may appear to perform exceptionally well simply because it has already seen similar examples during training.
This leads to inflated benchmark scores that do not necessarily reflect true reasoning ability.
Key Point
Model evaluations can be skewed toward models trained on data that overlaps with the evaluation dataset.
This is why benchmark results should always be interpreted carefully.
Summary
Dataset Collection
      ↓
Training
    • Forward Pass
    • Loss Computation
    • Backpropagation
    • Weight Update
      ↓
Evaluation
    • Automatic Benchmarks (fast but imperfect)
    • Human Evaluation (most reliable)
Interview One-Liners
What are the steps involved in fine-tuning an LLM?
Collect a high-quality dataset, train the model using forward pass, loss computation, backpropagation, and weight updates, then evaluate it using both automatic benchmarks and human evaluation.
Why is human evaluation important?
Human evaluation assesses qualities like correctness, helpfulness, safety, and fluency that automatic metrics often fail to capture, making it the most reliable measure of real-world model performance.
Why shouldn't we rely only on benchmarks?
Benchmarks are useful for comparing general capabilities, but they can be noisy or biased due to benchmark contamination. A high benchmark score does not always guarantee better real-world performance.
Prompt Tuning (Soft Prompt Tuning)
Definition
Prompt Tuning is a Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) technique where the LLM is completely frozen, and only a small set of learnable prompt embeddings (soft prompts) are trained.
Unlike normal prompts written in English, these prompts are continuous embedding vectors that the model learns automatically.
Training Process
Suppose we have a dataset of 1000 examples.
Example:
Input: I love this movie.
Output: Positive

Input: This movie is terrible.
Output: Negative
Step 1: Freeze the LLM
Freeze all model parameters.
Transformer Weights ❌ Frozen
Attention Weights ❌ Frozen
Embedding Layer ❌ Frozen
Feed Forward Layers ❌ Frozen
No weights inside the LLM will be updated.
Step 2: Initialize Learnable Prompt Embeddings
Create a small matrix of trainable vectors.
Example:
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]
Each prompt token is an embedding vector.
If
Prompt Length = 20
Embedding Dimension = 768
then
Soft Prompt Matrix

Shape = (20 × 768)
Initially, these vectors are randomly initialized.
Step 3: Concatenate Prompt with Input
For every training example
Instead of
I love this movie.
the model receives
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]

I love this movie.
These prompt vectors are added before the input embeddings.
Step 4: Forward Pass
The combined embeddings are passed through the frozen LLM.
Soft Prompt + Input

↓

Frozen Transformer

↓

Prediction
Step 5: Compute Loss
Compare the prediction with the correct label.
Example
Prediction : Negative

Actual : Positive
Compute the loss.
Step 6: Backpropagation
During backpropagation
Only the soft prompt embeddings receive gradients.
Loss

↓

Update P1 ✅
Update P2 ✅
Update P3 ✅
...
Update P20 ✅

LLM Weights ❌ No Update
Step 7: Repeat
The same prompt vectors are reused for every training example.
After each batch
P1 → P1'
P2 → P2'
...
They gradually become better at steering the frozen LLM toward the desired task.
Training Flow
Dataset
   │
   ▼
Initialize Random Soft Prompt
[P1][P2]...[P20]
   │
   ▼
Concatenate Prompt + Input
   │
   ▼
Frozen LLM
   │
   ▼
Prediction
   │
   ▼
Compute Loss
   │
   ▼
Backpropagation
   │
   ▼
Update ONLY the Soft Prompt
   │
   ▼
Next Batch
What Gets Updated?
Why Prompt Tuning Works
A pretrained LLM already contains knowledge about language, reasoning, and patterns.
Instead of changing the model itself, Prompt Tuning learns a small set of embedding vectors that guide (or steer) the frozen model toward a specific task.
Advantages
Very few trainable parameters
Low memory usage
Fast training
Easy to store and share
One base model can support many tasks by swapping soft prompts
Limitations
The prompt influences the model only at the input layer.
Performance may be lower than other PEFT methods (e.g., Prefix Tuning or LoRA) for complex tasks.
Interview Answer (30 seconds)
Prompt Tuning is a PEFT technique where the pretrained LLM is frozen and only a small set of trainable prompt embeddings, called soft prompts, are optimized. These prompt embeddings are prepended to the input embeddings and passed through the frozen model. During backpropagation, gradients update only the soft prompt matrix, while all LLM weights remain unchanged. This allows task adaptation by training only a few thousand parameters instead of billions.
Prefix Tuning
Definition
Prefix Tuning is a Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) technique where the LLM is frozen, but instead of learning only input prompt embeddings, we learn prefix key-value vectors that are injected into every Transformer layer's attention mechanism.
First, recall Self-Attention
In every Transformer layer, attention computes three things:
Input Embeddings
      │
      ▼
Linear Layers
      │
      ▼
Q (Query)
K (Key)
V (Value)
Attention then computes
Attention(Q, K, V)
The output depends on how Queries interact with Keys and Values.
Normal Transformer
Suppose your sentence is
I love AI
After embedding
[I]
[love]
[AI]
Layer 1 creates
Q1
K1
V1
Layer 2 again creates
Q2
K2
V2
and so on.
Nothing extra is added.
Prefix Tuning Idea
Instead of only adding prompt embeddings at the input,
we learn extra Keys and Values.
Imagine we learn
Prefix K

PK1
PK2
PK3
...

Prefix V

PV1
PV2
PV3
...
These are trainable.
What happens inside Layer 1?
Normally
Queries

Q

attend to

K

and use

V
With Prefix Tuning
Queries

Q

attend to

[Prefix Keys + Original Keys]

and use

[Prefix Values + Original Values]
So attention becomes
Keys

PK1
PK2
PK3

K1
K2
K3

Values

PV1
PV2
PV3

V1
V2
V3
The model can attend to both the learned prefixes and the actual input.
Why not Queries?
A common interview question:
Why are only Key and Value prefixed? Why not Query?
Because the Query comes from the actual input tokens.
The input should decide what it wants to look for.
The learned prefix acts as extra memory that the Query can attend to.
If we changed the Query as well, we'd change how the input itself asks questions.
Training Process
Suppose we have 1000 examples.
Exactly like Prompt Tuning.
Dataset
Movie is awesome → Positive
Movie is bad → Negative
...
Step 1
Freeze the LLM.
Attention ❌ Frozen

Feed Forward ❌ Frozen

Embeddings ❌ Frozen

Everything ❌ Frozen
Step 2
Initialize Prefix Parameters.
Suppose
20 Prefix Tokens
For every transformer layer.
If there are
24 Transformer Layers
then every layer gets
Prefix Keys

20 vectors

Prefix Values

20 vectors
Visual
Layer 1

PK
PV

Layer 2

PK
PV

Layer 3

PK
PV

...

Layer 24

PK
PV
These are randomly initialized.
Step 3
Forward Pass
Input
I love AI
↓
Layer 1
Q

↓

Attend over

PK + K

PV + V
↓
Layer 2
Again
Q

↓

PK + K

PV + V
↓
Output
Step 4
Compute Loss
Same as before.
Step 5
Backpropagation
Now,
ONLY
PK

PV
are updated.
Everything else stays frozen.
What gets updated?
Layer 1

PK ✅

PV ✅

Layer 2

PK ✅

PV ✅

...

Layer 24

PK ✅

PV ✅
Transformer weights
Attention ❌

Feed Forward ❌

Embeddings ❌
No updates.
Why is Prefix Tuning more powerful?
Prompt Tuning
Prompt

↓

Transformer

↓

Output
Once the input enters the transformer, there is no additional learned guidance.
Prefix Tuning
Input

↓

Layer 1 + Prefix

↓

Layer 2 + Prefix

↓

Layer 3 + Prefix

↓

...

↓

Output
Every layer receives learned information.
The model is continuously guided.
Analogy
Prompt Tuning
Imagine taking an exam.
Before entering,
your teacher says
"Remember, answer confidently."
That's it.
No more help.
Prefix Tuning
Now imagine the teacher is sitting beside you during every question.
Whenever you solve a question,
the teacher whispers
"Remember this concept."
"Watch out for this trick."
"Think about this formula."
The teacher is influencing your thinking throughout the exam.
That is Prefix Tuning.
Comparison with Prompt Tuning
Interview Answer
Prefix Tuning is a PEFT technique where the pretrained LLM remains frozen. Instead of learning only input prompt embeddings, it learns trainable prefix key and value vectors for every transformer layer. During attention, the model attends over both the learned prefix key/value vectors and the original key/value vectors. During backpropagation, only these prefix vectors are updated, while all original model weights remain unchanged.
The one concept I want to make sure is crystal clear
In Prompt Tuning, you were updating one soft prompt matrix:
Soft Prompt
[P1...P20]
In Prefix Tuning, you're updating many small matrices—one pair (Keys and Values) for each transformer layer:
Layer 1 : PK1, PV1
Layer 2 : PK2, PV2
Layer 3 : PK3, PV3
...
Layer 24 : PK24, PV24
So instead of learning one steering signal at the input, you're learning a steering signal at every layer of the network. That's the core idea behind Prefix Tuning.
Why was Adaptive Prompt Tuning introduced?
In Prompt Tuning, we learn one soft prompt.
For example,
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]
This same prompt is used for every input.
[P1...P20] + "Translate to French"

[P1...P20] + "Summarize this article"

[P1...P20] + "Write Python code"
But think about it...
Should the same prompt work equally well for:
Medical questions?
Legal questions?
Coding?
Translation?
Probably not.
Core Idea
Instead of learning one fixed prompt, we make the prompt adapt based on the input or task.
So,
Medical input
↓
[PM1][PM2]...[PM20]
Programming input
↓
[PC1][PC2]...[PC20]
Legal input
↓
[PL1][PL2]...[PL20]
Different inputs receive different learned prompts.
Architecture
Instead of
Input

↓

Fixed Soft Prompt

↓

Frozen LLM
we now have
Input

↓

Prompt Generator

↓

Adaptive Prompt

↓

Frozen LLM
The Prompt Generator decides what prompt should be used.
Training Process
Suppose our dataset contains different domains.
Medical:
Patient has fever.

Programming:
Reverse a linked list.

Legal:
What is contract law?
Step 1
Freeze the LLM.
Transformer ❌ Frozen

Attention ❌ Frozen

Embedding ❌ Frozen
Step 2
Initialize a Prompt Generator.
Instead of storing
[P1...P20]
we learn a small network that produces prompts.
For example
Input

↓

Small Neural Network

↓

Prompt Embeddings
Step 3
Medical Input
Patient has fever.
↓
Prompt Generator
↓
[PM1][PM2]...[PM20]
↓
Frozen LLM
↓
Prediction
Programming Input
Reverse a linked list.
↓
Prompt Generator
↓
[PC1][PC2]...[PC20]
↓
Frozen LLM
Notice that the prompt is different, even though the LLM is the same.
What gets updated?
Exactly like Prompt Tuning,
the LLM stays frozen.
Only the adaptation mechanism is trained.
Prompt Generator ✅

Generated Prompt ✅

Transformer ❌

Attention ❌

Embeddings ❌
Simple Analogy
Imagine you're a teacher.
Prompt Tuning
You always begin class with the same sentence.
"Today, pay attention."
Whether you're teaching:
Kindergarten
College
PhD students
You say the same thing.
Adaptive Prompt Tuning
Now you change your introduction depending on your audience.
For kindergarten:
"Let's learn with pictures!"
For college:
"Let's derive the equations."
For researchers:
"Let's analyze the assumptions."
Same teacher.
Different introduction.
That's Adaptive Prompt Tuning.
Comparison
Example
Suppose you're building a chatbot.
Prompt Tuning
Every query gets
[P1...P20]
[P1...P20] + "Write SQL"

[P1...P20] + "Explain diabetes"

[P1...P20] + "Translate to Spanish"
Adaptive Prompt Tuning
The system first identifies the type of query.
"Write SQL"

↓

Coding Prompt

↓

[PC1...PC20]
"Explain diabetes"

↓

Medical Prompt

↓

[PM1...PM20]
"Translate to Spanish"

↓

Translation Prompt

↓

[PT1...PT20]
The LLM receives a prompt that is better suited to that specific input.
Interview Answer
Adaptive Prompt Tuning extends Prompt Tuning by making the soft prompt dynamic rather than fixed. Instead of using the same learned prompt for every input, a small trainable adaptation mechanism generates or selects a prompt based on the input or task. The pretrained LLM remains frozen, and only the prompt generation or selection mechanism is trained. This allows the model to better handle diverse tasks and domains.
AutoGen — Core Idea
AutoGen is a framework for building applications where multiple AI agents can communicate and collaborate with each other.
Think:
User
  ↓
Agent A ─────→ Agent B
  ↑              ↓
  └──────────────┘
Instead of writing one huge prompt for an LLM, you create specialized agents and let them communicate.
1. What is an Agent?
An agent is essentially an LLM-powered entity that has:
instructions/system prompt
access to an LLM
possibly tools
memory/state
ability to communicate with other agents
For example:
ResearchAgent
    ↓
Researches information

CodingAgent
    ↓
Writes code

ReviewerAgent
    ↓
Reviews the code
They can work together.
2. Important AutoGen Agent Types
Your notes mention:
Assistant Agent
User Proxy Agent
Conversable Agent
These are concepts you'll encounter particularly in AutoGen's earlier/classic API.
The terminology has evolved across AutoGen versions, so don't memorize the names blindly.
ConversableAgent
This is the foundational concept.
A ConversableAgent is an agent capable of sending and receiving messages and participating in conversations.
Conceptually:
ConversableAgent
       │
       ├── sends messages
       ├── receives messages
       ├── maintains conversation history
       └── can generate replies
So your statement:
"Conversable agent → stores the previous"
is partially correct.
Better:
ConversableAgent manages a conversation and can maintain message history/context while communicating with other agents.
3. AssistantAgent
An AssistantAgent represents an AI assistant powered by an LLM.
For example:
AssistantAgent
      ↓
   LLM
      ↓
generates response
You might configure it with a system message such as:
You are an expert Python developer.
Write clean, production-quality Python code.
Then the agent can respond to tasks.
For example:
User:
Create a Flask API for user authentication.

AssistantAgent:
I'll design the API...

→ writes Python code
→ explains the implementation
Your definition:
"AssistantAgent → agents with elaborate system messages designed to assist an agent in coding-related tasks"
needs one correction.
It is not specifically for coding.
Better:
AssistantAgent is an LLM-powered agent configured to perform tasks according to its system instructions.
It can be a coding agent, research agent, analyst, etc.
4. UserProxyAgent
This one is interesting.
A UserProxyAgent acts as a proxy for the human user inside an agent conversation.
Conceptually:
             Human
               ↓
        UserProxyAgent
               ↓
      ┌────────┴────────┐
      ↓                 ↓
AssistantAgent     Other Agents
It can:
represent the user
send user requests
receive agent responses
execute code in some configurations
request human input when necessary
So your definition:
"Intermediates between the user and the rest of the system"
is basically correct.
A better interview definition:
UserProxyAgent acts on behalf of the human user and can facilitate interaction between the user and AI agents, including human input and code execution depending on configuration.
5. The Classic AutoGen Example
Imagine we want an AI to write Python code.
We create:
UserProxyAgent
        │
        │ "Create a Python program
        │  to calculate factorial"
        ↓
AssistantAgent
        │
        │ generates code
        ↓
UserProxyAgent
        │
        │ executes code
        ↓
AssistantAgent
        │
        │ checks result
        ↓
       DONE
This is where AutoGen becomes powerful.
The agents don't just generate independent responses.
They can communicate in a loop.
6. Agent Conversation
Suppose:
User:
Write a Python function to calculate factorial.
Step 1
UserProxy sends:
Write a Python function to calculate factorial.
to AssistantAgent.
Step 2
AssistantAgent responds:
deffactorial(n):ifn==0:return1returnn*factorial(n-1)
Step 3
The proxy can execute the code.
Maybe it discovers:
factorial(-1)
causes a problem.
Step 4
AssistantAgent receives the execution result.
It can modify the code.
deffactorial(n):ifn<0:raiseValueError("n must be non-negative")
    ...
This is the basic agentic loop.
7. Why AutoGen?
The important idea is:
Traditional LLM application
User
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
Response
AutoGen
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Planner   │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Coder     │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Reviewer  │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Executor  │
                  └─────────────┘
Each agent has a role.
8. Multi-Agent System
This is the main concept you should remember.
Suppose you're building a research application.
You could have:
User
 ↓
Manager Agent
 ↓
 ├── Web Research Agent
 │
 ├── RAG Agent
 │
 ├── Analysis Agent
 │
 └── Writer Agent
          ↓
       Final answer
The agents communicate.
For example:
Manager:
Research Apple's latest AI developments.

ResearchAgent:
I found these 5 developments...

Manager:
AnalysisAgent, analyze these.

AnalysisAgent:
The most significant development is...

Manager:
WriterAgent, create the final response.

WriterAgent:
...
This is the core philosophy of AutoGen.
1. Chinchilla Scaling Laws
Main idea:
Chinchilla scaling laws help determine the right balance between:
Model size → number of parameters
Training data → number of tokens
Compute → GPU/TPU resources used for training
Bigger model ≠ automatically better model.
A model needs an appropriate amount of training data relative to its parameter count.
Remember:
Model size ↔ Data size ↔ Compute
2. Parallel & Distributed Training
When an LLM becomes too large for a single GPU, we use multiple GPUs.
Distributed training
Instead of:
1 GPU → entire training workload
we do:
Multiple GPUs → share the workload
There are different ways to divide the workload.
3. Data Parallelism (DP)
Idea
Each GPU has a copy of the entire model, but processes different batches of data.
              Same Model
                  ↓
       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
       ↓          ↓          ↓
     GPU 1      GPU 2      GPU 3
   Data 1      Data 2      Data 3
After processing their batches, GPUs synchronize gradients.
In simple words
Same model + different data
Analogy
4 students have the same textbook but solve different questions.
4. Model Parallelism (MP)
Idea
The model itself is split across multiple GPUs.
        Large LLM
           ↓
    ┌──────┴──────┐
    ↓             ↓
  GPU 1         GPU 2
Layers 1–20    Layers 21–40
Each GPU stores and processes part of the model.
In simple words
Split the model across GPUs
Why?
When:
Model > GPU memory
5. Pipeline Parallelism (PP)
Pipeline parallelism is a form of model parallelism.
The model is divided into groups of layers/stages.
Input
  ↓
GPU 1 → Layers 1–10
  ↓
GPU 2 → Layers 11–20
  ↓
GPU 3 → Layers 21–30
  ↓
GPU 4 → Layers 31–40
  ↓
Output
Data moves through the GPUs like an assembly line.
Key idea
Different GPUs handle different stages/layers of the model.
Analogy
A factory assembly line:
Worker 1 → Build
Worker 2 → Add parts
Worker 3 → Test
Worker 4 → Package
6. Fully Sharded Data Parallelism (FSDP)
Normal Data Parallelism:
GPU 1 → Full model
GPU 2 → Full model
GPU 3 → Full model
GPU 4 → Full model
This creates redundancy because every GPU stores the same model/training states.
FSDP
Instead, training states are sharded (split) across GPUs.
        Model / Training States
                 ↓
       ┌─────────┼─────────┐
       ↓         ↓         ↓
     GPU 1     GPU 2     GPU 3
      1/3        1/3       1/3
Why?
To reduce GPU memory usage.
Shard = split something into pieces
7. ZeRO
ZeRO = Zero Redundancy Optimizer
The idea is similar to FSDP:
Avoid storing unnecessary duplicate training information on every GPU.
ZeRO progressively shards:
Optimizer states
Gradients
Model parameters
Main goal
Reduce memory redundancy during distributed training.
8. Tensor Parallelism (TP)
Idea
Instead of splitting the model by layers, we split the large mathematical operations inside a layer across GPUs.
For example, a huge weight matrix:
       HUGE Weight Matrix
              ↓
    ┌─────────┼─────────┐
    ↓         ↓         ↓
  GPU 1     GPU 2     GPU 3
   1/3        1/3       1/3
Each GPU performs part of the computation.
The results are then combined.
Key idea
Split the computation/tensors of a layer across GPUs.
9. Pipeline vs Tensor Parallelism
This distinction is important.
Pipeline Parallelism
Splits layers:
GPU 1 → Layers 1–10
GPU 2 → Layers 11–20
GPU 3 → Layers 21–30
Tensor Parallelism
Splits the computation inside a layer:
          Layer
           ↓
     Large Matrix
      ↙    ↓    ↘
   GPU 1 GPU 2 GPU 3
Easy memory trick:
Pipeline → Layers
Tensor → Math
10. 2D & 3D Parallelism
For extremely large LLMs, we can combine different parallelism strategies.
2D Parallelism
Usually combines two dimensions, such as:
Data Parallelism + Tensor Parallelism
3D Parallelism
Can combine:
Data + Tensor + Pipeline Parallelism
Example:
                 64 GPUs
                    ↓
        ┌───────────┼───────────┐
        ↓           ↓           ↓
      Data        Tensor      Pipeline
    Parallel     Parallel     Parallel
This allows very large models to be trained across huge GPU clusters.
Building an LLM From Scratch — Complete Pipeline
Collect Data
↓
Process / Clean Data
↓
Tokenization
↓
Model Architecture
↓
Train the Model
↓
Evaluate the Model
1. 📚 Collecting Training Data
The first step is to collect a large and diverse dataset.
Common sources
Falcon datasets
Large-scale web/text datasets used for training LLMs.
The Pile
A large, diverse open dataset containing books, papers, websites, code, etc.
The Stack
Large dataset of source code.
Useful for training code-capable LLMs.
Common Crawl
Massive collection of web pages crawled from the internet.
Frequently used as a source for building large language-model datasets.
Goal
Collect large amounts of high-quality text that the model can learn from.
2. 🧹 Data Processing
Raw internet data contains:
Duplicate content
Low-quality text
Spam
HTML
Incorrect formatting
Unwanted content
So we need to clean it before training.
Remove Duplicates
Duplicate documents waste training compute and can cause the model to overfit/repeat information.
Jaccard Similarity
Jaccard similarity measures how similar two sets are.
For two sets A and B:
Jaccard Similarity =
|A ∩ B|
────────
|A ∪ B|
Example:
Document A → {AI, ML, Python, LLM}

Document B → {AI, ML, Python, Java}
They have 3 elements in common.
Jaccard similarity:
3 / 5 = 0.6
Higher value → more similar.
Problem
Comparing every document against every other document is extremely expensive for huge datasets.
That's where MinHash comes in.
3. ⚡ MinHash
MinHash is an efficient technique for estimating Jaccard similarity.
Instead of comparing huge documents directly:
Document A ↔ Document B
Document A ↔ Document C
Document A ↔ Document D
...
MinHash creates a compact signature for each document.
Then we can efficiently find documents that are likely to be duplicates or near-duplicates.
Simple idea
Huge Document
      ↓
   MinHash
      ↓
Small signature
      ↓
Compare signatures
      ↓
Find similar documents
Remember
Jaccard = similarity measure
MinHash = efficient way to estimate that similarity
4. 🔤 Tokenization
LLMs don't directly understand raw text.
For example:
"I love Bangalore"
needs to become something like:
[1542, 891, 7234]
These numbers are called token IDs.
Two steps
Step 1 — Train the tokenizer
The tokenizer learns how text should be divided into tokens.
Step 2 — Apply tokenizer to training data
Once trained:
Raw training data
       ↓
    Tokenizer
       ↓
Token IDs
       ↓
Model
5. Types of Tokenization
A. Word-Level Tokenization
Each word becomes a token.
"I love AI"

→ ["I", "love", "AI"]
Problem
Vocabulary becomes huge.
Also, unknown/new words become a problem.
B. Character-Level Tokenization
Each character becomes a token.
"hello"

→ ["h", "e", "l", "l", "o"]
Advantage
Very small vocabulary.
Problem
Sequences become very long.
C. Subword Tokenization ⭐
This is the most commonly used approach in modern LLMs.
Words can be broken into meaningful pieces.
Example:
"unhappiness"

→ ["un", "happi", "ness"]
Common words may remain as a single token, while uncommon words can be broken into smaller pieces.
Why is it useful?
It provides a good balance between:
Word-level ↔ Character-level
6. 🔤 Byte Pair Encoding (BPE)
BPE = Byte Pair Encoding
BPE is one popular algorithm for creating a subword tokenizer.
Basic idea
Start with small units and repeatedly merge frequently occurring pairs.
Example:
l o w
l o w e r
If certain character/subword pairs occur frequently, they can be merged:
l + o → lo
lo + w → low
Eventually:
"lower"

→ ["low", "er"]
The exact tokens depend on the tokenizer's training data and vocabulary.
Key idea
BPE learns which pieces of text occur frequently together and merges them into tokens.
7. 🧠 Model Architecture
Now we have:
Raw Data
   ↓
Clean Data
   ↓
Tokens
   ↓
Model
The next question is:
What kind of neural network architecture should we use?
For modern LLMs, the Transformer architecture is the foundation.
8. Encoder vs Decoder
Transformers originally introduced two major components:
Transformer
├── Encoder
└── Decoder
Encoder
Good for understanding/representing input.
Examples:
BERT-style models
Classification
Embeddings
Decoder
Designed to generate text one token at a time.
Examples:
GPT-style models
Many modern generative LLMs
For a GPT-style LLM
You generally use:
Decoder-only Transformer
Input tokens
     ↓
Transformer blocks
     ↓
Next-token prediction
9. 🛠️ Modify Model Architecture
You don't necessarily have to implement the original Transformer exactly.
You can use ideas from existing architectures.
Examples from your notes:
Mistral
A modern decoder-only LLM architecture with several efficiency/design improvements.
MPT
Another decoder-style LLM architecture designed for efficient training and deployment.
Important idea
When building your own LLM, you can experiment with:
Number of layers
Hidden dimension
Attention mechanism
Number of attention heads
Context length
Activation functions
Positional encoding
Normalization
Vocabulary size
10. 🏋️ Train the Model
The basic training objective for a GPT-style model is:
Predict the next token.
Example:
"The cat is"

→ "sleeping"
The model predicts probabilities:
sleeping → 0.60
running  → 0.15
eating   → 0.10
...
The prediction is compared with the actual next token.
Then:
Loss
 ↓
Backpropagation
 ↓
Gradients
 ↓
Update weights
 ↓
Repeat
This is where the parameters (weights + biases) you were learning about earlier get updated.
11. 📊 Model Evaluation
After training, we need to answer:
"How good is my LLM?"
We use benchmarks.
General Knowledge / Reasoning
HellaSwag
Tests whether the model can select the most plausible continuation of a scenario.
Useful for evaluating:
Commonsense reasoning
BIG-Bench Hard (BBH)
BIG-Bench Hard
A collection of challenging tasks from BIG-Bench.
Tests things like:
Reasoning
Language understanding
Multi-step thinking
Pattern recognition
12. 🎯 Factuality
TruthfulQA
Tests whether the model gives truthful answers instead of producing common misconceptions or false information.
Main question:
Does the model tell the truth?
13. ➗ Mathematical Reasoning
GSM8K
A benchmark containing grade-school-level math word problems.
Tests:
Multi-step mathematical reasoning.
MATH
A more challenging mathematical reasoning benchmark.
Contains problems across areas such as:
Algebra
Geometry
Number theory
Probability
etc.
Difference
GSM8K → Grade-school math
MATH  → More advanced mathematical problems
14. 💻 Code Benchmark
HumanEval
HumanEval evaluates a model's ability to generate code from a natural-language description.
Example:
Prompt:
Write a Python function that returns the
factorial of a number.

        ↓

LLM

        ↓

def factorial(n):
    ...
The generated code is then tested against predefined test cases.
Main purpose
Evaluate code generation ability.
15. 🧪 How Do We Evaluate an LLM?
There are different ways of prompting the model during evaluation.
Zero-shot
Give the model the task without examples.
Question:
What is 2 + 2?

Answer:
No examples provided.
Zero examples → Zero-shot
Few-shot
Give the model a few examples first.
2 + 2 = 4
3 + 3 = 6

5 + 5 = ?
The model learns the expected pattern from the examples.
Few examples → Few-shot
Chain-of-Thought (CoT)
Ask the model to reason through the problem step by step.
Example:
Question:
If I have 3 apples and buy 2 more,
how many apples do I have?

Reason step by step.
The model produces intermediate reasoning before the final answer.
CoT → Encourage multi-step reasoning

                          │           │
                      Factuality    Math

                 Evaluation methods:
                 • Zero-shot
                 • Few-shot
                 • Chain-of-Thought
LLM as a judge 
Human Annotation
Code-based Evals
1. LLM-as-a-Judge
Use another LLM to evaluate the agent's output.
Give the input + agent response + evaluation criteria to a judge LLM.
Judge scores things like:
Correctness
Relevance
Helpfulness
Reasoning quality
Tool usage
Can use a 1–5 score, pass/fail, or pairwise comparison.
Example:
Agent answers: “The capital of Australia is Sydney.”
Judge LLM checks → Incorrect → Expected answer: Canberra.
Pros: Scalable, fast, good for subjective qualities
Cons: Judge can be biased or make mistakes; needs a good evaluation rubric.
2. Human Annotation
A human reviews the agent's output and labels/scores it.
For example:
Humans can evaluate things that are difficult to capture with code, such as tone, usefulness, safety, and overall quality.
Pros: High-quality ground truth
Cons: Expensive, slow, and potentially subjective.
3. Code-Based Evals
Use deterministic code/rules to check whether the agent did what it was supposed to do.
For example:
assertresponse.status_code==200assert"Canberra"inresponse.textasserttool_called=="weather_api"assertlatency<2
For an agent, you can evaluate:
Did it call the correct tool?
Did it call the tool the correct number of times?
Were the parameters correct?
Is the output in the required format?
Did it return the expected value?
Did it violate any constraints?
Latency / token usage / cost
Pros: Fast, cheap, objective, highly reproducible
Cons: Only works well when you can define deterministic criteria.
The key difference
Code-based → “Did it follow this exact rule?”
LLM judge → “How good/correct was this response?”
Human annotation → “Would a real person consider this good?”
Agent evaluation
Router →Determines which skill to call the function
Skill evaluation → Did we call the right function with the right parameter is passed
Memory
Path planning and reflection evals 
Planning → evaluate the plan generated by the agent before action → LLM as a judge
Reflection → decide whether to repeat the previous steps → LLM as a judge, code-based
Trajectory → Determine whether the optimal path was taken by the agent overall


=== PAGE: Training LLM from Scratch ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Training-LLM-from-Scratch-3d2fca3384fc808da62dffc553144ab8

1. Chinchilla Scaling Laws
Main idea:
Chinchilla scaling laws help determine the right balance between:
Model size → number of parameters
Training data → number of tokens
Compute → GPU/TPU resources used for training
Bigger model ≠ automatically better model.
A model needs an appropriate amount of training data relative to its parameter count.
Remember:
Model size ↔ Data size ↔ Compute
2. Parallel & Distributed Training
When an LLM becomes too large for a single GPU, we use multiple GPUs.
Distributed training
Instead of:
1 GPU → entire training workload
we do:
Multiple GPUs → share the workload
There are different ways to divide the workload.
3. Data Parallelism (DP)
Idea
Each GPU has a copy of the entire model, but processes different batches of data.
              Same Model
                  ↓
       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
       ↓          ↓          ↓
     GPU 1      GPU 2      GPU 3
   Data 1      Data 2      Data 3
After processing their batches, GPUs synchronize gradients.
In simple words
Same model + different data
Analogy
4 students have the same textbook but solve different questions.
4. Model Parallelism (MP)
Idea
The model itself is split across multiple GPUs.
        Large LLM
           ↓
    ┌──────┴──────┐
    ↓             ↓
  GPU 1         GPU 2
Layers 1–20    Layers 21–40
Each GPU stores and processes part of the model.
In simple words
Split the model across GPUs
Why?
When:
Model > GPU memory
5. Pipeline Parallelism (PP)
Pipeline parallelism is a form of model parallelism.
The model is divided into groups of layers/stages.
Input
  ↓
GPU 1 → Layers 1–10
  ↓
GPU 2 → Layers 11–20
  ↓
GPU 3 → Layers 21–30
  ↓
GPU 4 → Layers 31–40
  ↓
Output
Data moves through the GPUs like an assembly line.
Key idea
Different GPUs handle different stages/layers of the model.
Analogy
A factory assembly line:
Worker 1 → Build
Worker 2 → Add parts
Worker 3 → Test
Worker 4 → Package
6. Fully Sharded Data Parallelism (FSDP)
Normal Data Parallelism:
GPU 1 → Full model
GPU 2 → Full model
GPU 3 → Full model
GPU 4 → Full model
This creates redundancy because every GPU stores the same model/training states.
FSDP
Instead, training states are sharded (split) across GPUs.
        Model / Training States
                 ↓
       ┌─────────┼─────────┐
       ↓         ↓         ↓
     GPU 1     GPU 2     GPU 3
      1/3        1/3       1/3
Why?
To reduce GPU memory usage.
Shard = split something into pieces
7. ZeRO
ZeRO = Zero Redundancy Optimizer
The idea is similar to FSDP:
Avoid storing unnecessary duplicate training information on every GPU.
ZeRO progressively shards:
Optimizer states
Gradients
Model parameters
Main goal
Reduce memory redundancy during distributed training.
8. Tensor Parallelism (TP)
Idea
Instead of splitting the model by layers, we split the large mathematical operations inside a layer across GPUs.
For example, a huge weight matrix:
       HUGE Weight Matrix
              ↓
    ┌─────────┼─────────┐
    ↓         ↓         ↓
  GPU 1     GPU 2     GPU 3
   1/3        1/3       1/3
Each GPU performs part of the computation.
The results are then combined.
Key idea
Split the computation/tensors of a layer across GPUs.
9. Pipeline vs Tensor Parallelism
This distinction is important.
Pipeline Parallelism
Splits layers:
GPU 1 → Layers 1–10
GPU 2 → Layers 11–20
GPU 3 → Layers 21–30
Tensor Parallelism
Splits the computation inside a layer:
          Layer
           ↓
     Large Matrix
      ↙    ↓    ↘
   GPU 1 GPU 2 GPU 3
Easy memory trick:
Pipeline → Layers
Tensor → Math
10. 2D & 3D Parallelism
For extremely large LLMs, we can combine different parallelism strategies.
2D Parallelism
Usually combines two dimensions, such as:
Data Parallelism + Tensor Parallelism
3D Parallelism
Can combine:
Data + Tensor + Pipeline Parallelism
Example:
                 64 GPUs
                    ↓
        ┌───────────┼───────────┐
        ↓           ↓           ↓
      Data        Tensor      Pipeline
    Parallel     Parallel     Parallel
This allows very large models to be trained across huge GPU clusters.
Building an LLM From Scratch — Complete Pipeline
Collect Data
↓
Process / Clean Data
↓
Tokenization
↓
Model Architecture
↓
Train the Model
↓
Evaluate the Model
1. 📚 Collecting Training Data
The first step is to collect a large and diverse dataset.
Common sources
Falcon datasets
Large-scale web/text datasets used for training LLMs.
The Pile
A large, diverse open dataset containing books, papers, websites, code, etc.
The Stack
Large dataset of source code.
Useful for training code-capable LLMs.
Common Crawl
Massive collection of web pages crawled from the internet.
Frequently used as a source for building large language-model datasets.
Goal
Collect large amounts of high-quality text that the model can learn from.
2. 🧹 Data Processing
Raw internet data contains:
Duplicate content
Low-quality text
Spam
HTML
Incorrect formatting
Unwanted content
So we need to clean it before training.
Remove Duplicates
Duplicate documents waste training compute and can cause the model to overfit/repeat information.
Jaccard Similarity
Jaccard similarity measures how similar two sets are.
For two sets A and B:
Jaccard Similarity =
|A ∩ B|
────────
|A ∪ B|
Example:
Document A → {AI, ML, Python, LLM}

Document B → {AI, ML, Python, Java}
They have 3 elements in common.
Jaccard similarity:
3 / 5 = 0.6
Higher value → more similar.
Problem
Comparing every document against every other document is extremely expensive for huge datasets.
That's where MinHash comes in.
3. ⚡ MinHash
MinHash is an efficient technique for estimating Jaccard similarity.
Instead of comparing huge documents directly:
Document A ↔ Document B
Document A ↔ Document C
Document A ↔ Document D
...
MinHash creates a compact signature for each document.
Then we can efficiently find documents that are likely to be duplicates or near-duplicates.
Simple idea
Huge Document
      ↓
   MinHash
      ↓
Small signature
      ↓
Compare signatures
      ↓
Find similar documents
Remember
Jaccard = similarity measure
MinHash = efficient way to estimate that similarity
4. 🔤 Tokenization
LLMs don't directly understand raw text.
For example:
"I love Bangalore"
needs to become something like:
[1542, 891, 7234]
These numbers are called token IDs.
Two steps
Step 1 — Train the tokenizer
The tokenizer learns how text should be divided into tokens.
Step 2 — Apply tokenizer to training data
Once trained:
Raw training data
       ↓
    Tokenizer
       ↓
Token IDs
       ↓
Model
5. Types of Tokenization
A. Word-Level Tokenization
Each word becomes a token.
"I love AI"

→ ["I", "love", "AI"]
Problem
Vocabulary becomes huge.
Also, unknown/new words become a problem.
B. Character-Level Tokenization
Each character becomes a token.
"hello"

→ ["h", "e", "l", "l", "o"]
Advantage
Very small vocabulary.
Problem
Sequences become very long.
C. Subword Tokenization ⭐
This is the most commonly used approach in modern LLMs.
Words can be broken into meaningful pieces.
Example:
"unhappiness"

→ ["un", "happi", "ness"]
Common words may remain as a single token, while uncommon words can be broken into smaller pieces.
Why is it useful?
It provides a good balance between:
Word-level ↔ Character-level
6. 🔤 Byte Pair Encoding (BPE)
BPE = Byte Pair Encoding
BPE is one popular algorithm for creating a subword tokenizer.
Basic idea
Start with small units and repeatedly merge frequently occurring pairs.
Example:
l o w
l o w e r
If certain character/subword pairs occur frequently, they can be merged:
l + o → lo
lo + w → low
Eventually:
"lower"

→ ["low", "er"]
The exact tokens depend on the tokenizer's training data and vocabulary.
Key idea
BPE learns which pieces of text occur frequently together and merges them into tokens.
7. 🧠 Model Architecture
Now we have:
Raw Data
   ↓
Clean Data
   ↓
Tokens
   ↓
Model
The next question is:
What kind of neural network architecture should we use?
For modern LLMs, the Transformer architecture is the foundation.
8. Encoder vs Decoder
Transformers originally introduced two major components:
Transformer
├── Encoder
└── Decoder
Encoder
Good for understanding/representing input.
Examples:
BERT-style models
Classification
Embeddings
Decoder
Designed to generate text one token at a time.
Examples:
GPT-style models
Many modern generative LLMs
For a GPT-style LLM
You generally use:
Decoder-only Transformer
Input tokens
     ↓
Transformer blocks
     ↓
Next-token prediction
9. 🛠️ Modify Model Architecture
You don't necessarily have to implement the original Transformer exactly.
You can use ideas from existing architectures.
Examples from your notes:
Mistral
A modern decoder-only LLM architecture with several efficiency/design improvements.
MPT
Another decoder-style LLM architecture designed for efficient training and deployment.
Important idea
When building your own LLM, you can experiment with:
Number of layers
Hidden dimension
Attention mechanism
Number of attention heads
Context length
Activation functions
Positional encoding
Normalization
Vocabulary size
10. 🏋️ Train the Model
The basic training objective for a GPT-style model is:
Predict the next token.
Example:
"The cat is"

→ "sleeping"
The model predicts probabilities:
sleeping → 0.60
running  → 0.15
eating   → 0.10
...
The prediction is compared with the actual next token.
Then:
Loss
 ↓
Backpropagation
 ↓
Gradients
 ↓
Update weights
 ↓
Repeat
This is where the parameters (weights + biases) you were learning about earlier get updated.
11. 📊 Model Evaluation
After training, we need to answer:
"How good is my LLM?"
We use benchmarks.

HellaSwag
Tests whether the model can select the most plausible continuation of a scenario.
Useful for evaluating:

BIG-Bench Hard
A collection of challenging tasks from BIG-Bench.
Tests things like:
Reasoning

Pattern recognition

Tests whether the model gives truthful answers instead of producing common misconceptions or false information.
Main question:
Does the model tell the truth?
13. ➗ Mathematical Reasoning

A benchmark containing grade-school-level math word problems.
Tests:

A more challenging mathematical reasoning benchmark.
Contains problems across areas such as:

Probability
etc.

MATH  → More advanced mathematical problems

HumanEval

Example:

Write a Python function that returns the
factorial of a number.

        ↓

        ↓

def factorial(n):
    ...

Main purpose
Evaluate code generation ability.

Answer:
No examples provided.
Zero examples → Zero-shot

Ask the model to reason through the problem step by step.

=== PAGE: AutoGen ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/AutoGen-3b6fca3384fc80dba710da50ab997c7f

AutoGen — Core Idea
AutoGen is a framework for building applications where multiple AI agents can communicate and collaborate with each other.
Think:
User
  ↓
Agent A ─────→ Agent B
  ↑              ↓
  └──────────────┘
Instead of writing one huge prompt for an LLM, you create specialized agents and let them communicate.
1. What is an Agent?
An agent is essentially an LLM-powered entity that has:
instructions/system prompt
access to an LLM
possibly tools
memory/state
ability to communicate with other agents
For example:
ResearchAgent
    ↓
Researches information

CodingAgent
    ↓
Writes code

ReviewerAgent
    ↓
Reviews the code
They can work together.
2. Important AutoGen Agent Types
Your notes mention:
Assistant Agent
User Proxy Agent
Conversable Agent
These are concepts you'll encounter particularly in AutoGen's earlier/classic API.
The terminology has evolved across AutoGen versions, so don't memorize the names blindly.
ConversableAgent
This is the foundational concept.
A ConversableAgent is an agent capable of sending and receiving messages and participating in conversations.
Conceptually:
ConversableAgent
       │
       ├── sends messages
       ├── receives messages
       ├── maintains conversation history
       └── can generate replies
So your statement:
"Conversable agent → stores the previous"
is partially correct.
Better:
ConversableAgent manages a conversation and can maintain message history/context while communicating with other agents.
3. AssistantAgent
An AssistantAgent represents an AI assistant powered by an LLM.
For example:
AssistantAgent
      ↓
   LLM
      ↓
generates response
You might configure it with a system message such as:
You are an expert Python developer.
Write clean, production-quality Python code.
Then the agent can respond to tasks.
For example:
User:
Create a Flask API for user authentication.

AssistantAgent:
I'll design the API...

→ writes Python code
→ explains the implementation
Your definition:
"AssistantAgent → agents with elaborate system messages designed to assist an agent in coding-related tasks"
needs one correction.
It is not specifically for coding.
Better:
AssistantAgent is an LLM-powered agent configured to perform tasks according to its system instructions.
It can be a coding agent, research agent, analyst, etc.
4. UserProxyAgent
This one is interesting.
A UserProxyAgent acts as a proxy for the human user inside an agent conversation.
Conceptually:
             Human
               ↓
        UserProxyAgent
               ↓
      ┌────────┴────────┐
      ↓                 ↓
AssistantAgent     Other Agents
It can:
represent the user
send user requests
receive agent responses
execute code in some configurations
request human input when necessary
So your definition:
"Intermediates between the user and the rest of the system"
is basically correct.
A better interview definition:
UserProxyAgent acts on behalf of the human user and can facilitate interaction between the user and AI agents, including human input and code execution depending on configuration.
5. The Classic AutoGen Example
Imagine we want an AI to write Python code.
We create:
UserProxyAgent
        │
        │ "Create a Python program
        │  to calculate factorial"
        ↓
AssistantAgent
        │
        │ generates code
        ↓
UserProxyAgent
        │
        │ executes code
        ↓
AssistantAgent
        │
        │ checks result
        ↓
       DONE
This is where AutoGen becomes powerful.
The agents don't just generate independent responses.
They can communicate in a loop.
6. Agent Conversation
Suppose:
User:
Write a Python function to calculate factorial.
Step 1
UserProxy sends:
Write a Python function to calculate factorial.
to AssistantAgent.
Step 2
AssistantAgent responds:
deffactorial(n):ifn==0:return1returnn*factorial(n-1)
Step 3
The proxy can execute the code.
Maybe it discovers:
factorial(-1)
causes a problem.
Step 4
AssistantAgent receives the execution result.
It can modify the code.
deffactorial(n):ifn<0:raiseValueError("n must be non-negative")
    ...
This is the basic agentic loop.
7. Why AutoGen?
The important idea is:
Traditional LLM application
User
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
Response
AutoGen
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Planner   │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Coder     │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Reviewer  │
                  └──────┬──────┘
                         ↓
                  ┌─────────────┐
                  │   Executor  │
                  └─────────────┘
Each agent has a role.
8. Multi-Agent System
This is the main concept you should remember.
Suppose you're building a research application.
You could have:
User
 ↓
Manager Agent
 ↓
 ├── Web Research Agent
 │
 ├── RAG Agent
 │
 ├── Analysis Agent
 │
 └── Writer Agent
          ↓
       Final answer
The agents communicate.
For example:
Manager:
Research Apple's latest AI developments.

ResearchAgent:
I found these 5 developments...

Manager:
AnalysisAgent, analyze these.

AnalysisAgent:
The most significant development is...

Manager:
WriterAgent, create the final response.

WriterAgent:
...
This is the core philosophy of AutoGen.

=== PAGE: Fine-Tuning LLM ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Fine-Tuning-LLM-3b2fca3384fc80d68dccec22d752d14d

LLM fine-tuning is the process of taking a pre-trained language model and training it further on a smaller, task-specific dataset so that it performs better for a particular domain or task.
A base LLM is trained on general internet data.
It knows a lot about many topics, but it doesn't know:
your company's policies
your writing style
medical terminology
legal documents
how you want responses formatted
Fine-tuning teaches the model these things
Types of Fine-Tuning
1. Full Fine-Tuning
The entire model is updated.
All weights
↓

Updated
Example:
70 billion parameters
All 70B parameters are trained.
Pros
Highest accuracy
Learns deeply
Best customization
Cons
Extremely expensive
Needs many GPUs
Large storage
Slow
2. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT)
Instead of updating every parameter...
Only a small number of parameters are updated.
Popular methods:
LoRA
QLoRA
Adapters
Prefix Tuning
Most companies use PEFT.
LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation)
This is the most common interview topic.
Instead of changing the original weights...
LoRA adds small trainable matrices.
Original weights stay frozen.
Original Model (Frozen)

      +
Small LoRA Layers

↓

Combined Output
Imagine a 7B model.
Without LoRA:
Train all 7 billion parameters.
With LoRA:
Train maybe only 10–50 million parameters.
Huge savings.
QLoRA
QLoRA combines:
Quantization (compressing model weights, e.g., to 4-bit)
LoRA adapters
Benefits:
Lower memory usage
Faster training
Can fine-tune large models on a single high-memory GPU
Fine-Tuning Process
Collect Dataset

↓

Clean Dataset

↓

Create Prompt-Response pairs

↓

Tokenize

↓

Train

↓

Evaluate

↓

Deploy
Pre-training teaches general language and knowledge.
Fine-tuning specializes a model for a domain, task, or behavior by updating model parameters.
RAG adds up-to-date external knowledge without changing the model.
LoRA/QLoRA are efficient fine-tuning methods widely used in production because they require much less compute than full fine-tuning.
LLM Fine-Tuning Process (Interview Notes)
Step 1: Dataset Collection
The first step in fine-tuning is collecting a high-quality dataset relevant to the target task.
The dataset typically consists of input-output (prompt-response) pairs.
Examples
Customer Support
Prompt: How do I reset my password?
Response: Click "Forgot Password" and follow the instructions sent to your email.
SQL Generation
Prompt: Show all employees with salary > 50000.
Response: SELECT * FROM employees WHERE salary > 50000;
Medical Assistant
Prompt: What are the symptoms of diabetes?
Response: Common symptoms include increased thirst, frequent urination...
Good Dataset Characteristics
High-quality and accurate
Domain-specific
Diverse examples
Properly labeled
Free from duplicates and noise
Rule: A model is only as good as the data it is trained on.
Step 2: Training
During training, the model learns by comparing its predictions with the expected answers and gradually updating its weights.
Training Pipeline
Dataset
    ↓
Forward Pass
    ↓
Prediction
    ↓
Loss Computation
    ↓
Backward Propagation
    ↓
Weight Update
    ↓
Repeat
What Happens in Each Step?
1. Forward Pass
The model receives the input and predicts an output.
Example:
Input:
"What is Kubernetes?"

Prediction:
"Kubernetes is a container."
2. Loss Computation
The prediction is compared with the correct answer.
Example:
Expected:
"Kubernetes is a container orchestration platform."

Predicted:
"Kubernetes is a container."
A loss function measures how wrong the prediction is.
High loss → Poor prediction
Low loss → Good prediction
The objective is to minimize the loss over time.
3. Backpropagation
The model calculates how much each parameter (weight) contributed to the error.
Using gradients, it determines:
"Which weights should change, and by how much?"
4. Weight Update
An optimizer (e.g., AdamW) updates the model's weights.
The updated weights help the model make better predictions in the next iteration.
This cycle repeats for thousands of batches across multiple epochs until the model converges.
How Do We Train?
There are multiple ways to fine-tune an LLM.
1. PyTorch Training Loop
You manually implement the training process.
Typical flow:
for batch in dataloader:
	outputs=model(batch)
	loss=compute_loss(outputs)
	loss.backward()
	optimizer.step()
Pros
Maximum flexibility
Full control over training
Cons
More code
Easier to make mistakes
2. Hugging Face Transformers Trainer API
The Trainer API abstracts away most of the training loop.
You mainly configure:
Model
Dataset
Training arguments
Evaluation strategy
The library handles:
Training loop
Optimizer
Checkpointing
Logging
Evaluation
Pros
Very easy to use
Less boilerplate
Industry standard
3. Hugging Face Accelerate
Accelerate simplifies training and inference across different hardware setups.
It enables:
Multi-GPU training
TPU support
Mixed precision (FP16/BF16)
Distributed training
without requiring major code changes.
Advantages
Faster training
Better GPU utilization
Scales from one GPU to many GPUs
Step 3: Evaluation
After training, the model must be evaluated to determine how well it performs.
Evaluation measures whether the model has learned the desired behavior and generalizes to unseen data.
There are two main approaches:
1. Automatic Evaluation (Benchmarks)
The model is tested on standardized datasets using predefined metrics.
Examples include:
MMLU
GSM8K
HumanEval
TruthfulQA
HellaSwag
These benchmarks provide quantitative scores that make it easier to compare different models.
Advantages
Fast
Reproducible
Easy to compare models
Limitation
LLM benchmarks are a very noisy proxy. They are useful for measuring generic capabilities but should not be treated as the final indicator of model quality.
A high benchmark score does not always translate to better real-world performance.
2. Human Evaluation
Humans manually review model outputs and judge their quality.
Typical evaluation criteria include:
Correctness
Helpfulness
Accuracy
Safety
Fluency
Relevance
Coherence
Human evaluation remains the gold standard because it captures qualities that automatic metrics often miss.
Interview Tip: Human evaluation is considered the most reliable way to assess an LLM's real-world usefulness.
Why Can Model Evaluations Be Misleading?
A common issue is benchmark contamination or evaluation bias.
Sometimes, models are trained on data that overlaps with the benchmark datasets.
As a result, the model may appear to perform exceptionally well simply because it has already seen similar examples during training.
This leads to inflated benchmark scores that do not necessarily reflect true reasoning ability.
Key Point
Model evaluations can be skewed toward models trained on data that overlaps with the evaluation dataset.
This is why benchmark results should always be interpreted carefully.
Summary
Dataset Collection
      ↓
Training
    • Forward Pass
    • Loss Computation
    • Backpropagation
    • Weight Update
      ↓
Evaluation
    • Automatic Benchmarks (fast but imperfect)
    • Human Evaluation (most reliable)
Interview One-Liners
What are the steps involved in fine-tuning an LLM?
Collect a high-quality dataset, train the model using forward pass, loss computation, backpropagation, and weight updates, then evaluate it using both automatic benchmarks and human evaluation.
Why is human evaluation important?
Human evaluation assesses qualities like correctness, helpfulness, safety, and fluency that automatic metrics often fail to capture, making it the most reliable measure of real-world model performance.
Why shouldn't we rely only on benchmarks?
Benchmarks are useful for comparing general capabilities, but they can be noisy or biased due to benchmark contamination. A high benchmark score does not always guarantee better real-world performance.
Prompt Tuning (Soft Prompt Tuning)
Definition
Prompt Tuning is a Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) technique where the LLM is completely frozen, and only a small set of learnable prompt embeddings (soft prompts) are trained.
Unlike normal prompts written in English, these prompts are continuous embedding vectors that the model learns automatically.
Training Process
Suppose we have a dataset of 1000 examples.
Example:
Input: I love this movie.
Output: Positive

Input: This movie is terrible.
Output: Negative
Step 1: Freeze the LLM
Freeze all model parameters.
Transformer Weights ❌ Frozen
Attention Weights ❌ Frozen
Embedding Layer ❌ Frozen
Feed Forward Layers ❌ Frozen
No weights inside the LLM will be updated.
Step 2: Initialize Learnable Prompt Embeddings
Create a small matrix of trainable vectors.
Example:
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]
Each prompt token is an embedding vector.
If
Prompt Length = 20
Embedding Dimension = 768
then
Soft Prompt Matrix

Shape = (20 × 768)
Initially, these vectors are randomly initialized.
Step 3: Concatenate Prompt with Input
For every training example
Instead of
I love this movie.
the model receives
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]

I love this movie.
These prompt vectors are added before the input embeddings.
Step 4: Forward Pass
The combined embeddings are passed through the frozen LLM.
Soft Prompt + Input

↓

Frozen Transformer

↓

Prediction
Step 5: Compute Loss
Compare the prediction with the correct label.
Example
Prediction : Negative

Actual : Positive
Compute the loss.
Step 6: Backpropagation
During backpropagation
Only the soft prompt embeddings receive gradients.
Loss

↓

Update P1 ✅
Update P2 ✅
Update P3 ✅
...
Update P20 ✅

LLM Weights ❌ No Update
Step 7: Repeat
The same prompt vectors are reused for every training example.
After each batch
P1 → P1'
P2 → P2'
...
They gradually become better at steering the frozen LLM toward the desired task.
Training Flow
Dataset
   │
   ▼
Initialize Random Soft Prompt
[P1][P2]...[P20]
   │
   ▼
Concatenate Prompt + Input
   │
   ▼
Frozen LLM
   │
   ▼
Prediction
   │
   ▼
Compute Loss
   │
   ▼
Backpropagation
   │
   ▼
Update ONLY the Soft Prompt
   │
   ▼
Next Batch
What Gets Updated?
Why Prompt Tuning Works
A pretrained LLM already contains knowledge about language, reasoning, and patterns.
Instead of changing the model itself, Prompt Tuning learns a small set of embedding vectors that guide (or steer) the frozen model toward a specific task.
Advantages
Very few trainable parameters
Low memory usage
Fast training
Easy to store and share
One base model can support many tasks by swapping soft prompts
Limitations
The prompt influences the model only at the input layer.
Performance may be lower than other PEFT methods (e.g., Prefix Tuning or LoRA) for complex tasks.
Interview Answer (30 seconds)
Prompt Tuning is a PEFT technique where the pretrained LLM is frozen and only a small set of trainable prompt embeddings, called soft prompts, are optimized. These prompt embeddings are prepended to the input embeddings and passed through the frozen model. During backpropagation, gradients update only the soft prompt matrix, while all LLM weights remain unchanged. This allows task adaptation by training only a few thousand parameters instead of billions.
Prefix Tuning
Definition
Prefix Tuning is a Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) technique where the LLM is frozen, but instead of learning only input prompt embeddings, we learn prefix key-value vectors that are injected into every Transformer layer's attention mechanism.
First, recall Self-Attention
In every Transformer layer, attention computes three things:
Input Embeddings
      │
      ▼
Linear Layers
      │
      ▼
Q (Query)
K (Key)
V (Value)
Attention then computes
Attention(Q, K, V)
The output depends on how Queries interact with Keys and Values.
Normal Transformer
Suppose your sentence is
I love AI
After embedding
[I]
[love]
[AI]
Layer 1 creates
Q1
K1
V1
Layer 2 again creates
Q2
K2
V2
and so on.
Nothing extra is added.
Prefix Tuning Idea
Instead of only adding prompt embeddings at the input,
we learn extra Keys and Values.
Imagine we learn
Prefix K

PK1
PK2
PK3
...

Prefix V

PV1
PV2
PV3
...
These are trainable.
What happens inside Layer 1?
Normally
Queries

Q

attend to

K

and use

V
With Prefix Tuning
Queries

Q

attend to

[Prefix Keys + Original Keys]

and use

[Prefix Values + Original Values]
So attention becomes
Keys

PK1
PK2
PK3

K1
K2
K3

Values

PV1
PV2
PV3

V1
V2
V3
The model can attend to both the learned prefixes and the actual input.
Why not Queries?
A common interview question:
Why are only Key and Value prefixed? Why not Query?
Because the Query comes from the actual input tokens.
The input should decide what it wants to look for.
The learned prefix acts as extra memory that the Query can attend to.
If we changed the Query as well, we'd change how the input itself asks questions.
Training Process
Suppose we have 1000 examples.
Exactly like Prompt Tuning.
Dataset
Movie is awesome → Positive
Movie is bad → Negative
...
Step 1
Freeze the LLM.
Attention ❌ Frozen

Feed Forward ❌ Frozen

Embeddings ❌ Frozen

Everything ❌ Frozen
Step 2
Initialize Prefix Parameters.
Suppose
20 Prefix Tokens
For every transformer layer.
If there are
24 Transformer Layers
then every layer gets
Prefix Keys

20 vectors

Prefix Values

20 vectors
Visual
Layer 1

PK
PV

Layer 2

PK
PV

Layer 3

PK
PV

...

Layer 24

PK
PV
These are randomly initialized.
Step 3
Forward Pass
Input
I love AI
↓
Layer 1
Q

↓

Attend over

PK + K

PV + V
↓
Layer 2
Again
Q

↓

PK + K

PV + V
↓
Output
Step 4
Compute Loss
Same as before.
Step 5
Backpropagation
Now,
ONLY
PK

PV
are updated.
Everything else stays frozen.
What gets updated?
Layer 1

PK ✅

PV ✅

Layer 2

PK ✅

PV ✅

...

Layer 24

PK ✅

PV ✅
Transformer weights
Attention ❌

Feed Forward ❌

Embeddings ❌
No updates.
Why is Prefix Tuning more powerful?
Prompt Tuning
Prompt

↓

Transformer

↓

Output
Once the input enters the transformer, there is no additional learned guidance.
Prefix Tuning
Input

↓

Layer 1 + Prefix

↓

Layer 2 + Prefix

↓

Layer 3 + Prefix

↓

...

↓

Output
Every layer receives learned information.
The model is continuously guided.
Analogy
Prompt Tuning
Imagine taking an exam.
Before entering,
your teacher says
"Remember, answer confidently."
That's it.
No more help.
Prefix Tuning
Now imagine the teacher is sitting beside you during every question.
Whenever you solve a question,
the teacher whispers
"Remember this concept."
"Watch out for this trick."
"Think about this formula."
The teacher is influencing your thinking throughout the exam.
That is Prefix Tuning.
Comparison with Prompt Tuning
Interview Answer
Prefix Tuning is a PEFT technique where the pretrained LLM remains frozen. Instead of learning only input prompt embeddings, it learns trainable prefix key and value vectors for every transformer layer. During attention, the model attends over both the learned prefix key/value vectors and the original key/value vectors. During backpropagation, only these prefix vectors are updated, while all original model weights remain unchanged.
The one concept I want to make sure is crystal clear
In Prompt Tuning, you were updating one soft prompt matrix:
Soft Prompt
[P1...P20]
In Prefix Tuning, you're updating many small matrices—one pair (Keys and Values) for each transformer layer:
Layer 1 : PK1, PV1
Layer 2 : PK2, PV2
Layer 3 : PK3, PV3
...
Layer 24 : PK24, PV24
So instead of learning one steering signal at the input, you're learning a steering signal at every layer of the network. That's the core idea behind Prefix Tuning.
Why was Adaptive Prompt Tuning introduced?
In Prompt Tuning, we learn one soft prompt.
For example,
[P1][P2][P3]...[P20]
This same prompt is used for every input.
[P1...P20] + "Translate to French"

[P1...P20] + "Summarize this article"

[P1...P20] + "Write Python code"
But think about it...
Should the same prompt work equally well for:
Medical questions?
Legal questions?
Coding?
Translation?
Probably not.
Core Idea
Instead of learning one fixed prompt, we make the prompt adapt based on the input or task.
So,
Medical input
↓
[PM1][PM2]...[PM20]
Programming input
↓
[PC1][PC2]...[PC20]
Legal input
↓
[PL1][PL2]...[PL20]
Different inputs receive different learned prompts.
Architecture
Instead of
Input

↓

Fixed Soft Prompt

↓

Frozen LLM
we now have
Input

↓

Prompt Generator

↓

Adaptive Prompt

↓

Frozen LLM
The Prompt Generator decides what prompt should be used.
Training Process
Suppose our dataset contains different domains.
Medical:
Patient has fever.

Programming:
Reverse a linked list.

Legal:
What is contract law?
Step 1
Freeze the LLM.
Transformer ❌ Frozen

Attention ❌ Frozen

Embedding ❌ Frozen
Step 2
Initialize a Prompt Generator.
Instead of storing
[P1...P20]
we learn a small network that produces prompts.
For example
Input

↓

Small Neural Network

↓

Prompt Embeddings
Step 3
Medical Input
Patient has fever.
↓
Prompt Generator
↓
[PM1][PM2]...[PM20]
↓
Frozen LLM
↓
Prediction
Programming Input
Reverse a linked list.
↓
Prompt Generator
↓
[PC1][PC2]...[PC20]
↓
Frozen LLM
Notice that the prompt is different, even though the LLM is the same.
What gets updated?
Exactly like Prompt Tuning,
the LLM stays frozen.
Only the adaptation mechanism is trained.
Prompt Generator ✅

Generated Prompt ✅

Transformer ❌

Attention ❌

Embeddings ❌
Simple Analogy
Imagine you're a teacher.
Prompt Tuning
You always begin class with the same sentence.
"Today, pay attention."
Whether you're teaching:
Kindergarten
College
PhD students
You say the same thing.
Adaptive Prompt Tuning
Now you change your introduction depending on your audience.
For kindergarten:
"Let's learn with pictures!"
For college:
"Let's derive the equations."
For researchers:
"Let's analyze the assumptions."
Same teacher.
Different introduction.
That's Adaptive Prompt Tuning.
Comparison
Example
Suppose you're building a chatbot.
Prompt Tuning
Every query gets
[P1...P20]
[P1...P20] + "Write SQL"

[P1...P20] + "Explain diabetes"

[P1...P20] + "Translate to Spanish"
Adaptive Prompt Tuning
The system first identifies the type of query.
"Write SQL"

↓

Coding Prompt

↓

[PC1...PC20]
"Explain diabetes"

↓

Medical Prompt

↓

[PM1...PM20]
"Translate to Spanish"

↓

Translation Prompt

↓

[PT1...PT20]
The LLM receives a prompt that is better suited to that specific input.
Interview Answer
Adaptive Prompt Tuning extends Prompt Tuning by making the soft prompt dynamic rather than fixed. Instead of using the same learned prompt for every input, a small trainable adaptation mechanism generates or selects a prompt based on the input or task. The pretrained LLM remains frozen, and only the prompt generation or selection mechanism is trained. This allows the model to better handle diverse tasks and domains.
