=== PAGE: quick questions ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/quick-questions-3abfca3384fc803881e6e924cc3dedf6

AI / LLM
1. What is RAG?
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique where an LLM retrieves relevant information from external knowledge sources before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and improving accuracy.
2. Why embeddings?
Embeddings convert text into numerical vectors so that semantically similar texts are located close together, enabling efficient semantic search and retrieval.
3. What is a vector database?
A vector database stores and indexes embeddings (vectors) so that similar documents can be retrieved efficiently using similarity search.
4. Why chunking?
Chunking breaks large documents into smaller pieces so that relevant information can be retrieved accurately while staying within the LLM's context window.
5. Cosine Similarity
Cosine similarity measures the similarity between two vectors by calculating the cosine of the angle between them. A value closer to 1 indicates higher similarity.
6. Temperature
Temperature controls the randomness of an LLM's output. Lower values produce more deterministic responses, while higher values generate more creative responses.
7. Top-P
Top-P (nucleus sampling) limits token selection to the smallest set of tokens whose cumulative probability reaches a specified threshold, balancing creativity and coherence.
8. Hallucinations
Hallucinations occur when an LLM generates incorrect or fabricated information that appears believable but is not supported by facts.
9. Prompt Engineering
Prompt engineering is the practice of designing effective prompts to guide an LLM toward accurate, relevant, and structured responses.
10. Context Window
The context window is the maximum amount of input and output tokens an LLM can process in a single request.
11. Tokenization
Tokenization is the process of breaking text into smaller units called tokens before processing by an LLM.
12. Embedding Model
An embedding model converts text into dense numerical vectors that capture semantic meaning for tasks like semantic search and recommendation.
13. LangChain
LangChain is a framework for building LLM applications by integrating prompts, models, retrieval, memory, tools, and agents into reusable workflows.
14. FAISS
FAISS is a lightweight similarity search library developed by Meta for storing and searching high-dimensional vectors efficiently.
15. OpenAI API
The OpenAI API allows developers to integrate GPT models, embedding models, image generation, and other AI capabilities into applications through REST APIs.
16. LLM Lifecycle
The LLM lifecycle includes data collection, preprocessing, training, fine-tuning, evaluation, deployment, monitoring, and continuous improvement.
Backend
17. Flask
Flask is a lightweight Python web framework used for building REST APIs and web applications.
18. REST API
A REST API is an architectural style that allows applications to communicate over HTTP using standard methods like GET, POST, PUT, and DELETE.
19. JWT
JSON Web Token (JWT) is a secure, stateless token used for authentication and authorization between clients and servers.
20. Authentication
Authentication is the process of verifying the identity of a user or system before granting access.
21. Caching
Caching stores frequently accessed data temporarily so future requests can be served faster without repeated computation or database access.
22. Pagination
Pagination divides large datasets into smaller pages, improving performance and user experience by returning only a subset of records.
23. Logging
Logging is the process of recording application events, errors, and execution details for debugging, monitoring, and auditing.
24. Exception Handling
Exception handling is the process of detecting and managing runtime errors to prevent application crashes and provide graceful error recovery.
React
25. useState
useState is a React Hook that allows functional components to store and update local state, triggering a re-render when the state changes.
26. useEffect
useEffect is a React Hook used to perform side effects such as API calls, subscriptions, or timers after a component renders.
27. Context API
Context API is React's built-in state management solution for sharing data across components without prop drilling.
28. useMemo
useMemo memoizes the result of an expensive computation and recalculates it only when its dependencies change.
29. React.memo
React.memo is a higher-order component that prevents unnecessary re-rendering by memoizing a functional component when its props remain unchanged.
30. Virtual DOM
The Virtual DOM is a lightweight in-memory representation of the real DOM that React uses to efficiently detect changes and update only the necessary parts of the UI.
1. Artificial Intelligence (AI)
Definition
Artificial Intelligence (AI) is the field of creating systems that can perform tasks that normally require human intelligence, such as reasoning, learning, decision-making, and problem-solving.
Examples:
Chess engines
Self-driving cars
Face recognition
ChatGPT
2. Machine Learning (ML)

Rules + Data
      ↓
Output
Machine Learning
Data + Output
      ↓
Model Learns Rules

Train a model using

The model learns the rules.
3. Deep Learning

Deep Learning is a subset of Machine Learning that uses neural networks with multiple layers to learn complex patterns from large amounts of data.
Examples:
ChatGPT

Voice assistants
4. Large Language Model (LLM)
Definition
An LLM (Large Language Model) is a deep learning model trained on massive amounts of text to understand and generate human language.

Claude
Gemini
Llama
Mistral
It predicts the next most likely token based on the previous tokens.
Example:
The capital of France is
LLM predicts
Paris
Interview Definition
An LLM is a transformer-based deep learning model trained on large text datasets to understand, generate, summarise, and reason over natural language.
5. Generative AI

Generative AI refers to AI systems that create new content such as text, images, code, audio, or video.

6. RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation)
Definition
RAG is a technique that retrieves relevant external information and provides it to an LLM before generating a response.
Without RAG
Question

↓

LLM

↓

Answer
LLM only knows what it learned during training.
With RAG
Question

↓

Retriever

↓

Relevant Documents

↓

Now the answer is based on your documents.
7. AI Agent
This is where interviews become interesting.

An AI Agent is an LLM that can use tools, make decisions, and perform actions to accomplish a goal.

Find a free slot.

Send invitations.
Confirm the booking.
A standard LLM cannot do those actions on its own.
8. Agentic AI
This is the latest buzzword.

Agentic AI is a system where one or more AI agents autonomously plan, reason, use tools, and execute multiple steps to achieve a goal with minimal human intervention.

Agentic AI could:
Search Web

↓

↓

Generate Charts

↓

Create PowerPoint

↓

↓

No human needs to perform each step manually.

React Router:
React Router is the standard routing library for React applications. It enables you to:
Create multiple pages in your single-page application
Handle URL parameters and query strings
Manage browser history and navigation
Create nested routes and layouts
Implement protected routes for authentication
import './App.css';
import { BrowserRouter, Routes, Route } from 'react-router-dom'
import UseStateHook from './components/UseStateHook';

function App() {
  return (
    <div>
      <BrowserRouter>
        <Routes>
          <Route path='/' element={<UseStateHook />} />
          <Route path ='*' element={<NotFound />} />
        </Routes>
      </BrowserRouter>
    </div>
  );
}

export default App;


if the URL in the browser doesn’t match any <Route>Nothing renders, so you see a blank page.

path="*" matches any URL that doesn’t match previous routes.


UseEffect and UseState
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react'
export default function UseStateHook() {
    const [recipe, setRecipe] = useState([])
    useEffect(() => {
        fetch('https://varuniherle.github.io/Veggie-Vibe/recipes.json')
            .then((res) => res.json())
            .then((data) => {
                console.log(data)
                setRecipe(data)
            })
            .catch((error) => {
                console.error(error)
            })
    }, [])
    return <>
        <div>This is UseState
            {recipe ? <>
                {recipe.map((d) =>
                    (<div key={d.id}>{d.name}</div>)
                )}
            </> : <></>}
        </div>
    </>
}
more cleaner version
useEffect(() => {
    async function fetchData() {
        try {
            const res = await fetch('https://varuniherle.github.io/Veggie-Vibe/recipes.json');
            const data = await res.json();
            setRecipe(data);
        } catch (error) {
            console.error(error);
        }
    }
    fetchData();
}, []);

Interview Questions
import { useEffect, useState } from "react"
import Child from "./Child";

export default function Parent() {
    const [data, setData] = useState<any>(null)
    async function getData(url: string) {
        try {
            const response = await fetch(url);
            const result = await response.json();
            setData(result);
            // console.log(data);
        }
        catch (error) {
            console.error(error);
        }
    }
    useEffect(() => {
        const url = "https://official-joke-api.appspot.com/random_joke"
        getData(url)
    }, [])
    return (
        <div>
            Parent Class sending props
            <br></br>
            {data ? (
                <>
                    <Child joke= {data} />
                </>
            ) : (
                <>Loading...</>
            )}
        </div>
    )
}
1. What is useState?
Interview Answer
useState is a React Hook that allows functional components to store and manage state. Whenever the state is updated using its setter function, React re-renders the component with the new state.
Example:
const [data,setData]=useState(null);
Here:
data → current state
setData() → updates the state
Follow-up
Q: Why shouldn't we write?
data=result;
Answer:
React won't know that the state has changed, so it won't re-render the component. We should always use the setter function (setData) to update state.
Q: Why didn't console.log(data) print the updated value immediately after setData()?
Answer:
State updates are asynchronous. Calling setData() schedules a re-render, but the state variable inside the current function still contains the old value. The updated value is available during the next render.
2. What is useEffect?
Interview Answer
useEffect is a React Hook used to perform side effects in a functional component.
Common use cases include:
API calls
Timers
Event listeners
Subscriptions
DOM updates
Example:
useEffect(() => {getData();
}, []);
Q: Why did we use useEffect for the API call?
Answer:
If we called getData() directly inside the component, it would execute every time the component re-rendered, causing multiple API requests. Using useEffect with an empty dependency array ensures the API is called only once when the component mounts.
Q: What does [] mean?
useEffect(() => {

}, []);
Answer:
An empty dependency array means the effect runs only once after the component is mounted.
Q: What if there is no dependency array?
useEffect(() => {

});
Answer:
The effect runs after every render.
Q: What if I write?
useEffect(() => {

}, [data]);
Answer:
The effect runs whenever data changes.
3. What are Props?
Interview Answer
Props (Properties) are used to pass data from a parent component to a child component. Props are read-only and help achieve one-way data flow in React.
Example:
Parent
<Child joke={data}/>
Child
functionChild({ joke }) {

}
Q: Can a child modify props?
Answer:
No. Props are immutable. A child component can only read props. If data needs to change, the parent should update its state and pass the updated value down.
Props vs State
4. What is fetch()?
Interview Answer
fetch() is a JavaScript API used to make HTTP requests to a server. It returns a Promise that resolves to a Response object.
Example:
constresponse=awaitfetch(url);
Q: Why do we call response.json()?
Answer:
The response returned by fetch() is a Response object. Calling response.json() converts the JSON response body into a JavaScript object that we can use in our application.
Example:
constresult=awaitresponse.json();
5. What are async and await?
Interview Answer
async and await simplify working with asynchronous operations.
async marks a function as asynchronous.
await pauses execution until the Promise resolves.
Example:
asyncfunctiongetData() {constresponse=awaitfetch(url);
}
Q: Why not use .then()?
Answer:
async/await provides cleaner and more readable code compared to nested .then() calls, especially when handling multiple asynchronous operations.
6. What happens in your application?
This is an excellent interview question because it tests whether you understand the flow.
Component renders

↓

useState creates data = null

↓

React displays "Loading..."

↓

useEffect executes

↓

fetch() sends API request

↓

await waits for response

↓

response.json() converts JSON to object

↓

setData(result)

↓

React re-renders

↓

Parent passes data to Child using props

↓

Child displays joke
If you can explain this sequence confidently, it shows you understand how React components, hooks, async code, and props work together.
so if I have another useState which changes the data, then it will cause the ompontetn rerender right, in that case, will api call be made again
Yes, the component will re-render, but the API will NOT be called again (assuming your useEffect has an empty dependency array).


import { useEffect, useState } from "react";


export default function Parent1() {
    const [data, setData] = useState(null);
    const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
    async function getData(url: string) {
        try {
            const response = await fetch(url);
            const result = await response.json();
            setData(result);
            // console.log(data);
        }
        catch (error) {
            console.error(error);
        }
    }

    useEffect(() => {
        console.log("API called");
        getData("https://official-joke-api.appspot.com/random_joke");
    }, []);

    return (
        <>
            <button onClick={() => setCount(count + 1)}>
                Count: {count}
            </button>
        </>
    );
}
the component will re-render, but the API will NOT be called again (assuming your useEffect has an empty dependency array).
Let's look at an example.
functionParent() 
{
const [data,setData]=useState(null);
const [count,setCount]=useState(0);
useEffect(() => 
{
console.log("API called");
getData();
    }, []);
    
    return (<><buttononClick={() =>setCount(count+1)}>
                Count: {count}</button></>
    );
}
Initial Render
Component renders.
count = 0
data = null
useEffect runs because the component has just mounted.
API is called.
Output:
API called
Click the button
setCount(count+1);
What happens?
count changes.
React re-renders the component.
But useEffect(..., []) does not run again because the dependency array is empty.
So:
❌ API is NOT called again.
Why?
The dependency array tells React when to run the effect.
Case 1: Empty array
useEffect(() => {getData();
}, []);
Runs:
✅ Only once after the component mounts.
Case 2: Dependency on count
useEffect(() => {getData();
}, [count]);
Now every time count changes:
count changes
      ↓
Component re-renders
      ↓
useEffect sees count changed
      ↓
API called again
Case 3: No dependency array
useEffect(() => {getData();
});
This is dangerous.
Every render:
Component renders
      ↓
API call
      ↓
setData()
      ↓
Component renders again
      ↓
API call
      ↓
setData()
      ↓
...
This can create an infinite render loop if the effect updates state on every run.
Think of it this way
A component can re-render for many reasons:
State changes
Props change
Context changes
Parent re-renders
Every re-render does not automatically execute every useEffect.
React checks the dependency array:
Re-render
     ↓
Has any dependency changed?
     ↓
Yes  → Run effect
No   → Skip effect
If another useState causes a component to re-render, will an API call inside useEffect(() => {}, []) run again?
Answer:
No. Updating any state causes the component to re-render, but a useEffect with an empty dependency array runs only once after the initial mount. During subsequent re-renders, React skips that effect because none of its dependencies have changed.
Re-rendering is about React recalculating what the UI should look like.
Running an effect depends on the effect's dependency array.
Prop drilling is the process of passing props through multiple intermediate components so that a deeply nested component can access them, even though the intermediate components don't use those props
App
↓
Parent  ❌ Doesn't use user
↓
Child   ❌ Doesn't use user
↓
GrandChild ✅ Uses user
1. What is Context API?
Answer:
Context API is a built-in React feature that allows data to be shared across multiple components without passing props through every intermediate component. It helps avoid prop drilling.
2. What are the three steps to use Context API?
Create a context using createContext().
Wrap components with a Provider and pass a value.
Consume the value using useContext().
3. Why do we need a Provider?
The Provider supplies the context value to all components inside it. Without a Provider, useContext() returns the default value that was passed to createContext().
4. What is useContext()?
useContext() is a React Hook that reads the current value of a context from the nearest matching Provider.
5. Props vs Context API
Interview Tip
One thing interviewers often ask is:
"Should you use Context API for all state management?"
A good answer is:
No. Context API is great for sharing data that's needed by many components, such as the logged-in user, theme (light/dark mode), language, or authentication state. For local component state, useState is simpler and usually the better choice. Using Context for everything can cause unnecessary re-renders and make state harder to manage.
Understanding Re-rendering
Let's build this example.
App.tsx
import {useState }from"react";
importChildfrom"./Child";
export  default functionApp() {
const [count,setCount]=useState(0);
console.log("App Rendered");
return (
<>
<h1>{count}</h1>
<buttononClick={() =>setCount(count+1)}>Increment</button><Child/>
</>
    );
}
Child.tsx
export default functionChild() 
{console.log("Child Rendered");
return (
<h2>I am Child</h2>
    );
}
Initial Output
App Rendered
Child Rendered
Click Increment once
Console
App Rendered
Child Rendered
Click again
App Rendered
Child Rendered
Question
Did Child's data change?
❌ No.
Did Child still re-render?
✅ Yes.
Why?
Because when a parent re-renders, React re-renders its children by default.
Solution → React.memo 
Wrap the child.
import {memo } from"react";
functionChild() {
console.log("Child Rendered");
return<h2>I am Child</h2>;
}
export default memo(Child);
Now click Increment.
Console
App Rendered
Child is NOT rendered.
Why?
Because React.memo compares props.
Old props == New props
↓
Skip rendering.

React.memo works.
useCallback Interview Answer
useCallback memoizes a function. It returns the same function instance between renders unless one of its dependencies changes. It is useful when passing callback functions to memoized child components.
Biggest Interview Question
Difference between useMemo and useCallback?
Example:
constuser=useMemo(() => ({ name:"Varuni" }), []);
user is an object (a value).
consthandleClick=useCallback(() => {console.log("Clicked");
}, []);
handleClick is a function.
Complete Picture
Parent Re-render
        │
        ▼
Child Re-render
        │
        ▼
React.memo
        │
        ▼
Are props same?
     /      \
   Yes       No
   │          │
Skip       Render
The tricky part is that objects and functions created inside a component are new references on every render, even if they contain the same data. That's why useMemo (for values) and useCallback (for functions) are often used together with React.memo.
Interview tip
A common follow-up question is:
Should you use useMemo, useCallback, and React.memo everywhere?
A strong answer is:
No. They are optimization tools, not default coding patterns. They add their own overhead, so they should be used when profiling shows unnecessary re-renders or expensive computations, especially in large component trees or performance-critical parts of an application.
1. map()
What is it?
map() is used to transform each element of an array into something else. It returns a new array without modifying the original.
Syntax
array.map((item) => {returnsomething;
});
Example 1: Double the numbers
constnumbers= [1,2,3,4,5];constresult=numbers.map((num) =>num*2);console.log(result);
Output:
[2, 4, 6, 8, 10]
Original array:
[1,2,3,4,5]
New array:
[2,4,6,8,10]
Example 2: Extract Names
constusers= [
    { id:1, name:"Varuni" },
    { id:2, name:"Rahul" },
    { id:3, name:"Anu" }
];constnames=users.map((user) =>user.name);console.log(names);
Output
["Varuni", "Rahul", "Anu"]
React Example ⭐
constusers= ["Varuni","Rahul","Anu"];functionApp() {return (<div>
            {users.map((user,index) => (<pkey={index}>{user}</p>
            ))}</div>
    );
}
Output
Varuni

Rahul

Anu
Interview Question
Q: Why do we use map() in React?
Answer:
To render lists of components dynamically from an array.
2. filter()
What is it?
filter() is used to select only the elements that satisfy a condition. It returns a new array.
Example 1
constnumbers= [10,15,20,25,30];constresult=numbers.filter((num) =>num>20);console.log(result);
Output
[25, 30]
Example 2
constemployees= [
    { name:"A", salary:30000 },
    { name:"B", salary:70000 },
    { name:"C", salary:90000 }
];consthighSalary=employees.filter(
    (emp) =>emp.salary>50000
);console.log(highSalary);
Output
[
  { name: "B", salary: 70000 },
  { name: "C", salary: 90000 }
]
React Example
constusers= [
    { name:"Varuni", active:true },
    { name:"Rahul", active:false },
    { name:"Anu", active:true }
];functionApp() {return (<div>
            {users.filter((user) =>user.active).map((user) => (<pkey={user.name}>{user.name}</p>
                ))}</div>
    );
}
Output
Varuni

Anu
Notice we first filter, then map to render.
3. reduce()
This one looks scary but is actually straightforward.
What is it?
reduce() combines all elements of an array into a single value.
That value can be:
Sum
Product
Object
Array
Count
Grouped data
Syntax
array.reduce((accumulator,currentValue) => {returnupdatedAccumulator;
},initialValue);
Accumulator → stores the running result.
Current Value → current array element.
Example 1: Sum
constnumbers= [10,20,30,40];consttotal=numbers.reduce(
    (sum,num) =>sum+num,0
);console.log(total);
Output
100
How it works:
Example 2: Total Salary
constemployees= [
    { salary:50000 },
    { salary:60000 },
    { salary:70000 }
];consttotalSalary=employees.reduce(
    (sum,emp) =>sum+emp.salary,0
);console.log(totalSalary);
Output
180000
Comparison
map()
One item → One transformed item
[1,2,3]↓

[2,4,6]
Same number of elements.
filter()
Keep only matching elements.
[1,2,3,4,5]↓

[2,4]
May have fewer elements.
reduce()
Many elements → One result.
[10,20,30]↓60
Real React Example
Imagine an e-commerce cart.
constcart= [
    { item:"Laptop", price:50000 },
    { item:"Mouse", price:1000 },
    { item:"Keyboard", price:2000 }
];
Render Items
cart.map((product) => (<pkey={product.item}>
        {product.item}</p>
));
Expensive Items
cart.filter((product) =>product.price>1500);
Returns
Laptop

Keyboard
Total Price
cart.reduce(
    (total,product) =>total+product.price,0
);
Returns
53000
Interview Cheat Sheet
Common Interview Questions
Q1. Does map() modify the original array?
Answer: No. It returns a new array and leaves the original unchanged.
Q2. What's the difference between map() and filter()?
map() transforms every element and returns an array of the same length.
filter() selects elements based on a condition, so the returned array may be shorter.
Q3. When would you use reduce()?
Use it when you want to combine an array into a single result, such as calculating totals, counting items, grouping data, or building an object.

=== PAGE: Rag Theory ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Rag-Theory-3a9fca3384fc80b5832eeb5d0382e463

Documents : Data loaders are used to load external data
Tokenization
Definition: The process of breaking raw text into smaller units ("tokens") — which could be words, subwords, or characters — that a model can actually process as numbers. Every LLM/embedding model has its own tokenizer, and text is converted to token IDs before being fed into the model.
When to use: It's not optional — every interaction with an LLM or embedding model goes through tokenization first. In RAG specifically, tokenization matters because chunk sizes are usually measured in tokens, not characters or words.
How to use: You typically don't build tokenizers yourself — you use the one paired with your model (e.g., tiktoken for OpenAI models, AutoTokenizer from Hugging Face for open models). You call it to count tokens (for chunk sizing) or to encode/decode text.
Where to use: Anywhere you need to estimate/control cost, context window usage, or chunk size precisely — e.g., "will this chunk + prompt + retrieved context fit in the model's context window?"
Types of Tokenization
1. Word-based tokenization
Definition: Splits text on whitespace/punctuation into whole words.
Gotcha: Huge vocabulary size, can't handle unseen/rare words (out-of-vocabulary problem), poor with typos or compound words.
2. Character-based tokenization
Definition: Splits text into individual characters.
Gotcha: Tiny vocabulary, no OOV problem, but sequences become very long and lose word-level meaning — inefficient for LLMs.
3. Subword tokenization (what modern LLMs actually use)
Definition: Splits words into meaningful sub-units — common words stay whole, rare/complex words get broken into pieces (e.g., "tokenization" → "token" + "ization").
Why it's the standard: Balances vocabulary size and sequence length; handles unseen words gracefully by decomposing them into known subword pieces.
Main algorithms to know:
BPE (Byte Pair Encoding) — iteratively merges the most frequent adjacent character/subword pairs to build a vocabulary. Used by GPT models.
WordPiece — similar to BPE but merges based on likelihood maximization rather than raw frequency. Used by BERT.
SentencePiece — treats text as a raw stream (including spaces) and works language-agnostically, doesn't require pre-tokenized/whitespace-separated input. Useful for non-space-delimited languages (e.g., Japanese, Chinese). Used by T5, Llama.
Chunking
Definition: Splitting a large document into smaller pieces ("chunks" or "nodes") so they can be embedded and retrieved individually. Necessary because embedding models and LLM context windows have size limits, and retrieval precision improves when chunks are topically focused rather than huge.
When to use: Always, in any RAG pipeline — the question is which strategy, not whether.
Gotcha to know: There's a tradeoff — small chunks = precise retrieval but less context per chunk; large chunks = more context but noisier/less precise matches, and you risk exceeding token limits when you stuff multiple chunks into the prompt.
1. SentenceSplitter
Definition: Splits text at sentence boundaries (using punctuation/NLP sentence detection), then packs sentences together up to a target chunk size, with optional overlap.
When to use: Default/general-purpose choice for most text documents (articles, reports, docs). Good balance of speed and coherence.
How to use: Set chunk_size (tokens) and chunk_overlap. It respects sentence boundaries so you don't cut a sentence in half.
Where to use: LlamaIndex default splitter. Use for prose-heavy content: blogs, wiki pages, policy docs.
2. TokenTextSplitter
Definition: Splits text purely by token count, without respecting sentence/paragraph boundaries.
When to use: When you need strict control over chunk size (e.g., matching embedding model's exact token limit) and don't care about breaking mid-sentence.
How to use: Set chunk_size and chunk_overlap in tokens; it cuts mechanically at token boundaries.
Where to use: Less semantically clean — good for structured/log-like data, or when speed/simplicity matters more than coherence. Rarely the best choice for natural language docs since it can slice sentences awkwardly.
3. SemanticSplitterNodeParser
Definition: Instead of fixed size, splits text based on semantic meaning shifts — it embeds sentences and finds natural "breakpoints" where topic/meaning changes significantly.
When to use: When document topics vary widely within the same file (e.g., a long report covering multiple sub-topics) and you want chunks that are semantically coherent, not just size-coherent.
How to use: Requires an embedding model. It computes sentence embeddings, measures similarity between consecutive sentences/groups, and splits where similarity drops below a threshold (this is the cosine similarity part — see below).
Where to use: Higher-quality RAG pipelines, research/legal/medical docs where topic boundaries matter more than uniform chunk size. More compute-expensive than fixed-size splitting.
4. HierarchicalNodeParser
Definition: Creates a multi-level tree of chunks — e.g., large parent chunks split into medium chunks, split further into small child chunks. Preserves parent-child relationships.
When to use: When you want both precise retrieval (small chunk match) AND full context (return the parent chunk to the LLM) — this is the basis of "small-to-big" or "auto-merging" retrieval.
How to use: Define chunk sizes per level (e.g., [2048, 512, 128]). Retrieval matches on small chunks, then the parent/larger chunk is returned or "merged" for context.
Where to use: Complex, long documents (legal contracts, technical manuals) where you need surgical retrieval but rich context in the final answer. Pairs with AutoMergingRetriever in LlamaIndex.
5. SemanticSplitterNodeParser → Cosine Similarity
Definition: This is the mechanism underlying the semantic splitter — not a separate method. Sentences are embedded into vectors, and cosine similarity measures the angle between consecutive sentence embeddings. A sharp drop in similarity = topic change = chunk boundary.
When to use: This is automatic once you choose SemanticSplitterNodeParser — you're just tuning the similarity threshold/percentile (called breakpoint_percentile_threshold).
How to use: Lower threshold = more splits (smaller, more granular chunks); higher threshold = fewer splits (larger, more tolerant of topic drift).
Where to use: Same as above — mention this in interviews to show you understand why semantic splitting works, not just that it exists.
6. Fixed-size / CharacterTextSplitter
Definition: Splits text into chunks based purely on a fixed number of characters (not tokens, not sentences). No awareness of words, sentences, or meaning — it just counts characters and cuts.
When to use: As a quick baseline/prototype when you just want something working fast, or when text structure doesn't matter (e.g., quick demos, non-critical internal tools).
How to use: Set chunk_size (e.g., 1000 characters) and chunk_overlap (e.g., 100 characters). It slices the string mechanically at those intervals.
Where to use: Rarely production-grade. Mention it in interviews as the "naive baseline" everyone starts with before moving to smarter methods.
Gotcha: Can cut words/sentences in half, breaking semantic meaning — e.g., "The patient was diag" | "nosed with..." This is exactly why token- and sentence-aware splitters exist.
7. RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter (LangChain)
Definition: Tries a prioritized list of separators — typically ["\n\n", "\n", " ", ""] (paragraph → line → word → character) — and recursively splits using the first separator that gets chunks under the target size. Falls back to finer-grained separators only when needed.
When to use: Default go-to splitter for most general text in LangChain pipelines when you want reasonably coherent chunks without the overhead of semantic/embedding-based splitting.
How to use: Define chunk_size, chunk_overlap, and optionally customize the separator list (e.g., add "." for sentence-level fallback, or use different separators for code vs. prose).
Where to use: General-purpose RAG over articles, docs, reports — anywhere you want a fast, structure-respecting splitter without semantic embedding cost.
Gotcha to know: It's not semantic — it just respects structural boundaries better than raw character splitting. Often confused with SentenceSplitter; the key difference is the fallback hierarchy through multiple separator types, not just sentence detection.
8. Markdown/HTML/Code-aware Splitters
Definition: Splitters that use the structural syntax of a format (headers in Markdown, tags in HTML, function/class definitions in code) as natural chunk boundaries, instead of treating the input as plain text.
When to use: When your source documents have meaningful structure — technical documentation, READMEs, API docs, codebases — where splitting mid-function or mid-section would destroy meaning.
How to use:
MarkdownNodeParser splits on headers (#, ##, etc.), keeping each section (with its heading) as a chunk, often preserving header hierarchy as metadata.
CodeSplitter uses language-aware parsing (e.g., via tree-sitter) to split at function/class boundaries so a chunk is a complete, runnable unit of code.
HTML splitters split on tag structure (e.g., <div>, <section>, <h1>).
Where to use: Developer-facing RAG (code search, internal engineering docs), knowledge bases written in Markdown (Notion exports, GitHub wikis, docs sites).
Gotcha to know: Preserves semantic units of the format — this matters a lot in interviews because it shows you understand chunking should match the document's native structure, not force a one-size-fits-all splitter on everything.
9. Sliding Window Chunking
Definition: Not a distinct splitter class but a technique — chunks of fixed size are created with overlapping regions between consecutive chunks, so content near a chunk boundary appears in both the chunk before and after it.
When to use: Whenever there's a concern that important context sits right at a chunk boundary and a hard cut would lose it (e.g., a sentence explaining something is split so the "something" is in chunk 1 and the explanation is in chunk 2).
How to use: Set chunk_overlap as a parameter alongside chunk_size in almost any splitter (e.g., overlap = 10-20% of chunk size is a common default). This is why nearly every splitter (SentenceSplitter, TokenTextSplitter, RecursiveCharacterTextSplitter) exposes an overlap parameter.
Where to use: Universally applicable — it's a knob you tune on top of whichever splitter you choose, not a standalone method.
Gotcha to know: In interviews, clarify that this is an orthogonal technique to chunk-boundary strategy, not a competing splitter. Trade-off: more overlap = better context continuity but more redundant storage/compute and duplicate retrieval hits.
10. Document-based / Layout-aware Chunking
Definition: Splits based on the visual/structural layout of a document rather than raw text flow — recognizing sections, headers, tables, figures, slides, and columns using a layout parser (often ML-based document understanding models).
When to use: Complex, real-world documents where naive text extraction destroys structure — multi-column PDFs, scanned reports, slide decks, documents with embedded tables/images that need to stay intact or be handled specially.
How to use: Use tools like Unstructured.io or LlamaParse to first parse the document into structured elements (title, narrative text, table, list-item, image), then chunk each element type appropriately (e.g., keep tables whole, treat each slide as one chunk).
Where to use: PDF-heavy or multi-modal RAG systems — financial reports, scientific papers, legal filings, PowerPoint-based knowledge bases.
Gotcha to know: This solves a different problem than the splitters above — it's about correct extraction before chunking (garbage in, garbage out), not just how to cut text once extracted. Good to mention if asked "what if my PDF has tables/multi-column layout" — a common interview curveball.
11. Agentic / LLM-based Chunking
Definition: Uses an LLM itself to read the document and decide where meaningful chunk boundaries should go, based on actual understanding of meaning/topic/intent — rather than any mechanical rule (character count, separators, or embedding similarity).
When to use: Highest-stakes or highest-complexity documents where chunk quality directly impacts downstream accuracy, and cost/latency of running an LLM pass over the whole document is acceptable (e.g., legal contracts, medical records where boundary errors are costly).
How to use: Prompt an LLM with the document (or sliding sections of it) asking it to identify logical breakpoints, output chunk boundaries, or even directly output the chunks with self-contained context. Can also involve having the LLM generate a summary/label per chunk for better retrieval later.
Where to use: Emerging/cutting-edge technique — not yet common in production due to cost and latency, but worth mentioning if asked about "future directions" or "how would you improve chunking quality further."
Gotcha to know: This is expensive and slow compared to embedding-based semantic splitting, so in interviews frame it as "the next step up from SemanticSplitterNodeParser when you need even more precision and can afford the cost" — shows you understand the cost/quality spectrum across all these methods.
Embeddings
Definition: A numerical vector representation of text (or other data) that captures its semantic meaning in a high-dimensional space. Texts with similar meaning end up with vectors that are close together (measured via cosine similarity, dot product, or Euclidean distance).
When to use: Any time you need to compare meaning rather than exact keyword match — this is the backbone of RAG's retrieval step: embed the query, embed the chunks, find the closest ones.
How to use: Pass text through an embedding model to get a fixed-size vector (e.g., 1536 dimensions for text-embedding-3-small). Store these vectors in a vector database. At query time, embed the query the same way and search for nearest neighbors.
Where to use: Semantic search, RAG retrieval, clustering, recommendation systems, deduplication, anomaly detection — anywhere "similarity of meaning" is the task.
Key Embedding Concepts
1. Dense vs. Sparse embeddings
Dense embeddings: Every dimension has a (usually non-zero) value; captures semantic/contextual meaning. Produced by neural embedding models (OpenAI, Cohere, Sentence-Transformers). Good for conceptual/synonym-aware search.
Sparse embeddings: Mostly zero values, one dimension per vocabulary term (like TF-IDF/BM25-style representations). Good for exact keyword/term matching. Used in hybrid search alongside dense embeddings.
2. Similarity metrics
Cosine similarity: Measures the angle between two vectors, ignoring magnitude — most common for text embeddings.
Dot product: Similar to cosine but affected by vector magnitude — used when embeddings are normalized, or magnitude carries meaning.
Euclidean distance: Straight-line distance between points — less common for text, more common in traditional ML/clustering.
3. Popular embedding models to know
OpenAI: text-embedding-3-small / text-embedding-3-large — strong general-purpose, easy API access.
Cohere embed models — strong multilingual support.
Sentence-Transformers (open-source, e.g., all-MiniLM-L6-v2) — self-hosted, free, good for cost-sensitive or on-prem setups.
BGE / E5 models — open-source, competitive with commercial models, common on MTEB leaderboard mentions.
Interview gotcha: Be ready to mention the MTEB leaderboard (Massive Text Embedding Benchmark) as the standard way people compare embedding model quality across tasks (retrieval, clustering, classification).
4. Embedding dimensionality
Definition: The size of the vector (e.g., 384, 768, 1536, 3072 dimensions).
Tradeoff: Higher dimensions = more nuanced meaning captured, but more storage/compute cost and slower search. Some models (like OpenAI's v3) support Matryoshka embeddings — you can truncate the vector to fewer dimensions with graceful quality degradation, trading accuracy for speed/storage.
5. Symmetric vs. Asymmetric embedding tasks
Symmetric: Comparing similar types of text (e.g., two sentences of similar length/nature) — e.g., duplicate detection.
Asymmetric: Comparing different types of text (e.g., short query vs. long document) — this is exactly the RAG retrieval scenario. Some models have separate encoding modes for "query" vs. "passage" (e.g., E5 models require prefixing text with "query: " or "passage: ").
Embeddings vs. Vector DB
Embedding (model/process):
The model that converts text into a numerical vector.
It's a transformation step — text in, vector out.
Think of it as a function: embed("cats are mammals") → [0.12, -0.45, 0.88, ...]
Examples: OpenAI text-embedding-3-small, Sentence-Transformers, Cohere embed.
Vector Database (storage + retrieval system):
The database that stores all those vectors (usually alongside the original text/metadata) and lets you search them efficiently.
Its job is: given a query vector, find the top-K most similar stored vectors — fast, even across millions of vectors.
Examples: Pinecone, Weaviate, Qdrant, Milvus, Chroma, pgvector (Postgres extension), FAISS (technically a library, not a full DB).

The embedding model is responsible for representation — turning text into a meaningful vector. The vector database is responsible for storage and retrieval — efficiently indexing millions of those vectors and finding the nearest ones to a query vector. One creates the data, the other organizes and searches it.

Vector DB
Definition: A specialized database designed to store, index, and search high-dimensional vectors efficiently — enabling fast similarity search (finding the "nearest" vectors to a query) across potentially millions/billions of vectors, which would be far too slow with brute-force comparison or traditional relational DB indexes (B-trees, etc.).
How to use: Store each chunk as: {vector, original text, metadata (source, page, timestamp, tags)}. Query by embedding the search text into a vector and asking the DB for top-K nearest neighbors, often combined with metadata filters (e.g., "only search chunks where department = 'HR'").
Where to use: The retrieval backbone of any RAG system, semantic search product, or similarity-based recommendation engine.
Why not just brute-force compare?
Definition of the problem: Comparing a query vector against every stored vector one-by-one (k-NN, exact nearest neighbor) is accurate but O(n) — too slow at scale (millions/billions of vectors).
Solution: ANN (Approximate Nearest Neighbor) algorithms — trade a small amount of accuracy for a massive speed gain by using smart indexing structures instead of brute-force comparison.
Interview gotcha: Always mention the speed vs. accuracy tradeoff — ANN doesn't guarantee the exact top-K nearest neighbors, just a very close approximation, in exchange for being orders of magnitude faster.
Key ANN Indexing Algorithms
1. HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World)
Definition: Builds a multi-layer graph structure where each vector is a node, connected to its "neighbors." Top layers have fewer nodes/long-range connections (for fast coarse navigation), bottom layers have more nodes/short-range connections (for fine-grained accuracy).
How it works (intuition): Search starts at the top sparse layer, greedily moves toward the query's region, then drops down layer by layer, refining the search each time — like using a highway system to get to the general area, then local roads to reach the exact address.
When to use: The most popular, default choice in most modern vector DBs (Pinecone, Weaviate, Qdrant, Milvus) — great balance of speed, accuracy, and memory usage.
Where to use: Default choice for most production RAG systems unless you have a very specific reason not to.
Gotcha: High recall and fast query speed, but has higher memory usage than some alternatives (since it stores the graph structure) and index-building can be slower.
2. IVF (Inverted File Index)
Definition: Partitions the vector space into clusters (via k-means), each with a "centroid." At query time, it first finds the nearest cluster centroid(s), then only searches within those clusters instead of the whole dataset.
How it works (intuition): Like dividing a library into sections by topic first, then only searching the relevant section instead of the whole library.
When to use: Good when you need to balance speed and memory, especially at very large scale, and can tolerate a bit more approximation.
How to use: Key tunable parameter is nprobe — how many clusters to search (higher nprobe = more accurate but slower).
Where to use: Common in FAISS; often combined with other techniques (IVF + PQ, see below) for very large-scale deployments (billions of vectors).
Gotcha: Accuracy depends heavily on how well the clusters were formed and how many clusters you search (nprobe) — if a relevant vector's cluster isn't probed, it's simply missed.
3. PQ (Product Quantization)
Definition: A compression technique (not a search algorithm by itself) — splits each vector into sub-vectors, then quantizes each sub-vector to a small set of representative codes, drastically reducing memory footprint.
When to use: When memory is the bottleneck (billions of vectors) and you're willing to trade some accuracy for massive storage savings.
Where to use: Often combined with IVF (IVF-PQ) in FAISS for large-scale, memory-constrained deployments.
Gotcha: Mention this as the "memory efficiency" answer if asked "how would you scale vector search to billions of vectors cheaply?"
4. FAISS (Facebook AI Similarity Search)
Definition: Not a database itself — it's a library (from Meta) providing highly optimized implementations of ANN algorithms (Flat/brute-force, IVF, HNSW, PQ, and combinations).
When to use: When you want fine-grained control over indexing strategy, are comfortable managing infrastructure yourself, and don't need a full managed DB (no built-in persistence, metadata filtering, or multi-tenancy out of the box — you build that around it).
Where to use: Common in research, prototyping, or as the underlying engine inside other tools/vector DBs.
Interview gotcha: Be ready to clarify: FAISS = library/algorithm toolkit, not a full database. Vector DBs (Pinecone, Milvus, etc.) often use FAISS-like algorithms internally but add persistence, metadata filtering, scalability, and APIs on top.
Popular Vector Databases to Know
Metadata Filtering
Definition: The ability to combine vector similarity search with traditional filters (e.g., date > 2024-01-01 AND department = 'Legal ')) — narrowing the search space before or during ANN search.
When to use: Almost always in real production RAG — pure semantic search without filters often returns irrelevant results from the wrong document set, wrong time period, wrong access-permission group, etc.
Gotcha to know: This is called hybrid filtering (not to be confused with hybrid search, which is dense+sparse). A common interview question: "how do you handle access control in RAG?" — answer: metadata filtering at the vector DB level (e.g., tag each chunk with user_group and filter at query time).
Indexing algorithms (HNSW, IVF, PQ) exist purely to make the search/retrieval step fast. They have nothing to do with creating embeddings or deciding chunk boundaries — those already happened earlier in the pipeline.
Where indexing fits in the pipeline
Chunking → split document into chunks (text-based, no vectors yet)
Embedding → each chunk → passed through embedding model → vector produced
Indexing ← this is where HNSW/IVF/PQ come in → the vector DB organizes all these stored vectors into a structure that makes search fast
Retrieval/Search → query gets embedded → the index structure is used to quickly find the nearest vectors, instead of comparing against every single stored vector one-by-one
Indexing algorithms are 100% about search/retrieval speed. They're a way of organizing already-created embeddings so that finding the nearest neighbors to a query vector is fast, instead of scanning everything.
Interview-sharp one-liner if asked "what does indexing do in a vector DB?":
"Indexing doesn't change what the vectors are — it changes how quickly you can find the relevant ones. It trades a small amount of accuracy (approximate vs exact nearest neighbor) for a huge gain in search speed at scale."
One more gotcha worth knowing: Indexing happens once, at insert/build time (or periodically rebuilt), while search happens repeatedly, at every query. So there's also a build-time cost vs. query-time cost tradeoff to mention — e.g., HNSW has slower index-building but very fast queries.
Indexing doesn't decide how data is stored (that's the DB's storage engine's job — how vectors + metadata are persisted, compressed, replicated, sharded, etc.).
Indexing decides how the stored vectors are organized/connected so search is fast — building a graph (HNSW) or clusters (IVF) that reference those stored vectors.
Retrieval Strategies — Overview
Definition: The methods used to fetch the most relevant chunks from the vector DB (or other index) in response to a query. This is the step that directly determines RAG quality — bad retrieval means the LLM never even sees the right information, no matter how good the generation model is.
1. Dense Retrieval (Semantic Search)
Definition: Retrieval using dense embedding vectors + similarity search (cosine/dot product)  (embed query → search vector DB).
When to use: Default approach — captures semantic/conceptual similarity, handles synonyms and paraphrasing well (e.g., "car" matches "automobile").
Gotcha: Weak at exact keyword/term matching — struggles with rare terms, product codes, acronyms, names (e.g., searching "SKU-4471" might not retrieve the exact chunk containing it, because dense embeddings focus on meaning, not exact tokens).
2. Sparse Retrieval (Keyword-based Search)
Definition: Traditional term-matching retrieval — represents text as sparse vectors (mostly zeros, one dimension per vocabulary term), scored by term frequency/rarity.
Key algorithm: BM25
Definition: An improved version of TF-IDF — scores documents based on term frequency (how often query terms appear), inverse document frequency (how rare the term is across the corpus), and document length normalization.
When to use: Great for exact keyword matches, technical terms, IDs, names, acronyms — anywhere exact lexical match matters more than semantic similarity.
Gotcha: Doesn't understand synonyms or meaning — "car" and "automobile" are completely unrelated to BM25.
3. Hybrid Search
Definition: Combines dense (semantic) and sparse (keyword/BM25) retrieval, then merges/re-ranks the results — getting the best of both: semantic understanding + exact keyword precision.
When to use: Almost always the production-recommended approach — real queries often need both (e.g., "what's the refund policy for SKU-4471" needs semantic understanding of "refund policy" AND exact match on "SKU-4471").
How to use: Run both searches in parallel, then combine scores using a fusion method.
Key fusion technique: RRF (Reciprocal Rank Fusion)
Definition: Combines rankings from multiple retrieval methods by scoring each document based on its rank position in each list (not raw scores, which aren't directly comparable across methods), using the formula score = Σ 1/(k + rank).
Why rank-based, not score-based: Cosine similarity scores and BM25 scores are on completely different scales — you can't just average them directly. RRF sidesteps this by using rank position instead.
Where to use: Weaviate, Qdrant, Pinecone, and Elasticsearch all support hybrid search natively — mention this if asked "how would you improve retrieval for a system with lots of technical jargon/IDs."
4. Re-ranking
Definition: A two-stage retrieval process — first retrieve a larger candidate set (e.g., top 50) cheaply using vector search, then use a more expensive but more accurate model to re-score and re-order just those candidates down to the final top-K (e.g., top 5).
When to use: When initial retrieval is fast but imprecise (bi-encoder embeddings), and you want higher precision in the final results without paying the cost of running an expensive model over the entire corpus.
How to use: Use a cross-encoder re-ranker (e.g., Cohere Rerank, ms-marco cross-encoders, BGE-reranker) — it takes the query and each candidate chunk together as input (not separately, like embeddings do) and outputs a relevance score directly.
Key distinction — Bi-encoder vs. Cross-encoder:
Bi-encoder (regular embeddings): encodes query and document separately into vectors, then compares — fast, scalable, used for initial retrieval across millions of docs.
Cross-encoder (re-ranker): encodes query + document together in one pass — much more accurate (captures fine-grained interaction between query and text) but far slower — can't scale to millions of docs, only used on a small candidate set.
Interview gotcha: This bi-encoder vs. cross-encoder tradeoff is a classic question — "why not just use a cross-encoder for everything?" Answer: too slow/expensive to run over the whole corpus for every query — that's why re-ranking is a second stage over a small candidate set, not a replacement for initial retrieval.
5. MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance)
Definition: A retrieval technique that balances relevance to the query with diversity among the results — avoiding returning 5 chunks that are all near-duplicates saying the same thing.
When to use: When your corpus has redundant/overlapping content and you want the LLM to see varied information rather than 5 chunks all repeating the same point.
How to use: MMR iteratively selects the next chunk that maximizes: λ × relevance(query, chunk) − (1−λ) × max_similarity(chunk, already_selected_chunks). The λ parameter tunes the relevance/diversity tradeoff.
Where to use: LangChain/LlamaIndex both expose MMR as a retriever option — good for research/document summarization tasks where you want broad coverage, not just the single most relevant angle repeated.
6. Multi-Query Retrieval
Definition: Instead of retrieving using just the user's original query, use an LLM to generate multiple reworded/paraphrased versions of the query, retrieve for each, then combine/deduplicate the results.
When to use: When the user's query phrasing might not match how the information is phrased in the documents (vocabulary mismatch problem), or when the query is ambiguous/could be interpreted multiple ways.
How to use: Prompt an LLM: "generate 3 different ways to ask this question" → run retrieval for each → union/merge the retrieved chunks (often deduplicated).
Where to use: Improves recall in cases where a single embedding of the original query might miss relevant chunks phrased differently.
7. HyDE (Hypothetical Document Embeddings)
Definition: Instead of embedding the user's query directly, first ask an LLM to generate a hypothetical answer to the query, then embed that hypothetical answer and use it to search — the idea being that a hypothetical answer's embedding is closer to actual document chunks than a short question's embedding is.
When to use: When queries are short/ambiguous and don't semantically resemble the answer format found in documents (e.g., question vs. passage embedding mismatch — ties back to the asymmetric embedding point from earlier).
How to use: LLM(query) → hypothetical answer → embed(hypothetical answer) → search vector DB.
Gotcha to know: This adds an extra LLM call (cost/latency), and quality depends on the LLM's hypothetical answer being reasonably accurate in style/content, even if factually imperfect — because it's the semantic shape being matched, not the actual fact.
8. Small-to-Big / Auto-Merging Retrieval (Parent-Child)
Definition: Retrieve using small, precise chunks (for accurate matching), but return the larger parent chunk (or merge multiple matched child chunks into their shared parent) to give the LLM fuller context. Ties directly back to HierarchicalNodeParser from the chunking section.
When to use: When small chunks retrieve accurately but don't contain enough context alone for the LLM to generate a good answer.
How to use: LlamaIndex's AutoMergingRetriever — if enough child chunks under the same parent are retrieved, it automatically "merges" them and returns the parent chunk instead.
9. Self-Query / Query Construction
Definition: Uses an LLM to parse the user's natural language query and automatically extract structured metadata filters from it (e.g., "show me papers about transformers published after 2020" → semantic search on "transformers" + metadata filter date > 2020).
When to use: When users phrase filter conditions in natural language rather than using a structured filter UI, and you want the system to auto-detect and apply those filters.
Where to use: LangChain's SelfQueryRetriever — good to mention when discussing how to combine semantic search with metadata filtering intelligently rather than requiring the user to specify filters manually.
10. Contextual Compression
Definition: After retrieving chunks, use an LLM (or lightweight model) to extract/compress only the relevant portions of each chunk before passing to the final generation step — reducing noise and token usage.
When to use: When retrieved chunks are large and contain a lot of irrelevant filler alongside the relevant part — helps keep the final prompt focused and reduces hallucination risk from noisy context.
Evaluation Metrics for RAG
Definition: RAG evaluation is split into two halves because RAG has two failure points — retrieval (did we find the right chunks?) and generation (did the LLM produce a good answer using those chunks?). A strong interview answer always separates these two, since "the RAG system is bad" could mean either one.
Interview framing to open with: "RAG evaluation isn't one number — you need to evaluate retrieval and generation separately, because a good answer built from wrong context is a lucky guess, and a bad answer built from perfect context is a generation failure."
Part 1: Retrieval Metrics (did we fetch the right chunks?)
1. Context Precision
Definition: Of the chunks retrieved, how many are actually relevant to the query? Measures noise in retrieval.
When to use: When you suspect the retriever is pulling in irrelevant chunks alongside good ones (diluting context, wasting tokens, confusing the LLM).
How to use: Relevant chunks retrieved / Total chunks retrieved. Often calculated with LLM-as-judge scoring each retrieved chunk for relevance.
2. Context Recall
Definition: Of all the relevant chunks that exist in the corpus, how many did we actually retrieve? Measures missed information.
When to use: When you suspect the retriever is failing to surface information that's actually in the knowledge base (a bigger problem than precision — you can't generate a correct answer from information you never retrieved).
How to use: Requires a ground-truth/reference answer to check whether the retrieved context contains everything needed to support it. Relevant chunks retrieved / Total relevant chunks that exist.
Interview gotcha: Recall failures are more dangerous than precision failures — low precision means noisy but survivable context; low recall means the LLM literally never had a chance to give the right answer.
3. Context Relevance
Definition: A simpler, reference-free version of precision — just asks "how relevant is this retrieved context to the query overall?" without needing ground truth.
When to use: Quick sanity-check metric when you don't have labeled ground-truth answers available.
4. Traditional IR Metrics (good to mention as foundational)
Precision@K: Of the top K retrieved chunks, how many are relevant.
Recall@K: Of all relevant chunks, how many appear in the top K.
MRR (Mean Reciprocal Rank): Measures how high up the first relevant result appears — 1/rank of first relevant result, averaged over queries. Good when you only care about getting one correct answer near the top.
NDCG (Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain): Accounts for graded relevance (not just relevant/irrelevant) and rewards relevant results appearing higher in the ranking more than lower. More nuanced than Precision/Recall@K.
Interview gotcha: These are borrowed from classic search/information retrieval — mentioning them shows you know RAG evaluation didn't invent retrieval evaluation from scratch, it inherited it.
Part 2: Generation Metrics (did the LLM produce a good answer?)
5. Faithfulness (a.k.a. Groundedness)
Definition: Does the generated answer only contain claims that are actually supported by the retrieved context — or did the LLM hallucinate/add information not present in the context?
When to use: The single most important RAG-specific metric — this is what separates "RAG" quality evaluation from generic LLM output evaluation. Directly measures hallucination.
How to use: Break the generated answer into individual claims/statements, then check each one against the retrieved context (often via LLM-as-judge) — score = fraction of claims that are supported.
Interview gotcha: This is usually the #1 metric interviewers want to hear you mention first — RAG's whole purpose is to reduce hallucination by grounding answers in retrieved facts, so faithfulness directly measures whether that goal is being achieved.
6. Answer Relevance
Definition: Does the generated answer actually address the user's question — regardless of whether it's factually grounded? (A perfectly faithful but off-topic answer scores badly here.)
When to use: Catches cases where the LLM is grounded in the context but goes off on a tangent or answers a slightly different question than what was asked.
How to use: Often measured by generating hypothetical questions the answer would be a good response to, then comparing semantic similarity to the actual user question (this is roughly how RAGAS computes it).
Interview gotcha: Faithfulness and Answer Relevance are independent axes — you can be high on one and low on the other. Example: a technically accurate answer that doesn't actually address what the user asked = high faithfulness, low answer relevance. Good to explicitly state this independence when asked.
7. Answer Correctness / Answer Similarity
Definition: Compares the generated answer to a ground-truth reference answer — how factually and semantically similar are they?
When to use: When you have a labeled test set with "correct" answers, useful for regression testing (did a pipeline change make things better or worse) rather than open-ended production monitoring.
How to use: Combines semantic similarity (embedding-based comparison to reference) with factual overlap checks.
Part 3: The RAGAS Framework (know this by name)
Definition: A popular open-source framework specifically built for RAG evaluation, providing standardized implementations of the metrics above (faithfulness, answer relevance, context precision, context recall) — typically computed using an LLM-as-judge under the hood.
When to use: When asked "how would you actually implement RAG evaluation in practice" — RAGAS is the go-to name-drop, since it's become close to an industry standard.
How to use: Feed it (question, retrieved_contexts, generated_answer, ground_truth) tuples, and it returns scores per metric.
Where to use: CI/CD pipelines for RAG systems, regression testing after changing chunking/retrieval/prompting strategies, and benchmarking different RAG configurations against each other.
Interview gotcha: Be ready to mention that RAGAS itself heavily relies on LLM-as-judge — meaning your evaluation quality is bottlenecked by the judge LLM's own reliability, which is a known limitation worth flagging if pushed on it.
Part 4: LLM-as-Judge (the evaluation method, not a metric itself)
Definition: Using a strong LLM (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) to score/evaluate outputs against criteria (faithfulness, relevance, etc.) instead of relying purely on human evaluation or hard-coded rules.
When to use: When you need to evaluate at scale (thousands of examples) where human evaluation is too slow/expensive, and no simple rule-based metric captures the nuance needed (e.g., "is this answer faithful to the context" isn't a string-match problem).
How to use: Prompt an LLM with the question, context, and generated answer, asking it to score/classify according to a clear rubric — often with chain-of-thought reasoning to improve reliability.
Gotcha to know: Known issues to mention if asked about limitations:
Self-preference bias — LLMs can rate outputs from similar/same-family models more favorably.
Position bias — order of options presented can skew judgments in comparison tasks.
Cost/latency — still slower and more expensive than rule-based metrics at true scale.
Human Evaluation (still worth mentioning)
Definition: Direct human judgment on answer quality, faithfulness, and relevance — the "gold standard" but expensive and slow.
When to use: For final validation before shipping major changes, or for building/validating the ground-truth datasets used to check whether LLM-as-judge itself is reliable.
Interview gotcha: A mature answer mentions that LLM-as-judge should itself be periodically validated against human judgment on a sample — you don't fully replace human eval, you use it more sparingly to calibrate the automated approach.

Decorators in Python are a powerful and elegant way to extend or modify the behavior of functions or classes without permanently changing their source code
# decorcaters in python
def my_wrapper(func):
    def wrapper():
        print("this is something before the call")
        func()
        print("this is something after the call")
    return wrapper

@my_wrapper
def some_function():
    print("hi")

some_function()
Garbage collection:
Reference Counting
Reference counting is the primary memory management strategy in Python. Every object in Python has a reference count, which tracks the number of references (variables, container objects, etc.) pointing to it.
When the count increases, The reference count for an object goes up whenever a new reference is made to it. For example, when you assign an object to a new variable or add it to a list or dictionary.
When the count decreases: The reference count decreases when a reference is removed. This happens when a variable goes out of scope, is reassigned, or an object is removed from a container.
Deallocation: When an object's reference count drops to zero, Python's memory manager immediately deallocates the memory for that object.
A circular reference occurs when two or more objects refer to each other, but are no longer accessible from the rest of the program. In this scenario, their reference counts will never drop to zero, even though they are effectively "garbage.”
Generational Garbage Collection
To address the issue of circular references, Python uses a generational garbage collector. This collector runs periodically to find and clean up objects that cannot be deallocated by reference counting. The key idea behind this system is that most objects have a short lifespan.
The garbage collector divides objects into three "generations" based on their age:
Generation 0: Newly created objects.
Generation 1: Objects that survived a garbage collection pass in Generation 0.
Generation 2: Objects that survived a garbage collection pass in Generation 1.
The garbage collector's strategy is to sweep the generations at different frequencies. Generation 0 is checked most frequently, as it's likely to contain the most objects ready for cleanup. If an object survives a collection, it is promoted to the next generation. A collection in a higher generation is triggered only after a certain number of collections in the previous generation have occurred.
Python’s context managers provide a neat way to automatically set up and clean up resources, ensuring they’re properly managed even if errors occur.

The with statement ensures that the file is automatically and safely closed after the code block is executed, even if an error occurs.


# opening a file  with
with open('data.json','r') as file:
    file = json.load(file)
    print(file)
# laoding the json files without with
import json
data = open('data.json', 'r')
json_content = json.load(data)
data.close()


Synchronous Programming
Synchronous programming is an execution model where statements are executed one after another. Each operation must complete before the next one begins, so if one operation blocks (e.g., waiting for a database or API response), the entire program waits.
Example:
data=requests.get(url)# Waits here
print(data.json())# Executes only after the request completes
Asynchronous Programming
Asynchronous programming is an execution model where a task that is waiting for an external operation (such as a network request, database query, or file I/O) temporarily yields control, allowing other tasks to execute. Once the waiting operation completes, execution resumes from where it paused.
Example:
data= await fetch_data()# Yields control while waiting
print(data)
While fetch_data() is waiting, the event loop can execute another coroutine instead of leaving the CPU idle.
One-line interview definitions
Synchronous: Tasks execute sequentially, and each task must finish before the next one starts.
Asynchronous: Tasks can pause while waiting for I/O, allowing other tasks to run in the meantime, improving resource utilisation without blocking the thread.
async and await 
Python keywords used to write asynchronous code. Instead of creating multiple threads, asynchronous programming allows a single thread to handle multiple I/O-bound tasks by switching between them whenever one task is waiting.
Example:
import asyncio 
async def fetch():
	print("Fetching...")
	await asyncio.sleep(2)
	print("Done")
	asyncio.run(fetch())
What does await do?
When Python encounters:
await some_task()
it means:
"Pause this coroutine until the task completes, and let the event loop run another coroutine."
The thread is not blocked.
The event loop is the core of asyncio. It continuously monitors asynchronous tasks and executes whichever coroutine is ready to make progress.
asyncio.gather() schedules multiple coroutines concurrently and waits until all of them complete, making it useful when independent I/O operations can run at the same time.


Suppose:
asyncdeffetch_user():
    ...asyncdeffetch_orders():
    ...asyncdeffetch_products():
    ...
Sequential:
awaitfetch_user()awaitfetch_orders()awaitfetch_products()
Time:
2s + 2s + 2s = 6s
Using gather():
awaitasyncio.gather(fetch_user(),fetch_orders(),fetch_products()
)
All start together.
Start

↓

User

Orders

Products

↓

Finish
Time:
≈2 seconds
API Retry:
You can implement retry logic for failed API calls using a while loop with a counter, or more elegantly, using a dedicated library like tenacity or by building on the requests library's functionality.


import time
import requests

def fetch_with_retry(url, max_retries=3):
    retries = 0
    while retries < max_retries:
        try:
            response = requests.get(url, timeout=5)
            response.raise_for_status()  # Check for HTTP errors
            print("Request successful!")
            return response.json()
        except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
            retries += 1
            print(f"Request failed: {e}. Retrying... (Attempt {retries}/{max_retries})")
            time.sleep(2 ** retries)  # Exponential backoff
    print(f"Max retries ({max_retries}) exceeded. Request failed.")
    return None

# Example usage with a URL that will fail
# Example URL to simulate a failure (e.g., a server not found)
# You can replace this with a real URL to test success cases.
fail_url = "https://httpbin.org/status/503"
data = fetch_with_retry(fail_url)
print(data)
REST, which stands for Representational State Transfer, is an architectural style for designing networked applications. It's not a protocol or a standard but a set of principles that guide the creation of web services. A key idea behind REST is that a client can interact with a server by making simple HTTP requests to access and manipulate resources.
REST Constraints
To be considered "RESTful," a system must adhere to several constraints. These constraints are what make REST services scalable, reliable, and easy to use.
1. Statelessness
Every request from the client to the server must contain all the information needed to understand the request. The server should not store any information about the client's state between requests. Each request is independent and can be handled by any server in the system, which simplifies server design, improves reliability, and allows for greater scalability.
2. Uniform Interface
This is the most critical constraint. It simplifies the overall system architecture by ensuring that all components interact with each other in a standardized way. The uniform interface consists of four sub-constraints:
Identification of resources: Resources are identified by a unique URI (Uniform Resource Identifier).
Manipulation of resources through representations: A client can modify a resource's state by sending a representation (e.g., a JSON or XML document) of that resource to the server.
Self-descriptive messages: Each message includes enough information to describe how to process it. This often involves using standard HTTP methods like GET, POST, PUT, and DELETE.
Hypermedia as the Engine of Application State (HATEOAS): The client interacts with the application solely through hypermedia links provided by the server. The server's response should include links that the client can follow to discover available actions and resources.
3. Cacheable
Clients and intermediaries can cache responses to improve performance and network efficiency. The server must explicitly label its responses as cacheable or non-cacheable. This helps reduce the number of requests to the server, improving scalability. For example, a response to a GET request for a static resource, like an image, can be cached.
HTTP response codes are three-digit status codes returned by a server to a client after an HTTP request is made. They indicate whether a specific request has been successfully completed, providing a quick way for the client to understand the outcome. These codes are grouped into five classes based on their first digit:
1xx (Informational): The request was received and is continuing.
2xx (Success): The request was successfully received, understood, and accepted.
3xx (Redirection): Further action needs to be taken to complete the request.
4xx (Client Error): The request contains a syntax error or cannot be fulfilled.
5xx (Server Error): The server failed to fulfill an apparently valid request.
Common Response Code Examples
Success (2xx)
200 OK: The request succeeded. This is the most common success code.
201 Created: The request has been fulfilled, and a new resource has been created as a result. This is typically sent after a POST request.
Client Errors (4xx)
400 Bad Request: The server could not understand the request due to invalid syntax. This is often caused by malformed data in the request body.
401 Unauthorized: The request lacks valid authentication credentials for the target resource. This means the client needs to log in or provide an API key.
403 Forbidden: The server understood the request, but it refuses to authorize it. Unlike 401, this indicates that authentication was provided but is not sufficient to access the resource.
404 Not Found: The server cannot find the requested resource. The URL is likely incorrect.
Server Errors (5xx)
500 Internal Server Error: The server encountered an unexpected condition that prevented it from fulfilling the request. This is a generic "catch-all" server-side error.
API with flask 
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
from flask_jwt_extended import create_access_token, get_jwt_identity, jwt_required, JWTManager

app = Flask(__name__)
app.config['JWT_SECRET_KEY'] ="a-string-secret-at-least-256-bits-long"
jwt = JWTManager()
jwt.init_app(app)

@app.route("/")
def Home():
    return {"message" :"Hi"}


@app.route("/login",methods = ["POST"])
def Login():
    username = request.json.get("username")
    password = request.json.get("password")
    if username == "abc" and password =="abc":
        token = create_access_token(identity=username)
        return jsonify({"token" :token}), 200
    else:
        return jsonify({"message":"wrong password"}), 401

@app.route("/details", methods = ["GET"])
@jwt_required()
def get_details():
    current_user = get_jwt_identity()
    return jsonify({"message" :f"hi {current_user}"})


if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run()

🐍 Python Core & Internals
How does Python's GIL work, and when does it actually matter?
The Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) is a mutex (mutual-exclusion lock) used in the CPython interpreter. It ensures that only one thread executes Python bytecode at a time
This means:
Python supports multithreading,
BUT threads cannot run CPU-bound code in parallel inside CPython.
GIL ensures that only one thread executes Python bytecode at a time
Multithreading, async, and multiprocessing are concurrency models in Python. 
Multithreading uses multiple threads but is limited by the GIL for CPU-bound tasks. 
Async uses a single-threaded event loop to handle many I/O-bound tasks with minimal overhead efficiently. 
Multiprocessing uses separate processes to achieve true parallelism, making it suitable for CPU-intensive workloads
