1. Thread
A thread is the smallest unit of execution within a process. Multiple threads in the same process share the process's memory (heap, global variables, files, sockets), but each thread has its own call stack and execution state.
Key points
Lives inside a process
Shares memory with other threads
Lightweight to create
Best for concurrent I/O operations
2. Multithreading
Multithreading is a concurrency technique where multiple threads execute within the same process to perform multiple tasks concurrently. Since threads share memory, they can communicate efficiently, making multithreading suitable for I/O-bound workloads.
Example
ThreadA->APICall
ThreadB->ReadFile
ThreadC->DatabaseQuery
While one thread waits for I/O, another can execute.
3. Process
A process is an independent running instance of a program with its own memory space, resources, and at least one thread. Processes are isolated from each other and cannot directly access each other's memory.
Each process has
Own memory
Own Python interpreter
Own GIL (in CPython)
Own resources
4. Multiprocessing
Multiprocessing is a parallel execution technique where multiple independent processes execute simultaneously. Since each process has its own Python interpreter and GIL, multiprocessing can fully utilise multiple CPU cores for CPU-intensive tasks.
Example
Core 1 → Process A
Core 2 → Process B
Core 3 → Process C
Core 4 → Process D
5. Global Interpreter Lock (GIL)
The Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) is a mutex in CPython that allows only one thread to execute Python bytecode at a time within a single process. It simplifies memory management and ensures thread-safe manipulation of Python objects.
Important
The GIL does not prevent multiple threads from existing.
It only prevents multiple threads from executing Python bytecode simultaneously.
6. How GIL Works
Suppose:
t1.start()t2.start()
Internally:
Thread A acquires GIL
↓

Executes Python code

↓

Releases GIL

↓

Thread B acquires GIL

↓

Executes

↓

Releases GIL
Only one thread holds the GIL at a time.
7. Why does the GIL exist?
Python primarily uses reference counting for memory management.
If two threads modified Python objects simultaneously, reference counts could become inconsistent.
The GIL protects Python's internal object management by ensuring only one thread executes Python bytecode at a time.
8. Does GIL make multithreading useless?
No.
The GIL mainly affects CPU-bound Python code.
When a thread performs blocking I/O (network, file, database), it releases the GIL, allowing another thread to execute.
That's why multithreading is still highly effective for I/O-bound applications.
9. CPU-bound Task
A CPU-bound task spends most of its time performing computations and actively using the CPU.
Examples
Mathematical calculations
Image processing (pure Python)
Data transformations
Compression
Cryptography
For these tasks:
Use multiprocessing.
10. I/O-bound Task
An I/O-bound task spends most of its time waiting for external resources rather than performing CPU computations.
Examples
API requests
Database queries
Reading files
Writing files
Downloading data
For these tasks:
Use multithreading or asyncio.
11. Why is Multithreading good for I/O?
Suppose:
requests.get(url)
Timeline:
Thread A

Send request

↓

Waiting...

↓

Waiting...

↓

Response
While Thread A is waiting, it releases the GIL.
The CPU immediately runs another thread.
This keeps the CPU productive instead of idle.
12. Why is Multiprocessing good for CPU work?
Each process has:
Python Interpreter

↓

Own GIL
Therefore:
Core 1 → Process A

Core 2 → Process B
Both processes execute Python code simultaneously on different CPU cores.
This achieves true parallelism.
13. Thread vs Process
14. Multithreading vs Multiprocessing
A thread is the smallest unit of execution inside a process. Multiple threads share the same memory, making communication efficient. In CPython, however, the Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) allows only one thread to execute Python bytecode at a time, which limits multithreading for CPU-bound tasks. When a thread performs blocking I/O, it releases the GIL, allowing other threads to run, making multithreading ideal for API calls, file operations, and database queries. For CPU-intensive work, multiprocessing is preferred because each process has its own Python interpreter and GIL, enabling true parallel execution across multiple CPU cores

Built a pipeline to push the Vite app to NetStorage
Contributed to upgrading the React version from 14 to Vite
Created an Azure pipeline 
The pipeline is responsible for building the React Vite application and publishing the files as a pipeline artifact
This Azure DevOps pipeline is responsible for building the React Vite application and publishing the required files as pipeline artifacts.
When pipeline is triggered, it runs on a Microsoft-hosted Ubuntu build agent using the ubuntu-latest virtual machine image.
The NodeTool@0 task installs Node.js version 22.x on the build agent and adds the Node.js and npm executables to the system path.
The NpmAuthenticate@0 task authenticates the pipeline against the private npm registry configured in the project’s .npmrc file. It uses the private shared components Azure DevOps service connection so that private packages, such as shared React components, can be downloaded during dependency installation.
The pipeline then runs:
npm install --no-package-lock
This installs the dependencies defined in package.json. The --no-package-lock option prevents npm from creating or updating the package-lock.json file on the build agent.
Next, the pipeline runs:
npm run build
This executes the build script defined in package.json
The TypeScript compiler validates and compiles the TypeScript code, and Vite creates an optimized production build. The generated HTML, JavaScript, CSS, images, and other static assets are written to the dist folder.
The pipeline then publishes the dist folder as an Azure DevOps pipeline artifact named dist. This artifact contains the production-ready web application that can be downloaded and deployed by a release pipeline or deployment stage.
The tools folder is published separately as another artifact named tools. This folder is not generated by Vite and is not included inside the dist build. It already exists in the source repository and normally contains deployment scripts, such as an Akamai upload script, shell scripts, configuration utilities, or other files required during deployment.
At the end of the build, two separate artifacts are available:
dist
└── Production-ready React application

tools
└── Deployment scripts and supporting utilities
The overall pipeline flow is:
Push code to main
        ↓
Start Ubuntu build agent
        ↓
Install Node.js 22
        ↓
Authenticate private npm feed
        ↓
Install npm dependencies
        ↓
Compile TypeScript and build Vite application
        ↓
Generate dist folder
        ↓
Publish dist and tools as separate artifacts
This pipeline only builds and publishes the application artifacts. It does not deploy the application because no deployment task or deployment stage is included in this YAML.
In the release, we basically run files in the tools folder, i.e we create a config file and shell scripts that upload the files to Akamai. 
So the shell scripts have an SSH key (from the pipeline → library → secure files) to connect to Akamai using the hostname, CP code, and subfolder, if any, and upload the file to NetStorage. With SSH, we only update the file that has some changes; not all files get uploaded. 

Release Pipeline
The release pipeline handles deployment.
Steps
Downloads both artifacts:
dist
tools
Creates the deployment configuration.
The configuration contains information like:
Akamai hostname
CP Code
Target environment
Optional subfolder
Retrieves the SSH private key from:
Azure DevOps
→ Library
→ Secure Files
The key is never stored in source control.
Executes the deployment shell script.
Deployment to Akamai NetStorage
The shell script:
Uses SSH authentication.
Connects securely to Akamai NetStorage.
Uploads the frontend build.
Instead of copying everything every release, it uses rsync.
Benefits of rsync:
Uploads only modified files.
Faster deployments.
Lower bandwidth usage.
Less deployment time.
Cache Invalidation
After deployment:
CDN cache invalidation API is triggered.
Edge servers fetch the latest frontend assets.
Prevents users from receiving stale cached files.
Rollback Strategy
Each release is associated with a build artifact.
If a deployment fails:
Select a previous successful build.
Redeploy its published artifacts.
Restore the previous application version.
Multi-Environment Deployment
Different environments use different Akamai CP Codes.
Example:
Development
     ↓
CP Code A

QA
     ↓
CP Code B

Production
     ↓
CP Code C
The deployment script reads the environment configuration and uploads files to the corresponding NetStorage location.

APIs return a header called: Retry-After:

retry_after = response.headers.get("Retry-After", 60)

time.sleep(int(retry_after))

Azure AD B2C expiry, SendGrid quota monitoring 
API key is stored in the pipeline → library and explicitly mentioned in the pipeline; a cron job is set up
Multiple applications used Azure AD B2C for user authentication using OAuth.
Applications authenticate using:
Client ID
Client Secret
Client secrets have an expiration date.
If a secret expires and is not renewed before expiry:
User authentication fails.
Applications cannot log users in.
This can lead to production outages.
Several applications relied on Azure AD B2C for OAuth-based authentication, and expired client secrets could cause production login failures. I developed a Python automation that ran on a scheduled cron job, authenticated with the Microsoft Graph API, retrieved application credentials, identified secrets nearing expiration, and automatically notified the respective product owners using SendGrid. The same automation also generated Azure AD B2C registration and sign-in reports for the marketing team. Secrets such as the SendGrid API key were managed securely through the Azure DevOps Library, keeping credentials out of the source code

Things to study 
Thundering Herd Problem.
The thundering herd problem is a computing issue where a large group of processes, threads, or network clients wake up or send requests at the same time to grab a shared resource, overwhelming the system because only one can succeed or the backend cannot handle the sudden load
How to handle large data
To handle large data efficiently during the reading phase of automation, you must avoid loading the entire dataset into memory at once. Instead, you should rely on strategies like chunking, selective column filtering, and leveraging database-driven queries
How to handle rate limiting
How JWT works
The flow (this maps directly to your Azure AD B2C / Graph API work):
Client authenticates (username/password, or client ID + secret in your case)
Auth server (Azure AD B2C) validates credentials and issues a JWT, signed with its private key
Client stores the token (usually sends it as Authorization: Bearer <token> on subsequent requests)
Resource server (e.g., Microsoft Graph API) receives the request, verifies the signature using the auth server's public key — it does NOT need to call back to the auth server (this is the big advantage over session-based auth: it's stateless)
If the signature is valid and exp hasn't passed, the request is authorized
What's Akamai NetStorage, artifact
An artifact in Azure DevOps is a collection of compiled files, packages, or binaries generated during a build pipeline run. Its primary purpose is to act as a deployable unit of software that can be passed securely down to subsequent test stages or target environments in a continuous deployment (CD) workflow.
Akamai NetStorage is a secure, cloud-based object storage service optimized as a content delivery network (CDN) origin. It automatically replicates digital assets globally to ensure high availability for end-users. It is accessed via dedicated upload accounts and supports standard protocols like SFTP, SCP, and RSYNC

Topic 1: Data Loaders in LlamaIndex
1. What are Data Loaders?
Data loaders are used to load external data into LlamaIndex.
They convert:
PDFs
websites
Wikipedia pages
text files
databases
APIs
into LlamaIndex Document objects.
2. Why are Data Loaders Needed?
LLMs cannot directly understand:
PDFs
web pages
databases
So loaders:
read the raw data
extract text
create structured Document objects
3. Basic Flow
External Data
     ↓
Data Loader
     ↓
Document Objects
4. Common Loaders
5. SimpleDirectoryReader
Most commonly used loader.
Used for:
PDFs
TXT files
DOCX files
local folders
Example:
fromllama_index.coreimportSimpleDirectoryReader

documents=SimpleDirectoryReader(
"data"
).load_data()
OR:
documents=SimpleDirectoryReader(
input_files=["data/file.pdf"]
).load_data()
6. Output of Loader
Loaders return:
List[Document]
Example:
print(type(documents))
print(type(documents[0]))
Output:
list
Document
7. Structure of a Document
A Document contains:
Document(
text="...",
metadata={}
)
Main components:
8. Metadata
Metadata stores information like:
source
title
filename
page number
Example:
documents[0].metadata
Possible output:
{
"title":"Artificial Intelligence"
}
9. WikipediaReader
Used to load Wikipedia pages.
Example:
fromllama_index.readers.wikipediaimportWikipediaReader

reader=WikipediaReader()

documents=reader.load_data(
pages=[
"Artificial Intelligence",
"Transformer (deep learning)"
    ]
)
10. Important Observation
Some readers automatically include metadata.
Some do not.
Example:
documents[0].metadata
may return:
{}
depending on:
reader
LlamaIndex version
11. Manual Metadata Addition
You can manually add metadata:
fordoc,pageinzip(documents,pages):
doc.metadata= {
"source":"wikipedia",
"title":page
    }
12. PDF Loading Issues
Sometimes PDFs load as binary:
%PDF-1.5 ...
This happens when:
PDF parser missing
incorrect loader used
Fix:
pip install pypdf
or explicitly use:
fromllama_index.readers.fileimportPDFReader
Topic 2: Node Parsers / Chunking in LlamaIndex
1. What are Node Parsers?
Node parsers split large documents into smaller chunks called nodes.
They convert:
Document
   ↓
Smaller Chunks (Nodes)
2. Why Do We Need Chunking?
LLMs cannot efficiently process:
huge PDFs
long articles
entire books
Problems without chunking:
context window overflow
poor retrieval
slower inference
irrelevant information
So we split documents into manageable chunks.
3. Basic Flow
Documents
    ↓
Node Parser
    ↓
Nodes / Chunks
4. What is a Node?
A node is a chunk of text with extra information.
Example structure:
{
"text":"...",
"metadata": {...},
"relationships": {...}
}
5. Most Common Node Parser → SentenceSplitter
Example:
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportSentenceSplitter

splitter=SentenceSplitter(
chunk_size=512,
chunk_overlap=50
)

nodes=splitter.get_nodes_from_documents(documents)
6. Important Parameters
chunk_size
Controls maximum chunk length.
Example:
chunk_size=512
means:
approximately 512-token chunks
chunk_overlap
Controls overlap between neighboring chunks.
Example:
chunk_overlap=50
means:
50 tokens repeated between chunks
Purpose:
preserve context continuity
7. Why Overlap is Important
Without overlap:
Chunk 1 ends mid-topic
Chunk 2 starts mid-topic
Context gets lost.
Overlap helps preserve meaning across boundaries.
8. Example Chunking
Original text:
Transformers use attention mechanisms...
After chunking:
Node 1
Node 2
Node 3
Each node contains a portion of text.
9. Accessing Node Text
print(nodes[0].text)
10. Node Metadata
Nodes can contain metadata inherited from documents.
Example:
print(nodes[0].metadata)
11. Metadata Propagation
Sometimes metadata is not copied automatically.
Use:
SentenceSplitter(
include_metadata=True
)
to preserve metadata.
12. Relationships Between Nodes
LlamaIndex automatically creates relationships.
Examples:
PREVIOUS
NEXT
SOURCE
These relationships connect chunks together.
13. Checking Relationships
print(nodes[0].relationships)
Possible output:
{
NodeRelationship.NEXT: ...
}
14. Important Observation
Relationships are usually created:
within the same document only
NOT across different documents.
Example:
AI document chunks
Transformer document chunks
remain separate.
15. Different Types of Node Parsers
16. SentenceSplitter Characteristics
17. Typical RAG Flow
Documents
   ↓
Node Parser
   ↓
Nodes
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector Store
Topic 3: Semantic Chunking & SemanticSplitterNodeParser in LlamaIndex
1. What is Semantic Chunking?
Semantic chunking splits text based on meaning, not fixed size.
Instead of:
Split every 512 tokens
it does:
Split when topic/meaning changes
2. Why Semantic Chunking?
Problem with fixed chunking:
Chunk may cut ideas in the middle
Example:
Chunk 1:
"Transformers use attention..."

Chunk 2:
"...mechanisms to improve NLP"
Meaning gets broken.
Semantic chunking tries to keep related ideas together.
3. Core Idea
Semantic chunking uses:
embeddings
cosine similarity
to detect topic boundaries.
4. High-Level Flow
Sentences
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Cosine Similarity
   ↓
Topic Boundary Detection
   ↓
Nodes
5. Cosine Similarity
Semantic similarity is measured using cosine similarity.
cos⁡(θ)=A⋅B∥A∥∥B∥\cos(\theta)=\frac{A\cdot B}{\|A\|\|B\|}cos(θ)=∥A∥∥B∥A⋅B
Meaning:
high similarity → same topic
low similarity → new topic/chunk
6. Example
Input:
Sentence 1 → Transformers
Sentence 2 → Attention Mechanisms
Sentence 3 → NLP
Sentence 4 → Cooking recipes
Result:
Chunk 1:
Transformers + Attention + NLP

Chunk 2:
Cooking recipes
because topic changed.
7. Important Difference from SentenceSplitter
8. SemanticSplitterNodeParser
Example:
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportSemanticSplitterNodeParser
fromllama_index.embeddings.huggingfaceimportHuggingFaceEmbedding

embed_model=HuggingFaceEmbedding(
model_name="BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5"
)

splitter=SemanticSplitterNodeParser(
buffer_size=1,
breakpoint_percentile_threshold=95,
embed_model=embed_model
)

nodes=splitter.get_nodes_from_documents(documents)
9. Important Parameters
embed_model
Embedding model used for semantic comparison.
Example:
embed_model=embed_model
breakpoint_percentile_threshold
Controls splitting sensitivity.
Example:
breakpoint_percentile_threshold=95
Higher value:
fewer splits
larger chunks
Lower value:
more splits
smaller chunks
buffer_size
Controls nearby sentence context.
Example:
buffer_size=1
helps stabilize chunking decisions.
10. Important Understanding
In semantic chunking:
❌ You do NOT directly specify:
chunk_size=512
because chunk sizes are decided dynamically by semantic boundaries.
11. Variable-Sized Nodes
Semantic chunking creates:
Node 1 → 300 tokens
Node 2 → 700 tokens
Node 3 → 150 tokens
depending on semantic coherence.
12. Embeddings are Mandatory
Because semantic splitting depends on semantic similarity.
Without embeddings:
no similarity computation
no semantic boundary detection
13. Two Different Uses of Embeddings
A. During Semantic Chunking
Used to:
detect topic changes
B. During Vector Indexing
Used to:
create retrieval vectors
These are separate steps.
14. Important Clarification
Semantic chunking:
uses embeddings
BUT:
does NOT automatically store vectors in vector DB
It only creates semantically meaningful nodes.
15. Node Relationships
Even with semantic chunking:
relationships are usually sequential
Example:
Node A → NEXT → Node B
NOT automatically:
semantic similarity graph
Topic 4: Hierarchical Node Parsing & Parent-Child Relationships in LlamaIndex
1. What is Hierarchical Chunking?
Hierarchical chunking creates chunks at multiple levels.
Instead of:
Only one chunk size
it creates:
Large chunks
    ↓
Smaller chunks
forming a hierarchy/tree structure.
2. Why Hierarchical Chunking?
In RAG:
small chunks improve retrieval precision
large chunks improve context understanding
Hierarchical chunking combines both.
3. High-Level Structure
Document
   ↓
Parent Node
   ↓
Child Nodes
Example:
Parent Node → 2000 tokens

Child Node 1 → 512
Child Node 2 → 512
Child Node 3 → 512
4. Relationships Created
Hierarchical parsing creates:
PARENT
CHILD
relationships automatically.
Example:
Parent ↔ Child
5. Why Parent-Child Relationships Matter
Suppose retrieval finds:
Small chunk about transformers
Small chunk is precise,
but LLM may need broader context.
System can:
retrieve child chunk
fetch parent chunk automatically
This improves answer quality.
6. HierarchicalNodeParser
Example:
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportHierarchicalNodeParser

parser=HierarchicalNodeParser.from_defaults(
chunk_sizes=[2048,512,128]
)

nodes=parser.get_nodes_from_documents(documents)
7. What Happens Internally
Level-wise chunking:
Level 1 → 2048-token chunks
Level 2 → 512-token chunks
Level 3 → 128-token chunks
8. Hierarchy Structure
Example:
Document
   ↓
Parent Node (2048)
   ↓
Child Nodes (512)
   ↓
Sub-child Nodes (128)
9. Relationships in Nodes
Each node can contain:
node.relationships
Possible relationships:
PARENT
CHILD
NEXT
PREVIOUS
SOURCE
10. Inspecting Relationships
Example:
print(nodes[0].relationships)
11. Difference from SentenceSplitter
12. Retrieval Flow
Hierarchical retrieval:
Query
   ↓
Retrieve Small Child Chunk
   ↓
Expand Using Parent Chunk
   ↓
Send Rich Context to LLM
13. Benefits
✅ precise retrieval
✅ larger contextual understanding
✅ better long-document QA
✅ improved answer quality
✅ scalable retrieval
14. Typical Use Cases
Very useful for:
research papers
enterprise docs
legal documents
books
long PDFs
technical documentation
15. Chunk Size Strategy
Typical configuration:
chunk_sizes=[2048,512,128]
Meaning:
large context chunks
medium retrieval chunks
fine-grained chunks
16. Important Concept
Hierarchy parsing creates a:
Tree-like retrieval structure
instead of flat chunks.
17. Hierarchical RAG Architecture
Documents
   ↓
Hierarchical Parser
   ↓
Parent + Child Nodes
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector Store
   ↓
Hierarchical Retrieval
   ↓
LLM
18. Comparison with Semantic Chunking
19. Can Both Be Combined?
Yes.
Advanced RAG systems often combine:
semantic chunking
hierarchical retrieval
for better retrieval quality.
20. Key Takeaway
Hierarchical parsing helps balance:
Precision + Context
by creating:
small retrievable chunks
larger contextual parent chunks
Topic 5: Tokenization & tiktoken in LLM/RAG Systems
1. What is Tokenization?
LLMs do NOT understand raw text directly.
They first convert text into smaller units called tokens.
Example:
"Artificial Intelligence is amazing"
becomes:
[9472,16895,374,8056]
These numbers are tokens.
2. What is a Token?
A token can be:
word
subword
punctuation
character piece
Example:
3. Why Tokenization is Important
LLMs process:
tokens
NOT:
words
characters
Everything in LLMs depends on tokens.
4. What is tiktoken?
tiktoken is a tokenizer library.
Used for:
token counting
tokenization
estimating context size
estimating API cost
5. Installing tiktoken
pip install tiktoken
6. Basic Example
importtiktoken

encoding=tiktoken.get_encoding("cl100k_base")

text="Hello how are you"

tokens=encoding.encode(text)

print(tokens)
print(len(tokens))
7. Example Output
[9906,1268,527,499]
4
8. Important Understanding
❌ Words ≠ Tokens
Example:
9. Why Tokens Matter in RAG
Tokenization is important for:
chunk sizing
context window limits
retrieval optimization
API costs
performance optimization
10. Context Window
Every model has a maximum token limit.
Example:
If prompt exceeds limit:
Context length exceeded
11. Token Counting for Documents
Example:
text=documents[0].text

token_count=len(
encoding.encode(text)
)

print(token_count)
12. Token Counting for Nodes
Example:
fornodeinnodes:
count=len(
encoding.encode(node.text)
    )

print(count)
13. Why Chunk Size Uses Tokens
Chunking is usually based on tokens because:
LLM limits are token-based
embeddings operate on tokens internally
Example:
chunk_size=512
means:
~512 tokens
NOT:
512 words
14. Important Distinction
15. Tokenization vs Embeddings
Tokenization
Text
  ↓
Token IDs
Example:
[101,2057,2024]
Embeddings
Text
  ↓
Vector Representation
Example:
[0.22,-0.88,0.44, ...]
16. tiktoken vs HuggingFace Tokenizers
tiktoken
Good for:
OpenAI-style token counting
fast estimation
HuggingFace Tokenizers
Better for:
Llama models
exact tokenizer matching
Example:
fromtransformersimportAutoTokenizer
17. For Groq / Llama Models
You used:
model="llama-3.3-70b-versatile"
For accurate token counting:
fromtransformersimportAutoTokenizer

tokenizer=AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(
"meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct"
)
18. Why Token Counting Helps in RAG
Helps optimize:
chunk sizes
retrieval size
prompt construction
latency
cost
19. Typical Token-Aware RAG Flow
Documents
   ↓
Chunking
   ↓
Token Counting
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector Store
Topic 6: Embeddings & Vector Representations in RAG Systems
1. What are Embeddings?
Embeddings convert text into numerical vectors that capture semantic meaning.
Example:
"Artificial Intelligence"
becomes:
[0.12,-0.88,0.44, ...]
This vector represents the meaning of the text.
2. Why Embeddings are Needed
LLMs and vector databases cannot compare meaning directly from raw text.
Embeddings help systems understand:
Semantic similarity
between texts.
3. Core Idea
Similar meanings → similar vectors.
Example:
4. Embedding Flow
Text
  ↓
Embedding Model
  ↓
Vector Representation
5. Example Embedding Vector
[0.22,-0.91,0.44,0.13, ...]
Usually:
hundreds of dimensions
sometimes thousands
6. What Embeddings Capture
Embeddings capture:
meaning
topic
context
semantic relationships
NOT exact keywords.
7. Example
Texts:
"Transformers improve NLP"

"Attention mechanisms help language models"
may produce very similar embeddings because meanings are related.
8. Cosine Similarity
Similarity between embeddings is commonly measured using cosine similarity.
cos⁡(θ)=A⋅B∥A∥∥B∥
Meaning:
closer vectors → higher similarity
distant vectors → lower similarity
9. Why Embeddings are Important in RAG
Embeddings enable:
semantic search
vector retrieval
similarity search
semantic chunking
10. Embedding Workflow in RAG
Chunks
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector DB
   ↓
Similarity Retrieval
11. Creating Embeddings in LlamaIndex
Example:
fromllama_index.embeddings.huggingfaceimportHuggingFaceEmbedding

embed_model=HuggingFaceEmbedding(
model_name="BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5"
)
12. Generating Embeddings
Example:
embedding=embed_model.get_text_embedding(
"Artificial Intelligence"
)

print(len(embedding))
print(embedding[:5])
13. Example Output
384
[0.12,-0.88,0.44, ...]
Meaning:
vector dimension = 384
14. Popular Free Embedding Models
15. Embeddings vs Tokens
16. Important Clarification
Tokenization:
Text → Token IDs
Embeddings:
Text → Meaning Vector
They are completely different concepts.
17. Semantic Search Using Embeddings
Suppose query:
"What are transformers?"
Flow:
Query
   ↓
Embedding
   ↓
Compare with stored vectors
   ↓
Retrieve most similar chunks
18. Vector Databases
Embeddings are stored inside:
Chroma
Pinecone
Weaviate
FAISS
Qdrant
These databases enable fast similarity search.
19. Embeddings in Semantic Chunking
Embeddings are also used for:
semantic splitting
cosine similarity calculations
before vector storage.
20. Two Different Uses of Embeddings
A. Semantic Chunking
Used to:
detect topic changes
B. Vector Retrieval
Used to:
retrieve relevant chunks
21. Important Understanding
Nodes/chunks themselves are NOT vectors.
Example:
nodes[0].text
is still plain text.
Vectors are created later during embedding generation.
22. Creating Vector Index
Example:
index=VectorStoreIndex(
nodes,
embed_model=embed_model
)
This step:
creates embeddings
converts chunks into vectors
stores vectors in vector store
23. Similarity Retrieval
Vector retrieval works because:
semantically similar vectors are close together
Example:
AI ↔ Machine Learning
retrieve near each other.
24. Key Takeaway
Embeddings convert:
Meaning
   ↓
Mathematical Vector
This is the foundation of:
semantic search
RAG retrieval
vector databases
Final Mental Model
Text Chunk
    ↓
Embedding Model
    ↓
Vector Representation
    ↓
Vector DB
    ↓
Similarity Search
Topic 7: Vector Stores & ChromaDB in RAG Systems
1. What is a Vector Store?
A vector store (vector database) stores:
embeddings
vectors
metadata
and enables:
fast similarity search
2. Why Vector Stores are Needed
After embeddings are created:
Text Chunk
   ↓
Embedding Vector
we need a system to:
store vectors
search similar vectors efficiently
That is the job of a vector store.
3. Core Idea
Instead of keyword matching:
"transformers"
vector stores perform:
semantic similarity search
using embeddings.
4. Basic RAG Flow
Chunks
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector Store
   ↓
Similarity Search
   ↓
Retriever
5. Popular Vector Databases
6. What is ChromaDB?
Chroma is:
lightweight
local
beginner-friendly
persistent
Very popular for learning RAG.
7. Installing Chroma
pip install chromadb
pip install llama-index-vector-stores-chroma
8. Creating Chroma Client
Example:
importchromadb

db=chromadb.PersistentClient(
path="./chroma_db"
)
9. Persistent Storage
Important advantage:
Vectors are stored on disk:
./chroma_db
So after restarting notebook:
vectors still exist
embeddings don’t need regeneration
10. Creating Collection
A collection is like a table/container.
Example:
collection=db.get_or_create_collection(
"rag_collection"
)
11. ChromaVectorStore in LlamaIndex
Example:
fromllama_index.vector_stores.chromaimportChromaVectorStore

vector_store=ChromaVectorStore(
chroma_collection=collection
)
12. Full Vector Storage Flow
index=VectorStoreIndex(
nodes,
embed_model=embed_model,
vector_store=vector_store
)
This step:
generates embeddings
converts nodes into vectors
stores vectors in ChromaDB
13. Important Clarification
Before indexing:
nodes[0].text
is still plain text.
After:
VectorStoreIndex(...)
embeddings are created and stored.
14. What Gets Stored?
Typically:
vector embeddings
chunk text
metadata
node IDs
15. Similarity Search
Suppose query:
"What are transformers?"
Flow:
Query
   ↓
Embedding
   ↓
Compare with stored vectors
   ↓
Retrieve nearest vectors
16. Why Vector Search is Powerful
Vector search can retrieve:
"Attention mechanisms improve NLP"
even if query was:
"What are transformers?"
because meanings are similar.
17. Difference from Keyword Search
18. Querying the Index
Example:
query_engine=index.as_query_engine()

response=query_engine.query(
"What are transformers?"
)

print(response)
19. In-Memory vs Persistent Vector Store
In-Memory
VectorStoreIndex(nodes)
Vectors stay only in RAM.
After restart:
vectors disappear
Persistent Store
Using Chroma:
PersistentClient(...)
Vectors remain stored on disk.
Topic 8: Index Types in LlamaIndex
1. What is an Index?
An index organizes data for:
retrieval
querying
searching
Think of it as:
“How should documents be stored and retrieved?”
Different indexes optimize different retrieval strategies.
2. Why Different Index Types Exist
Different applications need different retrieval behavior.
Examples:
semantic QA
summarization
keyword search
hierarchical retrieval
One index type cannot optimize all of them.
3. Main Index Types
4. VectorStoreIndex
Most important and most commonly used index.
Purpose
Used for:
semantic search
RAG
question answering
Flow
Chunks
   ↓
Embeddings
   ↓
Vector Store
   ↓
Similarity Search
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportVectorStoreIndex

index=VectorStoreIndex.from_documents(
documents
)
Retrieval Process
User query:
"What are transformers?"
System:
converts query → embedding
compares vectors
retrieves similar chunks
Best For
✅ RAG
✅ semantic search
✅ chatbots
✅ retrieval systems
5. SummaryIndex
Designed for summarization-oriented retrieval.
Core Idea
Instead of embeddings:
builds summaries of chunks/documents
Flow
Chunks
   ↓
Summaries
   ↓
Combined summaries
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportSummaryIndex

index=SummaryIndex.from_documents(
documents
)
Retrieval Style
LLM reads summaries to answer questions.
Best For
✅ document summaries
✅ report generation
✅ overview-style questions
Weakness
❌ not precise retrieval
❌ weaker factual QA
6. KeywordTableIndex
(Previously called keyword index)
Purpose
Keyword-based retrieval.
Flow
Chunks
   ↓
Keyword Extraction
   ↓
Keyword Table
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportKeywordTableIndex

index=KeywordTableIndex.from_documents(
documents
)
Retrieval
Query:
"transformers"
matches:
extracted keywords
exact terms
Best For
✅ exact term matching
✅ technical keywords
✅ structured data
Weakness
❌ poor semantic understanding
7. DocumentSummaryIndex
Advanced summary-based retrieval.
Core Idea
Creates summaries per document.
Example:
Document 1 → Summary
Document 2 → Summary
Document 3 → Summary
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportDocumentSummaryIndex

index=DocumentSummaryIndex.from_documents(
documents
)
Retrieval Flow
find relevant document summaries
retrieve matching documents
answer question
Best For
✅ multi-document collections
✅ enterprise RAG
✅ document routing

Hierarchical retrieval index.
Core Idea
Builds tree structure using summaries.
Flow
Chunks
   ↓
Intermediate Summaries
   ↓
Root Summary
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportTreeIndex

index=TreeIndex.from_documents(
documents
)
Retrieval
Query traverses tree hierarchy:
Root
 ↓

 ↓

Best For
✅ large document collections

✅ structured retrieval

13. Retrieval Philosophy
14. Choosing the Right Index
Use VectorStoreIndex When
semantic QA
RAG
chatbot systems
retrieval-heavy tasks
Use SummaryIndex When
summarization
overviews
report generation
Use KeywordTableIndex When
exact technical terms matter
Use TreeIndex When
huge hierarchical data exists
15. Important Understanding
Indexes do NOT create chunks.
Chunking happens BEFORE indexing.
Pipeline:
Documents
   ↓
Node Parser
   ↓
Nodes
   ↓
Index
Topic 9: Retrievers in LlamaIndex
1. What is a Retriever?
A retriever is responsible for:
Finding relevant nodes/chunks
from an index based on a user query.
Why Retrievers Matter
The retriever decides:
which chunks the LLM sees
what context is sent
retrieval quality
Bad retrieval:
wrong answers
hallucinations
irrelevant context
Good retrieval:
accurate answers
relevant context
better RAG performance
Retriever vs Index
5. Basic Retriever Flow
User Query
   ↓
Retriever
   ↓
Relevant Nodes
6. Vector Index Retriever
Most common retriever.
Uses:
embeddings
cosine similarity
semantic search
Flow
Query
   ↓
Query Embedding
   ↓
Similarity Search
   ↓
Top Matching Nodes
Example
retriever=index.as_retriever()

nodes=retriever.retrieve(
"What are transformers?"
)
Best For
✅ semantic search
✅ QA systems
✅ RAG chatbots
7. Similarity Top-K
Controls:
how many chunks are retrieved
Example:
retriever=index.as_retriever(
similarity_top_k=3
)
Meaning:
retrieve top 3 most similar chunks
8. Summary Retriever
Used with:
SummaryIndex
Instead of:
vector similarity
it uses:
summaries
LLM reasoning
Flow
Query
   ↓
LLM reads summaries
   ↓
Relevant information selected
Example
fromllama_index.coreimportSummaryIndex

index=SummaryIndex.from_documents(
documents
)

retriever=index.as_retriever()
9. SummaryIndex Retriever
The retriever for SummaryIndex works differently from vector retrieval.
Instead of:
comparing embeddings
it:
traverses summaries
synthesizes answers
Best For
✅ summarization
✅ overview questions
✅ report generation
Weakness
❌ not precise semantic retrieval
10. Keyword Retriever
Used with:
KeywordTableIndex
Retrieves chunks using:
keyword matching
Flow
Query
   ↓
Extract Keywords
   ↓
Match Keyword Table
Best For
✅ exact term search
✅ technical keywords
✅ structured docs
11. Tree Retriever
Used with:
TreeIndex
Retrieval happens hierarchically.
Flow
Query
   ↓
Traverse Tree
   ↓
Find Relevant Branch
   ↓
Retrieve Nodes
Best For
✅ huge datasets
✅ hierarchical retrieval
✅ summarization trees
12. Document Summary Retriever
Used with:
DocumentSummaryIndex
Core Idea
First:
identify relevant documents
Then:
retrieve relevant nodes inside them
Flow
Query
   ↓
Compare with Document Summaries
   ↓
Find Relevant Documents
   ↓
Retrieve Relevant Chunks
Best For
✅ enterprise RAG
✅ multi-document systems
✅ document routing
13. Recursive Retriever
Advanced retriever in LlamaIndex.
Uses:
node relationships
to recursively expand retrieval.
Example
Retrieve:
Child Node
Then automatically fetch:
parent nodes
linked nodes
neighboring chunks
Flow
Relevant Node
   ↓
Follow Relationships
   ↓
Expand Context
Best For
✅ hierarchical RAG
✅ graph retrieval
✅ contextual expansion
14. Auto-Merging Retriever
Uses:
neighboring nodes
node relationships
to merge related chunks dynamically.
Flow
Retrieve Small Chunks
   ↓
Merge Nearby Chunks
   ↓
Send Richer Context
Benefit
Improves:
context completeness
coherence
15. Router Retriever
Advanced retriever that chooses:
which retriever/index to use
based on query type.
Example

    ↓
Summary Retriever

Fact-based query
    ↓
Vector Retriever
16. Hybrid Retrieval
Combines:
vector similarity
keyword search
Why Hybrid?

strong semantic understanding
Keyword search:
strong exact matching
Combining both improves retrieval.

Retriever can filter based on metadata.

"source":"wikipedia"
}
Only retrieve:
Wikipedia nodes

24. Key Takeaway
Retrievers decide:
“What context should the LLM see?”
This is one of the most critical parts of every RAG system.
Advanced Retrieval technique
1. Sentence Window Retrieval
Problem with Normal Chunk Retrieval
Suppose retrieved chunk:
"...attention mechanisms improve..."
But maybe:
previous sentence
next sentence
contain important context.
Normal retrieval may miss them.
Core Idea
Retrieve:
one sentence/chunk
BUT send:
neighboring sentences too.
Visual
Sentence 1
Sentence 2
Sentence 3 ← retrieved
Sentence 4
Sentence 5
System sends:
Sentence 1-5
instead of only sentence 3.
Why It Helps
Improves:
coherence
context continuity
answer quality
especially for:
technical docs
research papers
How It Works
Step 1 → Small Precise Chunks
Very small chunks:
sentence-level
tiny nodes
Step 2 → Retrieve Tiny Chunk
Find:
most relevant sentence
Step 3 → Expand Context Window
Retrieve nearby chunks:
previous
next
using node relationships.
Example
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportSentenceWindowNodeParser

node_parser=SentenceWindowNodeParser.from_defaults(
window_size=3,
window_metadata_key="window",
original_text_metadata_key="original_text"
)
Meaning
window_size=3
means:
include nearby surrounding sentences.
Why Sentence Window Retrieval is Powerful
Instead of:
large chunk retrieval
you get:
precise retrieval
rich context
Best of both worlds.
Typical Flow
Tiny Sentence Retrieval
        ↓
Window Expansion
        ↓
Rich Context
2. Auto-Merging Retriever
One of the coolest LlamaIndex features.
Problem
Small chunks improve retrieval precision.
BUT:
tiny chunks lose context.
Core Idea
Retrieve:
multiple nearby small chunks
THEN:
automatically merge them.
Visual
Retrieved:
Chunk A
Chunk B
Chunk C
Auto-merging:
Merged Context Block
Why This Helps
Improves:
coherence
contextual completeness
answer quality
Usually Used With
✅ HierarchicalNodeParser
because:
parent-child relationships exist.
Example Setup
Step 1 → Hierarchical Nodes
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportHierarchicalNodeParser

parser=HierarchicalNodeParser.from_defaults(
chunk_sizes=[2048,512,128]
)

nodes=parser.get_nodes_from_documents(
documents
)
Step 2 → Vector Index
index=VectorStoreIndex(
nodes,
embed_model=embed_model
)
Step 3 → Base Retriever
base_retriever=index.as_retriever(
similarity_top_k=6
)
Step 4 → Auto-Merging Retriever
fromllama_index.core.retrieversimportAutoMergingRetriever
fromllama_index.core.storage.docstoreimportSimpleDocumentStore

docstore=SimpleDocumentStore()
docstore.add_documents(nodes)

auto_merging_retriever=AutoMergingRetriever(
base_retriever,
storage_context=index.storage_context,
verbose=True
)
What Happens Internally
Suppose retrieved:
child chunk 1
child chunk 2
child chunk 3
Retriever notices:
they belong to same parent
Then merges automatically.
Final Result
Instead of fragmented chunks:
Tiny disconnected text
you get:
Larger coherent context
Why Auto-Merging is Great
Balances:
retrieval precision
contextual richness
VERY important in long-document RAG.
3. Auto Retriever
What is AutoRetriever?
An intelligent retriever selector.
It automatically decides:
which retriever/index to use
based on query type.
Example
User asks:
"Summarize AI"
System chooses:
Summary Retriever
User asks:
"What is attention mechanism?"
System chooses:
Vector Retriever
Core Idea

        ↓
Choose Best Retrieval Strategy
Why Useful
Different queries need:
different retrieval methods
Typical Choices
AutoRetriever may select:
vector retrieval
summary retrieval
keyword retrieval
hybrid retrieval
Example Idea

    ↓
Vector Search

Overview Query
    ↓
Summary Search

This becomes:
Router Retriever
Query Router
Agentic Retrieval

     ↓

Comparison
Most Useful in Practice
Response Synthesis
https://developers.llamaindex.ai/python/framework/module_guides/querying/response_synthesizers/
Response Synthesis
After retrieval:
Query
↓
Retriever
↓
Retrieved Chunks
↓
Response Synthesizer
↓
Final Answer
The synthesizer decides:
how retrieved chunks are combined
how the final answer is generated
Types of Response Synthesizers
1. refine
Process:
take first chunk
generate partial answer
refine using next chunk
Flow:
Chunk 1 → Initial Answer
Chunk 2 → Refine Answer
Chunk 3 → Refine Again
Best for:
detailed QA
high accuracy
Weakness:
slower
Example:
query_engine=index.as_query_engine(
response_mode="refine"
)
2. compact
Combines chunks into larger prompt batches.
Best for:
faster responses
fewer LLM calls
Weakness:
may lose detail
response_mode="compact"
3. tree_summarize
Builds hierarchical summaries.
Flow:
Chunks
↓
Partial Summaries
↓
Merged Summary
↓
Final Summary
Best for:
summarization
large docs
response_mode="tree_summarize"
4. accumulate
Generates answers independently from each chunk.
Then combines all answers.
Best for:
extracting all information
Weakness:
repetitive answers
Interview Point
Retriever decides:
“What context to fetch?”
Response Synthesizer decides:
“How to combine retrieved context?”

 retirver evalation metrics
Hit rate:
Did the LLM retrieve at least one relevant document for your query
If it hits, the Hit Rate is 1 (bullseye!), but if it misses entirely, it’s 0
Hit rate doesnt take the position of the retrieved answer so we take MRR
MRR takes position into the actual node in the retrieved set of nodes
response evaluation 
Failthfulness → evaludates wheather LLM response accurately refrets the provides context, coutering the model’s tendedcy to genrate the unfounded anwer
Claim Extraction: The evaluator identifies all atomic facts/claims made in the generated answer.
NLI Check: It performs a Natural Language Inference (NLI) check to see if each claim can be logically inferred directly from the retrieved text.
Scoring: The score represents the ratio of supported claims to the total claims in the response (ranging from \(0\) to \(1\)
Score = Number of supporteed claims from LLM / total number of claims
Relevancy evaluation → Evaluates if the answer generated are alingned with the retirved context and query
Correctness → defines the accuracy of the responses to the actual responses
evaluates the generated response and correct answers and gives the score 
True postive → the ground truth and the gererated answer
FP →
FN →
Vector index evaluation is the process of measuring how accurately and efficiently your vector database retrieves the most relevant documents for a given query → using ragEvaluatorPack
RAG Evaluation Notes
Types of Evaluation in RAG
RAG evaluation is mainly divided into:
Retriever Evaluation Metrics
Response Evaluation Metrics
Vector Index Evaluation
1. Retriever Evaluation Metrics
These metrics evaluate how well the retriever fetches relevant documents/chunks from the vector database.
1. Hit Rate
Definition
Checks whether the retriever fetched at least one relevant document for the query.
If at least one relevant chunk is retrieved → Hit = 1
If no relevant chunk is retrieved → Hit = 0
Formula
Hit Rate={1if relevant document retrieved0otherwise\text{Hit Rate} = \begin{cases}1 & \text{if relevant document retrieved}\\0 & \text{otherwise}\end{cases}Hit Rate={10if relevant document retrievedotherwise
Key Point
Very simple metric
Does not consider ranking/position
Even if the relevant chunk is last, Hit Rate is still 1
Example
Query: "What is Retrieval-Augmented Generation?"
Retrieved Chunks:
Weather info
AI agents
RAG explanation ✅
Hit Rate = 1
2. Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR)
Definition
MRR evaluates where the first relevant document appears in the retrieved results.
Unlike Hit Rate, it considers ranking/position.
Formula
MRR=1N∑i=1N1rankiMRR = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{rank_i}MRR=N1∑i=1Nranki1
Where:
N = total number of queries
rank_i = position of first relevant result
Reciprocal Rank
Key Point
Higher MRR means:
Relevant documents appear earlier
Better retrieval quality
Example
Retrieved Results:
Cooking article
RAG document ✅
Random blog
Relevant result position = 2
MRR = 1/2 = 0.5
2. Response Evaluation Metrics
These metrics evaluate the quality of the generated answer.
3. Faithfulness
Definition
Measures whether the generated answer is fully supported by the retrieved context.
Used to detect:
Hallucinations
Unsupported claims
Fake/generated information
Goal
The LLM should answer only from provided context.
How Faithfulness Works
Step 1: Claim Extraction
The evaluator extracts all atomic claims/facts from the response.
Example:
Response:
"RAG combines retrieval and generation."
Claims:
RAG combines retrieval
RAG performs generation
Step 2: NLI Check (Natural Language Inference)
Checks whether each claim can be logically inferred from the retrieved context.
Step 3: Scoring
Formula
Faithfulness Score=Supported ClaimsTotal Claims\text{Faithfulness Score} = \frac{\text{Supported Claims}}{\text{Total Claims}}Faithfulness Score=Total ClaimsSupported Claims
Example
Total claims = 5
Supported claims = 4
Faithfulness = 4/5 = 0.8
Key Point
High Faithfulness means:
Answer is grounded in context
Less hallucination
Low Faithfulness means:
Model generated unsupported information
4. Correctness
Definition
Measures how accurate the generated response is compared to the ground truth (expected answer).
It compares:
Generated Answer
Actual/Reference Answer
Correctness Components
True Positive (TP)
Information present in:
Ground truth
Generated answer
False Positive (FP)
Information generated by the LLM but NOT present in the ground truth.
False Negative (FN)
Important information present in ground truth but missing in generated answer.
Key Point
Correctness checks:
Accuracy
Completeness
Missing information
Sometimes also called:
Context Reference
Accuracy Reference Evaluation
5. Semantic Similarity
Definition
Measures how semantically similar the generated answer is to the reference answer.
Even if wording differs, meaning should remain same.
Example
Ground Truth
"RAG improves LLM responses using external knowledge."
Generated Answer
"RAG enhances answers by retrieving outside information."
Different wording → Same meaning ✅
So semantic similarity score will be high.
Key Point
Uses:
Embeddings
Cosine similarity
Semantic understanding
instead of exact keyword matching.
3. Relevancy Evaluation
Definition
Checks whether the generated answer is aligned with:
User query
Retrieved context
Goal
Ensure:
Answer is relevant to question
Answer uses retrieved information properly
4. Vector Index Evaluation
Definition
Evaluates how efficiently and accurately the vector database retrieves relevant chunks/documents.
Focus areas:
Retrieval quality
Search efficiency
Embedding performance
Commonly Done Using
RagEvaluatorPack
LlamaIndex evaluation modules
Quick Summary Table
Important Interview Points
Hit Rate vs MRR
Faithfulness vs Correctness
One-Line Definitions
Hit Rate → Did retrieval find anything useful?
MRR → How early was the useful result found?
Faithfulness → Is the answer grounded in context?
Correctness → Is the answer actually correct?
Semantic Similarity → Does the answer mean the same thing?
Relevancy → Is the answer relevant to the query?
Vector Index Evaluation → How good is the vector DB retrieval system?
Metadata Extractors in RAG (LlamaIndex)
Metadata Extractors automatically generate extra information from documents/chunks and attach them as metadata.
This metadata helps improve:
Retrieval quality
Filtering
Semantic search
Query understanding
Reranking
Why Metadata Extraction is Important
Without metadata:
Retriever only depends on embeddings
With metadata:
Retriever gets additional structured context
Example:
This improves retrieval accuracy significantly.
Reranking 
This is one of the biggest missing production concepts.
What is Reranking?
After vector retrieval:
Top 20 retrieved chunks
↓
Reranker reorders them
↓
Top 5 best chunks sent to LLM
Why Needed?
Vector retrieval may return:
semantically similar
but not most relevant
Reranker improves precision.
Types
Cross-Encoder Reranker
Very accurate.
Example:
BAAI/bge-reranker-base
Flow
Query
↓
Vector Retrieval
↓
Reranker
↓
Best Chunks
↓
LLM
LlamaIndex Example
fromllama_index.postprocessor.flag_embedding_rerankerimport (
FlagEmbeddingReranker
)

reranker=FlagEmbeddingReranker(
top_n=5,
model="BAAI/bge-reranker-base"
)
Types of Metadata Extractors
Common extractors in LlamaIndex:
QuestionsAnsweredExtractor
SummaryExtractor
TitleExtractor
KeywordExtractor
EntityExtractor
1. QuestionsAnsweredExtractor
Definition
Generates possible questions that can be answered by a chunk.
These questions are stored as metadata.
Why It Helps
Instead of retrieving only by semantic similarity,
the retriever can match user queries against generated questions.
This improves:
Recall
Query matching
Retrieval relevance
Especially useful when:
User wording differs from document wording
Example
Original Chunk
RAG combines retrieval systems with language models
to improve factual accuracy.
Generated Metadata
{
"questions_this_excerpt_can_answer": [
"What is RAG?",
"How does RAG improve factual accuracy?",
"Why are retrieval systems used in LLMs?"
  ]
}
How It Works Internally
Step 1
Chunk is passed to an LLM
Step 2
LLM generates:
probable user questions
query variations
Step 3
Questions are attached as metadata
Benefits
LlamaIndex Example
fromllama_index.core.extractorsimportQuestionsAnsweredExtractor

extractor=QuestionsAnsweredExtractor(
questions=3
)
Full Pipeline Example
fromllama_index.core.ingestionimportIngestionPipeline
fromllama_index.core.extractorsimportQuestionsAnsweredExtractor

pipeline=IngestionPipeline(
transformations=[
QuestionsAnsweredExtractor(questions=3)
    ]
)

nodes=pipeline.run(documents=documents)
Resulting Metadata
node.metadata
Output:
{
"questions_this_excerpt_can_answer": [
"What is vector search?",
"How does retrieval work?"
  ]
}

2. SummaryExtractor

"Overview of RAG architecture and components"
}

Step 3
Summary added as metadata

Section Summary

Summary for entire document

fromllama_index.core.extractorsimportSummaryExtractor

extractor=SummaryExtractor(
summaries=["self"]
)
Meaning of "self"
summaries=["self"]
means:
summarize each chunk/node itself
Other Summary Modes
Full Pipeline Example
fromllama_index.core.ingestionimportIngestionPipeline
fromllama_index.core.extractorsimportSummaryExtractor

pipeline=IngestionPipeline(
transformations=[
SummaryExtractor(
summaries=["self"]
        )
    ]
)

Complete Example
fromllama_index.core.ingestionimportIngestionPipeline
fromllama_index.core.extractorsimport (
SummaryExtractor,
QuestionsAnsweredExtractor
)

pipeline=IngestionPipeline(
transformations=[
SummaryExtractor(
summaries=["self"]
        ),
QuestionsAnsweredExtractor(
questions=3
        )
    ]
)

nodes=pipeline.run(documents=documents)

"section_summary":
"Overview of RAG pipelines",

  ]

Fine Tuneing the embeddings

Embedding Fine-Tuning 
What is an Embedding?
Embedding:
text → vector (numbers)
Example:
"What is AI?"
↓
[0.12, -0.44, 0.91, ...]
Embeddings capture semantic meaning.
Similar meanings → similar vectors.
Why Embeddings are Used
Computers cannot understand raw text directly.
Embedding models convert text into vectors where:
similar concepts are close
unrelated concepts are far
Example:
"What is AI?"
and
"Explain artificial intelligence"
should have nearby vectors.
Embeddings in RAG
Step 1 — Convert document chunks into embeddings
chunk → embedding
Store in vector DB.
Step 2 — Convert user query into embedding
query → embedding
Step 3 — Similarity Search
Find closest vectors using cosine similarity.
Higher similarity = more relevant chunk.
What is Embedding Fine-Tuning?
Embedding fine-tuning means:
teaching the model what should be semantically similar in YOUR domain.
The model learns:
what should be close
what should be far
in vector space.
Important Point
Embedding fine-tuning does NOT teach:
chatting
reasoning
answering questions
It ONLY teaches:
semantic relationships
Example
Before tuning:
"surrender value"
may match finance/investing content.
After tuning:
"surrender value"
becomes close to:
policy maturity
annuity
insurance premium
Goal of Fine-Tuning
Improve retrieval quality.
Benefits:
better chunk retrieval
fewer irrelevant chunks
improved RAG answers
domain-aware retrieval
Core Idea
The model learns:
query ↔ relevant chunk = closer
query ↔ irrelevant chunk = farther
Training Data Format
Usually:
Positive Samples
Relevant matches.
Example:
"What is RAG?"
↔
"Retrieval-Augmented Generation combines retrieval with LLMs."
Negative Samples
Irrelevant matches.
Example:
"What is RAG?"
↔
"Transformers use self-attention."
Hard Negatives
Hard negatives are confusing negatives.
Bad negative:
"What is RAG?"
↔
"Cats are animals"
Too easy.
Good hard negative:
"What is RAG?"
↔
"Transformers are neural architectures"
Much harder.
Hard negatives improve retrieval quality significantly.
How Modern Systems Get Training Data
Manual labeling is expensive.
So companies use:
user clicks
search behavior
synthetic queries
retrieved negatives
in-batch negatives
Real-World Retrieval Evolution
Initial Phase
Use:
pretrained embedding models
hybrid search
rerankers
No fine-tuning initially.
User Interaction Collection
User searches:
"What is policy surrender?"
Retriever returns documents.
User clicks one document.
System stores:
(query, clicked_doc) = positive
Other unclicked retrieved docs become weak negatives.
After Enough Data
After weeks/months:
millions of interactions collected
relevance dataset built
Now:
fine-tune embeddings
improve retrieval quality
This is Called
implicit feedback learning
clickstream training
behavioral retrieval tuning
Industry Pipeline
Users search
    ↓
Collect clicks/interactions
    ↓
Build relevance dataset
    ↓
Mine hard negatives
    ↓
Fine-tune embeddings/rerankers
    ↓
Deploy improved retriever
Important Industry Reality
Most companies do NOT fine-tune embeddings first.
Usually they improve:
chunking
metadata
hybrid retrieval
rerankers
query rewriting
before embedding tuning.
Rerankers vs Embeddings
Embedding retrieval:
fast
approximate
Rerankers:
slower
more accurate
Modern retrieval systems often use:
Embedding Retrieval
        ↓
Reranker
        ↓
LLM
Popular Embedding Models
Popular Loss Function
Most common:
MultipleNegativesRankingLoss
Idea:
positive pairs pulled closer
other batch samples treated as negatives
Simplified Training Flow
Training examples
        ↓
Embedding model
        ↓
Generate embeddings
        ↓
Compute similarity
        ↓
Calculate loss
        ↓
Update weights
        ↓
Better embeddings
Mental Model
Embedding fine-tuning is basically:
rearranging vector space so domain concepts are grouped correctly.
One-Line Summary
Embedding fine-tuning:
teaching a model what texts should be semantically close in a specific domain.
Sentence Window Retrieval 
is one of the smartest retrieval techniques in LlamaIndex because it solves a major RAG problem:
What Sentence Window Retrieval does
Instead of:
storing big chunks
It stores:
small precise sentences
BUT during retrieval:
returns neighboring sentences too
Core Idea
Store
Small sentence embeddings.
Retrieve
Relevant sentence.
Expand
Return surrounding window.
Visual Flow
Sentence 1
Sentence 2
Sentence 3 ← matched
Sentence 4
Sentence 5
Retrieved response:
Sentence 2
Sentence 3
Sentence 4
This gives:
precise retrieval
richer context
Best of both worlds.
Why this is powerful
Normal chunking tradeoff:
Sentence window retrieval solves both.
In LlamaIndex
LlamaIndex stores:
sentence node
metadata containing neighboring sentences
At query time:
retrieves sentence
replaces with larger contextual window
Architecture
Document
    ↓
Sentence Splitter
    ↓
Each sentence becomes node
    ↓
Store neighboring sentences in metadata
    ↓
Embed sentence nodes
    ↓
Retrieve best sentence
    ↓
Expand using metadata window
Implementation in LlamaIndex
Step 1 — Install
pip install llama-index
Step 2 — Load documents
Example:
fromllama_index.coreimportDocument

documents= [
Document(
text="""
Transformers were introduced in 2017.

The transformer uses self-attention.

This mechanism allows parallel processing.

RNNs process sequentially.
"""
    )
]
Step 3 — Create Sentence Window Parser
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportSentenceWindowNodeParser
Step 4 — Configure parser
node_parser=SentenceWindowNodeParser.from_defaults(
window_size=2,
window_metadata_key="window",
original_text_metadata_key="original_text",
)
Important Parameters
window_size=2
Means:
2 sentences before
2 sentences after
will be attached.
Example
Matched sentence:
"The transformer uses self-attention."
Returned context:
Transformers were introduced in 2017.

The transformer uses self-attention.

This mechanism allows parallel processing.
Step 5 — Generate nodes
nodes=node_parser.get_nodes_from_documents(documents)
Step 6 — Build index
fromllama_index.coreimportVectorStoreIndex

index=VectorStoreIndex(nodes)
Step 7 — Create query engine
Need metadata replacement postprocessor.
fromllama_index.core.postprocessorimportMetadataReplacementPostProcessor
Step 8 — Configure postprocessor
postprocessor=MetadataReplacementPostProcessor(
target_metadata_key="window"
)
Step 9 — Query engine
query_engine=index.as_query_engine(
similarity_top_k=2,
node_postprocessors=[postprocessor]
)
Step 10 — Query
response=query_engine.query(
"How do transformers work?"
)

print(response)
What actually happens internally?
Retrieval Phase
Embeddings generated for:
individual sentences
Very precise retrieval.
Expansion Phase
Matched sentence replaced with:
neighboring sentence window
using metadata.
Why this works so well
Because embeddings perform better on:
smaller focused text
But LLMs perform better with:
broader context
Sentence window combines both.
Auto-Merging Retriever 
in LlamaIndex is another advanced retrieval technique designed to solve this problem:
retrieved chunks are individually relevant, but fragmented.
It tries to:
retrieve small precise chunks
automatically merge related parent chunks
give the LLM more coherent context
First understand the problem
Suppose document:
Transformers were introduced in 2017.

They use self-attention.

Self-attention enables parallel processing.

This improved NLP performance significantly.
Normal retrieval may return:
"They use self-attention."
and
"This improved NLP performance significantly."
These are relevant BUT fragmented.
LLM may struggle because:
context disconnected
reasoning incomplete
Auto-Merging Retriever solves this
Idea:
retrieve child chunks
        ↓
if many nearby chunks retrieved
        ↓
merge into larger parent chunk
So instead of isolated pieces:
Sentence A
Sentence B
Sentence C
you return:
Entire paragraph/block
Core intuition
It dynamically expands context ONLY when needed.
Unlike fixed large chunks.
Architecture
Documents are split hierarchically:
Document
   ↓
Large parent chunks
   ↓
Small child chunks
Embeddings are created mainly for:
smaller child chunks
Retrieval happens on:
precise child chunks
Then:
nearby children merged into parent
Visual Example
Parent chunk:
[Paragraph about transformers]
Children:
Child 1
Child 2
Child 3
Suppose retrieval finds:
Child 1
Child 2
Auto-merging says:
These belong together
and returns full parent paragraph.
Why this is useful
Small chunks:
better embeddings
better precision
Large chunks:
better reasoning
better context
Auto-merging gives both.
Comparison
How it works internally
Step 1 — Hierarchical chunking
Create:
parent nodes
child nodes
Step 2 — Embed child nodes
Smaller semantic units.
Step 3 — Retrieve top child nodes
Example:
Child 2
Child 3
Step 4 — Merge if overlap detected
Retriever notices:
same parent
nearby semantic area
Then merges into:
larger parent chunk
In LlamaIndex
You use:
HierarchicalNodeParser
AutoMergingRetriever
Implementation
Step 1 — Create hierarchical chunks
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportHierarchicalNodeParser
Step 2 — Configure chunk sizes
node_parser=HierarchicalNodeParser.from_defaults(
chunk_sizes=[2048,512,128]
)
This creates:
large parent chunks
medium chunks
small child chunks
Hierarchy tree.
Example hierarchy
2048-token parent
    ↓
512-token child
    ↓
128-token child
Step 3 — Generate nodes
nodes=node_parser.get_nodes_from_documents(documents)
Step 4 — Separate leaf nodes
Retriever embeds smallest chunks.
fromllama_index.core.node_parserimportget_leaf_nodes

leaf_nodes=get_leaf_nodes(nodes)
Step 5 — Build index
fromllama_index.coreimportVectorStoreIndex

index=VectorStoreIndex(leaf_nodes)
Step 6 — Create retriever
base_retriever=index.as_retriever(similarity_top_k=6)
Step 7 — Create storage context
Needed for parent-child relationships.
fromllama_index.core.storage.docstoreimportSimpleDocumentStore

docstore=SimpleDocumentStore()

docstore.add_documents(nodes)
Step 8 — Auto-Merging Retriever
fromllama_index.core.retrieversimportAutoMergingRetriever

retriever=AutoMergingRetriever(
base_retriever,
docstore,
verbose=True
)
Step 9 — Query
nodes=retriever.retrieve(
"How do transformers work?"
)

forninnodes:
print(n.text)
What happens internally?
Suppose retrieval gets:
Child chunk 1
Child chunk 2
Child chunk 3
all from same parent.
Auto-merger decides:
merge into parent context
Then returns larger coherent chunk.
Key Difference vs Sentence Window Retrieval
Sentence Window vs Auto-Merging
Sentence window:
great for sequential text
Auto-merging:
great for hierarchical documents
PDFs
reports
research papers
long structured docs
Industry Insight
Modern RAG systems increasingly use:
hierarchical retrieval
contextual expansion
recursive retrieval
instead of naive chunking.
Because:
retrieval precision matters
context coherence matters
Auto Retriever
letting the LLM automatically decide HOW retrieval should happen.
Instead of hardcoding:
filters
top_k
metadata conditions
retrieval strategy
the LLM dynamically generates retrieval parameters.
Why this exists
Normal retrieval is usually fixed:
retriever=index.as_retriever(
similarity_top_k=5
)
Problems:
same retrieval strategy for every query
no metadata intelligence
no adaptive filtering
Example problem
Suppose your documents contain metadata:
User asks:
"Show recent RAG papers"
Normal retriever:
only semantic search
Auto Retriever:
understands:
recent → year filter
RAG → category filter
Then automatically builds retrieval query.
Core Idea
Instead of:
User Query
    ↓
Embedding Search
we do:
User Query
    ↓
LLM analyzes intent
    ↓
Generate retrieval parameters
    ↓
Retriever executes search
What can Auto Retriever decide?
It can dynamically choose:
metadata filters
top_k
retrieval mode
vector search conditions
Example
User query:
"Recent transformer papers from 2025"
LLM converts this into:
{
"filters": {
"year":2025,
"topic":"transformers"
    },
"top_k":3
}
Automatically.
Why this is powerful
Because users speak naturally:
"Show beginner RAG tutorials from last year"
Auto Retriever extracts:
beginner
RAG
last year
into structured retrieval constraints.
Without Auto Retriever
You manually write filters:
MetadataFilters(...)
for every query pattern.
Not scalable.
With Auto Retriever
LLM becomes:
retrieval planner
query interpreter
Architecture
User Query
    ↓
LLM analyzes query
    ↓
Generate metadata filters
    ↓

    ↓
Return relevant docs
Typical use cases

In LlamaIndex

fromllama_index.core.vector_storesimportMetadataInfo

metadata_info= [

LLM internally decides:

Something like:

Router Query Engine
 is a specialized module, primarily used in the LlamaIndex framework, that intelligently directs a user's natural language query to the most suitable underlying query engine. [1, 2]
It acts as a decision-making layer, allowing a single interface to handle diverse tasks like summarization, specific fact retrieval, or querying different datasets. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Core Components: The engine typically consists of three main parts: [1]
• Query Engines: A collection of specialized engines (e.g., one for summarization and one for vector-based semantic search).
• Query Engine Tools: These wrap the query engines with metadata (names and descriptions) so the router understands what each engine is capable of.
• Selector: The "brain" that analyzes the user query and the tool descriptions to choose the right engine. Common types include:
    ◦ LLM Selectors: Use an LLM to choose a tool based on a text prompt.
    ◦ Pydantic Selectors: Use function-calling capabilities to return a structured choice. , 
How It Works
1. Input: The user submits a natural language question.
2. Selection: The router compares the query against the descriptions of available tools.
3. Routing: It directs the query to one (or sometimes multiple) of the candidate query engines.
4. Execution & Response: The selected engine processes the query and returns the final answer to the user.
Sub-question query engine:
Sub-Question Query Engine in LlamaIndex is used when:
a user query is too complex to answer with one retrieval step.
So the system:
breaks the query into smaller sub-questions
queries different indexes/tools separately
combines the answers
Why this exists
Normal RAG works well for:
"What is RAG?"
But struggles with:
"Compare transformers, RNNs, and CNNs for NLP tasks"
because this requires:
multiple concepts
multiple retrievals
synthesis across documents
Core Idea
Instead of:
one query → one retrieval
we do:
complex query
    ↓
split into sub-questions
    ↓
retrieve independently
    ↓
combine answers
Example
User asks:
"How are transformers different from RNNs?"
Sub-question engine may generate:
What are transformers?
What are RNNs?
What are the architectural differences?
Which performs better for long context?
Each gets retrieved separately.
Why this improves RAG
Complex questions often need:
multiple sources
decomposition
reasoning across documents
Sub-questioning improves:
retrieval coverage
reasoning quality
answer completeness
Architecture
User Query
    ↓
LLM decomposes question
    ↓
Generate sub-questions
    ↓
Each sub-question sent to retriever/tool
    ↓
Collect answers
    ↓
LLM synthesizes final answer
Important concept
This is similar to:
agentic retrieval
query planning
decomposition-based reasoning
Example with multiple tools
Suppose you have:
User asks:
"How did engineering growth affect company revenue?"
System creates:
revenue-related query
engineering-growth query
retrieves separately,
then combines.
Why normal retrieval fails here
Single embedding search may:
focus on one topic only
miss related concepts
retrieve incomplete context
Sub-questioning solves this
By forcing:
focused retrieval
modular reasoning
In LlamaIndex
Uses:
QueryEngineTool
SubQuestionQueryEngine
Implementation
Step 1 — Create indexes
Example:
vector_index=VectorStoreIndex(nodes)
summary_index=SummaryIndex(nodes)
Step 2 — Create query engines
vector_query_engine=vector_index.as_query_engine()
summary_query_engine=summary_index.as_query_engine()
Step 3 — Convert into tools
fromllama_index.core.toolsimportQueryEngineTool
Step 4 — Define tools
query_engine_tools= [

QueryEngineTool.from_defaults(
query_engine=vector_query_engine,
name="vector_tool",
description="Useful for detailed retrieval"
    ),

QueryEngineTool.from_defaults(
query_engine=summary_query_engine,
name="summary_tool",
description="Useful for summarization"
    )
]
Step 5 — Create sub-question engine
fromllama_index.core.query_engineimportSubQuestionQueryEngine
Step 6 — Configure
query_engine=SubQuestionQueryEngine.from_defaults(
query_engine_tools=query_engine_tools,
verbose=True
)
Step 7 — Query
response=query_engine.query(
"Compare transformers and RNNs"
)

print(response)
What happens internally?
LLM may generate:
1. What are transformers?
2. What are RNNs?
3. Compare architecture
4. Compare performance
Each queried independently.
Then:
answers combined
synthesized into final response
Why this is powerful
Because retrieval becomes:
modular
targeted
compositional
instead of:
one giant retrieval
Real-world use cases
Excellent for:
enterprise copilots
research assistants
financial analysis
multi-document reasoning
comparison questions
Important Insight
Sub-question querying is basically:
complex problem
    ↓
divide into smaller retrieval problems
Very similar to how humans reason.
Comparison
Relation to Agents
Sub-question engines are an early form of:
AI agents
retrieval planning
tool orchestration
because the LLM decides:
what to ask
which tool to use
how to combine answers
Simple Mental Model
Sub-Question Query Engine:
break big question into smaller searchable questions
That’s the core idea.

What is LangChain?
LangChain is an open-source framework used to build applications powered by Large Language Models (LLMs).
It helps developers with:
Data loading
Text splitting
Embeddings
Retrieval
Chains
Agents
Memory
Output parsing
LangChain simplifies the development of:
Chatbots
RAG applications
AI assistants
Search systems
Workflow automation
What are Document Loaders?
Document Loaders are components in LangChain that:
Load data from different sources
Convert them into standardized Document objects
Supported sources:
PDF
CSV
HTML
Markdown
JSON
YouTube
Wikipedia
GitHub
MS Office Docs
Purpose:
Standardize data before sending it to LLM pipelines
Document Object Structure
A LangChain Document contains:
Creating a Document Object
from langchain_core.documents impor tDocument

data=Document(
page_content="This is a LangChain document",
id=1,
metadata={"source":"AV"}
)

print(data)
Output:
Document(
id='1',
metadata={'source':'AV'},
page_content='This is a LangChain document'
)
Types of Document Loaders
1. File-Based Loaders
Load data based on file type.
Examples:
CSV
PDF
HTML
Markdown
JSON
2. Source-Based Loaders
Load data from external platforms.
Examples:
YouTube
Wikipedia
GitHub
AWS
Azure
Categories
CSV Loader
Used to load CSV files row-by-row as Documents.
Example
from langchain_community.document_loaders.csv_loader import CSVLoader

loader=CSVLoader(

)

data=loader.load()

data[0].metadata

'

Text Splitting
Large documents are split into smaller chunks before embeddings.
Why?
LLMs have token/input limitations
Smaller chunks improve retrieval accuracy
CharacterTextSplitter Example
fromlangchain.text_splitterimportCharacterTextSplitter

text_splitter=CharacterTextSplitter(
chunk_size=100,
chunk_overlap=20,
separator="\n"
)

chunks=text_splitter.split_text(page_content)
Important Parameters
Vector Stores in LangChain
After splitting:
Generate embeddings
Store embeddings in vector databases
Purpose:
Fast similarity search
Retrieval for RAG systems
Chroma Vector Store Example
fromlangchain.vectorstoresimportChroma
fromlangchain.embeddingsimportOpenAIEmbeddings

embeddings=OpenAIEmbeddings()

db=Chroma.from_documents(
documents,
embeddings
)

query="What is transformer?"

docs=db.similarity_search(query)
RAG Pipeline Flow
Steps
1. Load
Load documents using Document Loaders.
2. Split
Split large text into chunks.
3. Embed
Convert chunks into vectors.
4. Store
Store embeddings in vector DB.
5. Retrieve
Retrieve relevant chunks.
6. Generate
LLM generates final response.
LangChain Chains
Chains combine multiple operations together.
Example:
Prompt → LLM → Output
LCEL (LangChain Expression Language)
Modern way to build chains in LangChain.
Example
fromlangchain_core.promptsimportPromptTemplate
fromlangchain_openaiimportOpenAI

prompt_template="Tell me a {adjective} joke"

prompt=PromptTemplate(
input_variables=["adjective"],
template=prompt_template
)

llm=OpenAI()

chain=prompt|llm

result=chain.invoke("funny")
LangChain Core Components
Vector Database

A Vector Database is a specialized database designed to store, manage, and search high-dimensional vectors.
Vectors are numerical representations of data such as:
Text
Images
Audio
Videos
User behavior
Unlike traditional databases that search exact values using rows and columns, vector databases search based on semantic similarity.
Example:
Searching for "red dress" can also return images/products related to red gowns even if the exact words are not present.
Vector databases are widely used in:
AI applications
Recommendation systems
Semantic search
Chatbots
Image retrieval
NLP systems
How Vector Databases Work
1. Data Ingestion
Convert Data into Vectors
Machine learning embedding models convert data into vectors.
Examples:
Text → Word embeddings
Images → Image embeddings
Store the Vectors
The generated vectors are stored along with metadata.
Example metadata:
Product name
User ID
Timestamp
2. Indexing
Vector databases use advanced indexing methods for fast retrieval.
Indexing in a vd creates a map or guide, pointing to where each vector is located in a list
I
Common indexing techniques:
HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World)
ANN (Approximate Nearest Neighbor)
PQ (Product Quantization)
Goal:
Balance speed and accuracy
Efficient search on huge datasets
3. Querying
Similarity Search
Instead of exact matching, vector databases find the most similar vectors.
Common similarity metrics:
Retrieval Process
Convert query into vector
Compare with stored vectors
Return nearest matches
4. Integration with Applications
Vector databases provide:
APIs
SDKs
ML integrations
Used in:
Search engines
Recommendation systems

Real-time analytics

This enables:

1. Retriever Metrics

3. Random football article ❌

Did the LLM generate a good answer?
Common Generator Metrics

Generated answer:
Einstein proposed natural selection.
Retriever OK ✅
Generator failed ❌
