The decoder is given the real previous word, not its own prediction:
Target: "Bien merci <END>"

Step 1: Feed <START>  → predict "Bien"  ✅ (correct: "Bien")
Step 2: Feed "Bien"   → predict "merci" ✅ (correct: "merci")  ← real word, not prediction
Step 3: Feed "merci"  → predict <END>   ✅
This speeds up training dramatically — errors don't compound.
During Inference — Autoregressive
The decoder uses its own predictions as input:
Step 1: Feed <START>  → predict "Bien"
Step 2: Feed "Bien"   → predict "merci"   ← own prediction fed back
Step 3: Feed "merci"  → predict <END>
⚠️ The Bottleneck Problem
Here's the critical weakness of vanilla Seq2Seq:
"The cat sat on the mat and looked at the dog"
 ──────────────────────────────────────────────
              10 words of meaning
                      ↓
               [  256 numbers  ]   ← context vector
                      ↓
        "Le chat était assis sur le tapis
         et regardait le chien"
One fixed-size vector must carry ALL the meaning of the input. For long sentences this becomes a severe bottleneck — early words get "forgotten" by the time the encoder finishes.
This is what motivated the invention of Attention — but that's a separate topic!
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
import random

# ── 1. Encoder ───────────────────────────────────────────────
class Encoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, vocab_size, embed_dim, hidden_size,
                 num_layers=2, dropout=0.3):
        super().__init__()
        self.embedding = nn.Embedding(vocab_size, embed_dim, padding_idx=0)
        self.lstm = nn.LSTM(
            embed_dim, hidden_size,
            num_layers=num_layers,
            batch_first=True,
            dropout=dropout
        )
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x):
        # x: (batch, src_seq_len)
        embedded = self.dropout(self.embedding(x))
        # embedded: (batch, src_seq_len, embed_dim)

        outputs, (hidden, cell) = self.lstm(embedded)
        # outputs: (batch, src_seq_len, hidden)  ← all hidden states
        # hidden:  (num_layers, batch, hidden)   ← final hidden state
        # cell:    (num_layers, batch, hidden)   ← final cell state

        return hidden, cell   # context vector — passed to decoder

# ── 2. Decoder ───────────────────────────────────────────────
class Decoder(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, vocab_size, embed_dim, hidden_size,
                 num_layers=2, dropout=0.3):
        super().__init__()
        self.embedding = nn.Embedding(vocab_size, embed_dim, padding_idx=0)
        self.lstm = nn.LSTM(
            embed_dim, hidden_size,
            num_layers=num_layers,
            batch_first=True,
            dropout=dropout
        )
        self.fc = nn.Linear(hidden_size, vocab_size)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x, hidden, cell):
        # x: (batch,) — one token at a time
        x = x.unsqueeze(1)   # (batch, 1) — add seq dimension

        embedded = self.dropout(self.embedding(x))
        # embedded: (batch, 1, embed_dim)

        output, (hidden, cell) = self.lstm(embedded, (hidden, cell))
        # output: (batch, 1, hidden)

        prediction = self.fc(output.squeeze(1))
        # prediction: (batch, vocab_size) — scores for each word

        return prediction, hidden, cell

# ── 3. Seq2Seq — Puts It All Together ────────────────────────
class Seq2Seq(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, encoder, decoder, device):
        super().__init__()
        self.encoder = encoder
        self.decoder = decoder
        self.device  = device

    def forward(self, src, trg, teacher_forcing_ratio=0.5):
        # src: (batch, src_len)
        # trg: (batch, trg_len)
        batch_size = src.shape[0]
        trg_len    = trg.shape[1]
        trg_vocab  = self.decoder.fc.out_features

        # Store all decoder outputs
        outputs = torch.zeros(batch_size, trg_len, trg_vocab).to(self.device)

        # ── Encode ──────────────────────────────────────────
        hidden, cell = self.encoder(src)

        # First decoder input = <START> token (index 1)
        dec_input = trg[:, 0]   # (batch,)

        # ── Decode word by word ──────────────────────────────
        for t in range(1, trg_len):
            prediction, hidden, cell = self.decoder(dec_input, hidden, cell)
            outputs[:, t, :] = prediction

            # Teacher forcing: use real word or model's prediction
            use_teacher = random.random() < teacher_forcing_ratio
            top_word    = prediction.argmax(1)   # model's best guess
            dec_input   = trg[:, t] if use_teacher else top_word

        return outputs   # (batch, trg_len, vocab_size)

# ── 4. Instantiate & Train ───────────────────────────────────
device = torch.device('cuda' if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'cpu')

SRC_VOCAB = 8000
TRG_VOCAB = 10000
EMBED_DIM  = 256
HIDDEN     = 512
LAYERS     = 2

encoder = Encoder(SRC_VOCAB, EMBED_DIM, HIDDEN, LAYERS)
decoder = Decoder(TRG_VOCAB, EMBED_DIM, HIDDEN, LAYERS)
model   = Seq2Seq(encoder, decoder, device).to(device)

optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss(ignore_index=0)  # ignore padding

# Training step
def train_step(src, trg):
    model.train()
    optimizer.zero_grad()

    output = model(src, trg, teacher_forcing_ratio=0.5)
    # output: (batch, trg_len, vocab)
    # trg:    (batch, trg_len)

    # Reshape for loss: skip <START> token at position 0
    output = output[:, 1:, :].reshape(-1, TRG_VOCAB)
    trg    = trg[:, 1:].reshape(-1)

    loss = criterion(output, trg)
    loss.backward()
    torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 1.0)
    optimizer.step()
    return loss.item()

# Test forward pass
src = torch.randint(1, SRC_VOCAB, (32, 15)).to(device)  # batch=32, src_len=15
trg = torch.randint(1, TRG_VOCAB, (32, 12)).to(device)  # batch=32, trg_len=12
out = model(src, trg)
print(f"Output shape: {out.shape}")  # → (32, 12, 10000)
```

---

## 🗺️ Visual Mental Model
```
SOURCE: "How    are    you?"
         ↓       ↓      ↓
       [Emb]   [Emb]  [Emb]
         ↓       ↓      ↓
ENCODE: [LSTM]→[LSTM]→[LSTM]
                           ↓
                     (hidden, cell)
                     context vector 🔵
                           ↓
DECODE:              [LSTM] → "Bien"
                       ↓
                     [LSTM] → "merci"
                       ↓
                     [LSTM] → <END>
                     
TARGET:  "Bien  merci  <END>"
```

---

## ✅ Strengths of Seq2Seq

- Handles **variable length** input and output naturally
- Clean separation of **understanding** (encoder) and **generation** (decoder)
- Works for any sequence transformation task
- Foundation for modern NLP architectures

## ❌ Limitations

| Problem | Consequence |
|---|---|
| **Bottleneck** | One vector can't capture long sequences well |
| **Sequential** | Can't be parallelized — slow to train |
| **Forgetting** | Early words lost in long sequences |
| **Fixed context** | Decoder can't look back at encoder states |

---

## 🌍 Real World Applications

| Task | Input → Output |
|---|---|
| **Machine Translation** | English → French |
| **Text Summarization** | Long article → Short summary |
| **Chatbot** | Question → Answer |
| **Code Generation** | Description → Code |
| **Speech Recognition** | Audio → Text |
| **Image Captioning** | Image → Sentence |

---

## 🔮 What Comes Next?

Seq2Seq's bottleneck problem directly motivated two major innovations:
```
Seq2Seq (2014)
     ↓
Seq2Seq + Attention (2015)  ← decoder looks at ALL encoder states
     ↓
Transformer (2017)          ← attention only, no RNN at all
     ↓
BERT, GPT, ChatGPT...
🔤 Plain English — How Attention Works
LSTM — Plain English
The Problem It Solves
A vanilla RNN forgets things from far back in a sentence. LSTM fixes this by having two separate memory systems — one for short term, one for long term.
Step 1 — Two memories enter the cell
At every word, LSTM receives two things from the previous step:
hₜ₋₁  →  short term memory  "what I just saw"
cₜ₋₁  →  long term memory   "what I've been carrying for a while"
Plus the current word xₜ as input.
Step 2 — Forget Gate decides what to throw away
The cell looks at the current word and short term memory and asks:
"Is anything in my long term memory now useless?"
It produces values between 0 and 1 for each memory slot:
0  →  completely erase this slot
1  →  keep this slot fully
For example, if the sentence changes topic, old context gets erased here.
Step 3 — Input Gate decides what new info to write
Now it asks:
"What new information from the current word is worth saving?"
Two things happen together:
Input gate   →  HOW MUCH new info to write  (0 to 1)
Candidate    →  WHAT new info to write      (-1 to 1)
These two are multiplied together — only important new info gets written.
Step 4 — Long term memory gets updated
The long term memory cₜ is now updated:
Old memory  ×  forget gate   →  what survived from before
New info    ×  input gate    →  what gets added now
Add both together            →  new long term memory
This is the heart of LSTM — memory is updated additively, which prevents gradients from vanishing.
Step 5 — Output Gate decides what to expose
Not everything in long term memory is relevant right now. The output gate asks:
"Which parts of long term memory should I share as output?"
It filters the long term memory and produces the new short term memory hₜ.
Step 6 — Two outputs leave the cell
hₜ  →  short term memory  passed to next step + used as output
cₜ  →  long term memory   passed to next step silently
This repeats for every word in the sequence.
💡 One Line Summary
LSTM has two memory lanes — a long term highway that carries important info across many steps, and a short term output. Three gates control what gets erased, written, and exposed at each step.
🟢 GRU — Plain English
The Problem It Solves
LSTM works great but has lots of parameters and two separate memory streams. GRU simplifies this into one memory stream and two gates — faster and nearly as powerful.
Step 1 — Only one memory enters
Unlike LSTM's two inputs, GRU only receives:
hₜ₋₁  →  one single hidden state  "everything I remember"
xₜ    →  current word
No separate long term memory — one stream does both jobs.
Step 2 — Reset Gate decides how much past to use
The reset gate asks:
"When computing new candidate memory, how much of my past should I look at?"
reset → 0  →  ignore the past completely  (fresh start)
reset → 1  →  use all of the past         (full context)
This is applied before computing new candidate memory — it controls how context-aware the new memory will be.
Step 3 — Candidate memory is computed
Using the reset-filtered past and the current word, a candidate new memory is computed:
candidate = tanh( current word + reset gate × past hidden state )
This is what the new hidden state could be — but not necessarily what it will be.
Step 4 — Update Gate decides how much to change
This is GRU's most powerful gate. It asks:
"Should I update my hidden state a lot or a little?"
update → 1  →  replace mostly with new candidate
update → 0  →  keep mostly the old hidden state
Notice — this single gate does the job of both the forget gate AND input gate from LSTM. It's a blend dial between old and new.
Step 5 — New hidden state is a blend
new hidden state = (1 - update) × old hidden state
                 +  update      × candidate
In plain words:
Keep some of the old  +  Add some of the new  =  Updated memory
Step 6 — One output leaves
hₜ  →  the single hidden state, passed to next step and used as output
💡 One Line Summary
GRU has one memory stream and two gates — the reset gate controls how much past context to use when forming new memory, and the update gate controls how much the memory actually changes. Simpler than LSTM, nearly as powerful.
🟠 Seq2Seq — Plain English
The Problem It Solves
Normal RNNs require input and output to be the same length. But translation, summarisation, and chatbots need different length inputs and outputs. Seq2Seq splits the job into two separate networks.
🔷 ENCODER — The Reader
Step 1 — Read the input sentence word by word
The encoder is just an LSTM or GRU that reads the source sentence:
"How"  →  LSTM  →  hidden state h₁
"are"  →  LSTM  →  hidden state h₂
"you?" →  LSTM  →  hidden state h₃
Each step updates the hidden state with new information.
Step 2 — Discard everything except the last hidden state
After reading all words, the encoder throws away all intermediate hidden states and keeps only the final one:
h₃  →  this is called the CONTEXT VECTOR
This single vector is supposed to capture the entire meaning of the input sentence — like compressing a paragraph into a sticky note.
Step 3 — Encoder stops — its job is done
The encoder hands the context vector to the decoder and steps aside.
🔶 DECODER — The Writer
Step 4 — Decoder starts with the context vector
The decoder initialises its hidden state with the encoder's context vector:
decoder hidden state  =  context vector  "I know what the source means"
Step 5 — Feed a special START token
The decoder is given a special <START> token as its first input — a signal that says:
"Begin generating output now"
Step 6 — Predict the first output word
The decoder combines the START token and context vector to predict the first word:
<START> + context vector  →  LSTM  →  predict "Bien"
It looks at probabilities over the entire vocabulary and picks the most likely word.
Step 7 — Feed prediction back as next input
The predicted word becomes the input for the next step:
"Bien"  →  LSTM  →  predict "merci"
"merci" →  LSTM  →  predict <END>
This is called autoregressive generation — the model feeds itself.
Step 8 — Stop at END token
When the decoder generates the special <END> token, it stops. The collected words form the output sentence:
"Bien merci" ✅
🔴 During Training — Teacher Forcing
During training, instead of feeding the model's own predictions back, you feed the real correct words:
Step 1: feed <START>  →  predict "Bien"
Step 2: feed "Bien"   →  predict "merci"   ← real word, not model's guess
Step 3: feed "merci"  →  predict <END>
This makes training faster and more stable — errors don't snowball.
💡 One Line Summary
Seq2Seq has two networks — the encoder reads and compresses the entire input into one context vector, and the decoder starts from that vector and generates the output word by word until it decides to stop.
🔁 How They All Connect
LSTM / GRU
  ↓
used as building blocks inside...
  ↓
Seq2Seq  (encoder + decoder, both made of LSTM/GRU)
  ↓
still had a bottleneck — one context vector not enough
  ↓
Attention  (decoder looks at ALL encoder steps, not just the last)
  ↓
Transformer  (attention everywhere, no RNN needed at all)
🟦 ENCODER SIDE
Step 1 — Read the input sentence word by word
The encoder reads each word one at a time. For example:
"The"  →  processed  →  saves hidden state h₁
"black" → processed  →  saves hidden state h₂
"cat"  →  processed  →  saves hidden state h₃
Step 2 — Keep ALL hidden states, not just the last one
Unlike vanilla Seq2Seq which only kept the final hidden state, attention saves every single hidden state from every word.
h₁ = memory of "The"
h₂ = memory of "black"
h₃ = memory of "cat"
... and so on
Think of these as individual notes the encoder took while reading each word.
🟨 ATTENTION SIDE — The Bridge
Step 3 — Decoder looks at ALL encoder notes
When the decoder wants to generate a word, it first looks at all the encoder's saved hidden states and asks:
"Which of these notes is most useful to me RIGHT NOW?"
Step 4 — Give each encoder note a relevance score
The decoder compares its current state against every encoder hidden state and assigns a score to each:
h₁ ("The")   → score: 0.05   → not very relevant
h₂ ("black") → score: 0.10   → somewhat relevant
h₃ ("cat")   → score: 0.85   → very relevant ✅
Higher score = more relevant to the current decoding step.
Step 5 — Convert scores into weights using Softmax
The raw scores are converted into proper weights that all add up to 1 — like percentages:
h₁ → 5%  attention
h₂ → 10% attention
h₃ → 85% attention   ← focus here
This is called the attention distribution.
Step 6 — Build a dynamic context vector
Now multiply each encoder hidden state by its attention weight and add them all up:
context = (0.05 × h₁) + (0.10 × h₂) + (0.85 × h₃)
This produces a single vector that is heavily influenced by "cat" — the most relevant word. This context vector is different at every decoding step — that's the magic!
🟩 DECODER SIDE
Step 7 — Decoder combines context + its own hidden state
The decoder takes two things together:
Its own hidden state  →  "what I've generated so far"
Context vector        →  "what I should focus on in the source"
These are combined together into one vector.
Step 8 — Predict the next word
That combined vector is passed through a final layer which outputs probabilities over the entire vocabulary:
"chat"   → 72%  ✅
"chien"  → 12%
"maison" → 3%
...
The word with the highest probability is picked — "chat" (French for cat).
Step 9 — Repeat for every output word
The decoder repeats steps 3 to 8 for every word it generates. Each time, the attention weights shift to focus on different source words:
Generating "Le"    → focus on "The"   (h₁)
Generating "noir"  → focus on "black" (h₂)
Generating "chat"  → focus on "cat"   (h₃)
Step 10 — Stop when <END> token is generated
The decoder keeps generating words until it produces a special end token, which signals it's done.
🔁 Full Flow — One Simple Picture
SOURCE SENTENCE:
"The    black    cat"
  ↓       ↓       ↓
  h₁      h₂      h₃   ← encoder saves ALL hidden states

          ↓  ↓  ↓
       ATTENTION LAYER
    "which note matters most?"
          ↓  ↓  ↓
       5% 10% 85%      ← attention weights

          ↓
    context vector     ← weighted mix, mostly h₃

          ↓
    decoder hidden state + context vector

          ↓
       "chat" ✅        ← predicted French word
💡 One Line Summary
The encoder takes individual notes for every word. The decoder, at each step, reads all those notes, decides which ones matter most right now, and uses that focused information to generate the next word.

=== PAGE: Basics of ML ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Basics-of-ML-2edfca3384fc80bcb8ffc7b7e374dad1

What is Data Division (Data Splitting)?
Definition:
Data division is the process of splitting a dataset into multiple parts so that a machine learning model can be trained, validated, and tested properly.
Purpose:
Evaluate model performance on unseen data
Prevent overfitting
Estimate real-world performance
🔹 1. Train–Test Split
Definition:
The dataset is split into two parts:
Training set → used to train the model
Test set → used to evaluate the final model
Common Split Ratios:
80% Train – 20% Test
70% Train – 30% Test
Diagram (conceptual):
[ Training Data | Test Data ]


Advantages:
Simple and fast
Good for large datasets
Disadvantages:
Performance depends on how the split is done
Can be unreliable for small datasets
🔹 2. Train–Validation–Test Split
Definition:
The dataset is divided into three parts:
Training set → trains the model
Validation set → tunes hyperparameters
Test set → final evaluation
Common Ratio:
70% Train – 15% Validation – 15% Test
Why Validation Set?
Helps choose best model without touching test data
🔹 3. K-Fold Cross Validation
Definition:
The dataset is split into K equal parts (folds).
The model is trained K times, each time using:
K−1 folds for training
1 fold for validation
Final performance = average of all K results
Diagram (K=5):
Fold1 →Test|Fold2–5 →Train
Fold2 →Test|Fold1,3–5 →Train
...


Common Values of K:
K = 5
K = 10
Advantages:
Uses entire dataset efficiently
More reliable performance estimate
Disadvantages:
Computationally expensive
🔹 4. Stratified Train–Test Split
Definition:
A train–test split where class proportions are preserved in both training and test sets.
Used When:
Classification problems
Imbalanced datasets
Example:
If dataset has:
70% Class A
30% Class B
Both train and test sets keep the same ratio.
🔹 5. Leave-One-Out Cross Validation (LOOCV)
Definition:
A special case of K-Fold where:
K = number of data points


Each iteration:
1 sample → test
remaining → train
Pros:
Maximum data usage
Cons:
Very slow for large datasets
StandardScaler
What is StandardScaler?
Definition:
StandardScaler is a feature scaling technique that transforms data so that:
Mean = 0
Standard Deviation = 1
It standardizes features using Z-score normalization.
📐 Formula
For each feature:
z = (x − μ) / σ


Where:
x = original value
μ = mean of the feature
σ = standard deviation of the feature
📊 What Does It Do?
Before scaling:
Feature 1:  [10,20,30]
Feature 2:  [1000,2000,3000]


After StandardScaler:
Feature1:[-1.22, 0, 1.22]
Feature2:[-1.22, 0, 1.22]


➡️ Both features now have equal importance.
⭐ Why is StandardScaler Important?
🔹 1. Prevents Dominance of Large Values
Models that use distance or gradients get biased if one feature has larger values.
🔹 2. Improves Model Performance
Helps algorithms converge faster and give better accuracy.
🔹 3. Required for Many Algorithms
Classification evaluation model
A confusion matrix is a NxN table that helps evaluate a machine learning model's performance by
comparing its predictions to actual values, with rows and columns representing classes.
TP: Correctly predicted positive
TN: Correctly predicted negative
FP: Predicted positive, but actually negative (Type I error)
FN: Predicted negative, but actually positive (Type II error)
🔹 1. Accuracy
✅ Definition
Accuracy measures how many predictions the model got correct overall.
📐 Formula
Accuracy=TP+TNTP+TN+FP+FN\text{Accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}
Accuracy=TP+TN+FP+FNTP+TN
🧠 When to use
Classes are balanced
Cost of FP and FN is similar
🔹 2. Precision
✅ Definition
Precision measures how many predicted positives are actually positive.
📐 Formula
Precision=TP/FP+TP
🧠 When to use
False positives are costly
Example: Spam detection
🔹 3. Recall (Sensitivity / True Positive Rate)
✅ Definition
Recall measures how many actual positives were correctly identified.
📐 Formula
Recall=TP/FN+TP
🧠 When to use
False negatives are costly
Example: Disease detection
🔹 4. F1 Score
✅ Definition
F1 Score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall, balancing both.
📐 Formula
F1 Score = 2*(recall * precision/(recall+ precision))
🧠 When to use
Imbalanced datasets
Need a balance between precision & recall
🔹 Quick Summary Table
ROC
ROC Curve plots TPR against FPR at various thresholds, separating signal from noise.
X-axis: False Positive Rate - FP/FP + TN
Y-axis: True Positive Rate (TPR) - TP/TP+FN
Each point on the curve corresponds to a different classification threshold.
AUC (Area Under the Curve) is the area under the ROC curve.
📐 Value Range
0.0 – 1.0
🧠 Interpretation
1.0 → Perfect classifier
0.5 → Random guessing
< 0.5 → Worse than random (labels likely flipped)
👉 Probabilistic meaning:
AUC is the probability that the model ranks a random positive example higher than a random negative one.
Regression - Evaluation methods
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
 Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
 R2 Score
 Adjusted R2 Score
Any predictive model can be evaluated based on correctness and the reliability
Mean Absolute error: 
sum(Abs(actual values - predicted values))/n
Take the predicted values from your model for each data point
Subtract the corresponding actual values from the predicted values
Convert the differences in absolute errors
Calculate the sum of absolute errors
Find the mean of absolute error
R² measures the proportion of variance in the dependent variable that is explained by the independent variables.
Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
Definition:
Mean Absolute Error measures the average absolute difference between actual values and predicted values.
It tells you how far predictions are from real values, on average, without considering direction.
MAE = (1 / n) × Σ | yᵢ − ŷᵢ |

n = number of observations
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)
Definition:
Root Mean Squared Error measures the square root of the average squared differences between actual and predicted values.
It penalizes large errors more heavily than MAE.
Formula:
RMSE = √[ (1 / n) × Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² ]


Where:
n = number of observations
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
📌 R² Score (Coefficient of Determination)
Definition:
R² Score explains how much variance in the target variable is explained by the model.
Values range from 0 to 1, where higher is better.
Note:
Variance measures how spread out the data points are from the mean.
It tells you how much the values vary in a dataset.
Low variance → data points are close to the mean
High variance → data points are widely spread
Formula:
R² =1 − [ Σ (yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² / Σ (yᵢ − ȳ )² ]


Where:
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
ȳ = mean of actual values
📌 Adjusted R² Score
Definition:
Adjusted R² adjusts the R² score by penalizing unnecessary features.
It is more reliable when comparing models with different numbers of predictors.
Formula:
AdjustedR² =1 − [ (1 −R²) × (n −1) / (n −p −1) ]


Where:
n = number of observations
p = number of independent variables
R² = R² score
KNN
Definition:
K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is a supervised, non-parametric algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It makes predictions based on the K closest data points to a new input.
How Does KNN Work?
Step-by-Step Process
Choose K
Number of nearest neighbors (e.g., K = 3, 5)
Calculate Distance
Compute distance between the new point and all training points
(commonly Euclidean distance)
Select K Nearest Neighbors
Pick the K points with the smallest distances
Make Prediction
Classification → majority vote
Regression → average of values
Distance Formula (Most Common)
Euclidean Distance
d = √[ (x₁ − y₁)² + (x₂ − y₂)² + ... + (xₙ − yₙ)² ]


📊 KNN for Classification
Example:
K = 5
3 neighbors → Class A
2 neighbors → Class B
➡️ Predicted class = A
📈 KNN for Regression
Example:
K = 4
Neighbor values:
10, 12, 14, 16


➡️ Prediction = (10 + 12 + 14 + 16) / 4 = 13
🔢 Choosing the Value of K
👉 Usually found using cross-validation
⭐ Advantages of KNN
Simple to understand
No training phase
Works well with small datasets
⚠️ Disadvantages of KNN
Slow for large datasets
Sensitive to noise
Requires feature scaling
High memory usage
❗ Why Scaling is Important in KNN
KNN is distance-based, so features with large values dominate.
➡️ Always use StandardScaler or MinMaxScaler
🧠 When to Use KNN
✅ Small to medium datasets
✅ Low-dimensional data
❌ Very large datasets
❌ High-dimensional data (curse of dimensionality)
Logistic Regression:
Used for a classification problem based on linear regression
odds ratio: 
log loss function -? perfect model will be 0
Logistic Regression (statsmodels) — Interpretation & Diagnostics
Logistic Regression is used when the target variable is categorical (usually binary: 0/1, Yes/No).
Instead of predicting a value, it predicts a probability using the log-odds (logit) function.
📌 Logistic Regression Equation
log(p / (1 − p)) = β₀ + β₁X₁ + β₂X₂ + ... + βₙXₙ


Where:
p = probability of class 1
β = coefficients
X = features
🔍 statsmodels Logistic Regression Summary — Key Metrics
When you run:
model = sm.Logit(y, X).fit()


You get the following diagnostics 👇
📌 Coefficients (β)
Meaning:
Represents the change in log-odds for a 1-unit increase in the feature.
Interpretation Tip:
Positive β → increases probability of class 1
Negative β → decreases probability of class 1
📌 Odds Ratio (exp(β))
Meaning:
Exponentiating coefficients gives odds ratios.
OddsRatio= e^β


Interpretation:
OR > 1 → feature increases odds
OR < 1 → feature decreases odds
📌 p-value (Very Important)
Meaning:
Tests whether a feature is statistically significant in predicting the target class.
Null Hypothesis:
Feature has no effect on the outcome.
Desired Value:
p-value <0.05  ✅


📌 Log-Likelihood (LL)
Meaning:
Measures how well the model fits the data.
Higher (less negative) → better fit
Used for model comparison, not absolute interpretation.
📌 Log-Likelihood Ratio (LLR)
Meaning:
Compares the fitted model against a null model (no predictors).
Desired:
Large value + smallp-value


📌 LLR p-value
Meaning:
Tests whether at least one predictor significantly improves the model.
Desired Value:
LLRp-value <0.05  ✅


➡️ Confirms the model is better than guessing.
📌 Pseudo R² (McFadden’s R²)
Meaning:
Indicates how well the model explains the outcome
(Not the same as linear regression R²)
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
👉 Even 0.3 is considered very good in logistic regression.
📌 Convergence
Meaning:
Indicates whether the optimization algorithm successfully found optimal coefficients.
Desired:
converged:True  ✅


❌ False → model may be unreliable.
🧪 Assumption Checks for Logistic Regression
📌 Multicollinearity (VIF)
Same as Linear Regression
High correlation between features inflates variance
Check using VIF
Desired:
VIF <5 (preferably <3)


📌 Linearity of Log-Odds
Meaning:
Logistic regression assumes a linear relationship between predictors and log-odds, not the target itself.
❌ Violations → poor predictions
📌 Independence of Observations
Each data point must be independent
No repeated or grouped observations
🧠 What Logistic Regression Does NOT Use
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics (After Training)
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Logistic Regression:
Predicts probabilities using log-odds for classification problems.
p-value:
Tells whether a feature significantly affects the probability of the outcome.
Pseudo R²:
Measures model fit but is not interpreted like linear regression R².
Decision Tree
A Decision Tree is a supervised learning algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It works by splitting data into smaller subsets based on feature conditions, forming a tree-like structure.
🧠 Core Idea (Intuition)
“Ask a sequence of yes/no questions to reach a decision.”
Each internal node = question
Each branch = answer
Each leaf node = final prediction
⚙️ How Decision Tree Works
Choose the best feature to split the data
Split data to maximize purity
Repeat recursively
Stop when:
Node is pure
Max depth reached
Minimum samples reached
📐 Splitting Criteria (Very Important)
📌 1. Gini Impurity (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures how impure a node is.
Formula:
Gini =1 − Σ (pᵢ)²


Desired Value:
Lower isbetter (0 = pure node)


📌 2. Entropy (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures disorder or randomness in a node.
Formula:
Entropy = − Σ pᵢ log₂(pᵢ)


Desired Value:
Loweris better


📌 3. Information Gain
Meaning:
Measures how much entropy is reduced after a split.
Formula:
InformationGain=Entropy(parent) −WeightedEntropy(children)


Desired Value:
Higheris better


📌 4. Variance Reduction (Regression Trees)
Meaning:
Used in Decision Tree Regression.
Tree splits data to minimize variance in target values.
Desired:
Lower variance in leaf nodes


📊 Example (Classification)
Dataset
Tree Rule
IFStudent=Yes→Buy=Yes
ELSE→Buy=No


➡️ Final prediction comes from leaf node
📌 Important Hyperparameters (Control Overfitting)
🔹 Max Depth
Maximum depth of tree
Desired:
Small–moderate (e.g.,3–10)


🔹 Min Samples Split
Minimum samples required to split a node
Higher value → simpler tree
🔹 Min Samples Leaf
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node
Helps smooth predictions
🔹 Max Features
Number of features considered at each split
⚠️ Overfitting in Decision Trees
Problem:
Trees can memorize data
Very deep trees → poor generalization
Solution:
Pruning
Limiting depth
Ensemble methods (Random Forest)
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics
📌 For Classification
📌 For Regression
🧠 Advantages of Decision Tree
Easy to interpret
No feature scaling required
Handles non-linear data
Works with categorical & numerical data
❌ Disadvantages
Overfitting
Sensitive to small data changes
Lower accuracy than ensembles
📌 Assumptions (Minimal!)
👉 Decision Trees are assumption-light
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Decision Tree:
A tree-based model that splits data using feature conditions to make predictions.
Gini / Entropy:
Measures node impurity; lower values mean purer splits.
Overfitting:
Deep trees overfit; pruning and depth control fix this.
🔁 Comparison (Quick)
Decision Tree Hyperparameters
Hyperparameters control how the tree grows and help prevent overfitting.
📌 random_state
What it is:
Controls the randomness in the tree-building process.
Why it matters:
Ensures reproducible results
Same data + same random_state → same tree
Desired Practice:
Settoany fixed number (e.g.,42)


👉 Does not affect accuracy directly — only reproducibility.
📌 criterion
What it is:
Metric used to decide how to split nodes.
Classification:
gini (default)
entropy
Regression:
squared_error
Desired:
gini → faster
entropy → more information-theoretic
📌 max_depth (Most Important)
What it is:
Maximum depth of the tree.
Effect:
Large depth → overfitting
Small depth → underfitting
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
3 – 10


📌 min_samples_split
What it is:
Minimum number of samples required to split a node.
Effect:
Higher value → simpler tree
Lower value → complex tree
Typical Values:
2 (default),5,10


📌 min_samples_leaf
What it is:
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node.
Why important:
Smooths predictions
Reduces overfitting
Typical Values:
1, 2, 5


📌 max_features
What it is:
Number of features considered when looking for the best split.
Options:
None → all features
sqrt → √features (common for classification)
log2
Integer or float
Effect:
Smaller value → more randomness
Helps reduce overfitting
📌 max_leaf_nodes
What it is:
Maximum number of leaf nodes.
Effect:
Controls model complexity
Smaller value → simpler tree
✂️ Pruning (Very Important)
📌 What is Pruning?
Definition:
Pruning removes unnecessary branches to prevent overfitting.
Two types:
Pre-pruning
Post-pruning
🔹 Pre-Pruning (Early Stopping)
Done while building the tree using hyperparameters:
🔹 Post-Pruning (Cost Complexity Pruning)
📌 ccp_alpha
What it is:
Controls cost-complexity pruning.
How it works:
Penalizes complex trees
Removes branches that add little value
Effect:
Larger ccp_alpha → more pruning
Smaller ccp_alpha → deeper tree
Desired Practice:
Tuneusing cross-validation


📊 Example (Intuition)
DecisionTreeClassifier(
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Balanced tree
➡️ Less overfitting
➡️ Reproducible results
🧠 Key Takeaways
max_depth is the most important hyperparameter
Pruning controls overfitting
Decision Trees do not need scaling
Always tune hyperparameters using cross-validation
🎯 Interview One-Liners
random_state:
Ensures reproducibility of results.
Pruning:
Reduces overfitting by removing unnecessary branches.
max_depth:
Controls the complexity of the tree.
Handling Imbalanced Data in Decision Trees
An imbalanced dataset occurs when one class appears much more frequently than the other(s).
Example:
Class0:95%
Class1:5%


➡️ A naive model may always predict the majority class and still get high accuracy.
🚨 Why Imbalance Is a Problem
Model becomes biased toward majority class
Minority class is poorly predicted
Accuracy becomes misleading
🛠️ Techniques to Handle Imbalance in Decision Trees
📌 1. class_weight (Most Important)
What it is:
Assigns higher importance (penalty) to the minority class during splitting.
📐 How It Works
Misclassifying minority class is penalized more
Tree learns splits that focus on minority samples
✅ Common Options
class_weight="balanced"


Automatically assigns:
weight = total_samples / (n_classes × class_frequency)


🧠 When to Use
Strong class imbalance
Classification problems
When recall on minority class matters
📌 2. Frequency / Sample Weights (sample_weight)
What it is:
Assigns a weight to each training sample.
Minority class samples get higher weights
Similar effect to class_weight, but more flexible
📐 Example
sample_weight = [5if y==1else1for yin y_train]
model.fit(X_train, y_train, sample_weight=sample_weight)


🧠 When to Use
You want custom control
Unequal importance per observation
📌 3. Resampling Techniques
🔹 Oversampling (Increase Minority Class)
Duplicate minority samples
Or generate synthetic samples (SMOTE)
Pros:
Improves recall
Cons:
Risk of overfitting
🔹 Undersampling (Reduce Majority Class)
Remove majority samples
Pros:
Faster training
Cons:
Loss of information
📌 4. Adjust Tree Hyperparameters
🔹 min_samples_leaf
Larger value prevents tree from ignoring minority class
min_samples_leaf =5or10


🔹 max_depth
Restricts overfitting to majority class patterns
max_depth =4 –8


📌 5. Change Evaluation Metrics (Very Important)
❌ Avoid accuracy
✅ Use:
Recall
Precision
F1-score
ROC–AUC
PR–AUC (very good for imbalance)
📌 6. Threshold Tuning
Instead of default:
0.5


Use:
Lower threshold → higher recallfor minorityclass


📊 Example (Decision Tree with Imbalance Handling)
from sklearn.treeimport DecisionTreeClassifier

model = DecisionTreeClassifier(
    class_weight="balanced",
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Better minority class detection
➡️ Reduced bias
🧠 Comparison: class_weight vs Resampling
🎯 Interview One-Liners
class_weight:
Assigns higher penalty to minority class misclassification.
sample_weight:
Adjusts importance of individual samples.
Best practice:
Combine class weighting with proper evaluation metrics.
✅ Best Strategy (Real Projects)
Start with class_weight="balanced"
Tune tree depth & leaf size
Use Recall / F1 instead of Accuracy
Try SMOTE if needed
Validate with cross-validation
If you want next:
Imbalanced data in Random Forest
SMOTE explained simply
Confusion matrix interpretation
End-to-end example
Just say 👍
tell about over and under sampling with SMOTE algo
Got it 👍
Here are clear, Notion-ready notes on Over-sampling, Under-sampling, and the SMOTE algorithm, explained step-by-step with intuition, example, pros/cons, and best practices.
⚖️ Handling Imbalanced Data
Over-Sampling, Under-Sampling & SMOTE
📌 Imbalanced Dataset
Definition:
An imbalanced dataset is one where one class heavily dominates the others.
Example:
Class0 (Majority):950
Class1 (Minority):50


🔹 Under-Sampling
📌 What is Under-Sampling?
Definition:
Under-sampling reduces the majority class samples to balance the dataset.
📊 Example
Before:
Class0:950
Class1:50


After under-sampling:
Class0:50
Class1:50


✅ Advantages
Faster training
Simple to apply
Useful for very large datasets
❌ Disadvantages
Loss of information
Important patterns may be removed
🧠 When to Use
Extremely large datasets
Majority class is redundant
🔹 Over-Sampling
📌 What is Over-Sampling?
Definition:
Over-sampling increases the minority class samples to balance the dataset.
📊 Example (Naive Over-Sampling)
Before:
Class0:950
Class1:50


After over-sampling:
Class0:950
Class1:950 (duplicates)


✅ Advantages
No loss of majority data
Improves minority class recall
❌ Disadvantages
Overfitting due to duplicate samples
Increased training time
🧠 When to Use
Small to medium datasets
Minority class is very important
🤖 SMOTE (Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique)
📌 What is SMOTE?
Definition:
SMOTE is an advanced over-sampling technique that creates synthetic (new) minority samples, instead of duplicating existing ones.
🧠 Core Idea
“Create new minority points between existing ones.”
⚙️ How SMOTE Works (Step-by-Step)
Select a minority class sample
Find its K nearest minority neighbors
Pick one neighbor randomly
Create a new point along the line between them
📐 SMOTE Formula (Conceptual)
NewSample= x + rand(0,1) × (x_neighbor − x)


📊 Example
Minority samples:
(2,3), (3,4)


Synthetic sample:
(2.6,3.6)


➡️ Lies naturally between real samples
✅ Advantages of SMOTE
Avoids duplicate data
Reduces overfitting
Creates realistic minority samples
❌ Disadvantages of SMOTE
Can create noisy samples
Not suitable for categorical features (without variants)
Can cause class overlap
🧠 When to Use SMOTE
Minority class is very small
Feature space is continuous
Tree / Logistic / SVM models
⚠️ Important Rules (Very Important)
🚫 Never Apply SMOTE on Test Data
Correct Workflow:
1. Split data into train & test
2. Apply SMOTE only on training data
3. Train model
4. Evaluate on original test data


📊 Comparison Summary
🧪 SMOTE vs class_weight
👉 Often class_weight + SMOTE gives best results.
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Under-sampling:
Reduces majority class to balance data.
Over-sampling:
Increases minority class using duplication or synthesis.
SMOTE:
Creates synthetic minority samples using nearest neighbors.
✅ Best Practice (Real Projects)
Start with class_weight="balanced"
Try SMOTE on training data
Tune decision threshold
Use Recall / F1 / PR-AUC
Validate using cross-validation
Linear Regression 
Linear regression has single and multiple regression
Y = mx+c
y is the predicted value
m is slope 
c is the intercept
x is a feature
best fit line:
It minimises the errors between the predicted and actual values of the dependent variable
Optimization technique: They are used to find the best parameters for the model that minimizes the loss function 
Loss function calculates the errors of the model prediction - ordinary least squares
What is Linear Regression?
Linear Regression is a supervised learning algorithm used to model the relationship between:
Independent variable(s) (features)
Dependent variable (target)
It predicts continuous values.
🔹 Types of Linear Regression
1️⃣ Simple Linear Regression
Uses one feature
Y = mX + c


2️⃣ Multiple Linear Regression
Uses multiple features
Y = m₁X₁ + m₂X₂ + ... + mₙXₙ + c


📐 Equation Explanation
Y = mX + c


📊 What is Slope (m)?
Definition:
Slope represents how much Y changes when X increases by 1 unit.
Positive slope → Y increases with X
Negative slope → Y decreases with X
📍 What is Intercept (c)?
Definition:
Intercept is the value of Y when X = 0.
It shows where the regression line cuts the Y-axis.
📈 Best Fit Line
Definition:
The best fit line is the line that minimizes the total error between:
Actual values
Predicted values
This error is measured using a loss function.
📉 Error (Residual)
Error = Actual Value − Predicted Value


Each data point has a residual, and the goal is to make these as small as possible.
⚙️ Optimization Technique
Definition:
Optimization techniques are used to find the best values of m and c that minimize the loss function.
Common techniques:
Gradient Descent
Normal Equation
📌 Loss Function (Ordinary Least Squares – OLS)
Definition:
Loss function measures how wrong the model predictions are.
Linear Regression uses Ordinary Least Squares (OLS), which minimizes the sum of squared errors.
📐 OLS Formula
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )²


Where:
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
➡️ Squaring:
Removes negative signs
Penalizes large errors
🧮 Example (Simple Linear Regression)
Dataset
Assume the model learns:
m =10
c =30


Prediction Equation
Y =10X +30


Predict for X = 4
Y =10(4) +30
Y =70


➡️ Predicted Marks = 70
How statsmodels Handles Linear Regression (OLS)
statsmodels does the heavy mathematical lifting to find the optimal slope (m) and intercept (c) using Ordinary Least Squares (OLS).
But it doesn’t just stop there.
👉 Instead of only drawing a best-fit line, statsmodels gives a complete statistical diagnosis of your model — like a medical check-up for your data.
🧠 What Happens Behind the Scenes?
When you run:
model = sm.OLS(y, X).fit()


statsmodels performs the following steps:
🔹 1. Calculates the Coefficients (m and c)
What it does:
Finds the exact values of:
Slope (m) → effect of the feature on the target
Intercept (c) → baseline value when X = 0
Interpretation Example:
A slope of 2.5 means:
For every 1-unit increase in page traffic, sales increase by 2.5 units.
🔹 2. Measures Error (Standard Error)
What it does:
Calculates the Standard Error of each coefficient
Shows how much the coefficient would vary if the dataset changed
Why it matters:
Smaller standard error → more reliable estimate
Larger standard error → unstable coefficient
Intuition:
“How much does this slope wiggle if I collect new data?”
🔹 3. Tests Statistical Significance (p-value)
What it does:
Calculates a p-value for each coefficient
Tests whether the relationship is real or due to random noise
Rule of Thumb:
p-value <0.05  → statistically significant
p-value ≥0.05 → not significant


Interpretation Example:
A low p-value for page traffic means traffic genuinely impacts sales.
🔹 4. Quantifies Goodness of Fit (R² Score)
What it does:
Calculates R-squared
Measures how well the model explains the target variable
Meaning:
R² =0.80


➡️ 80% of the variation in sales is explained by page traffic
🔹 5. Computes Confidence Intervals
What it does:
Provides a range within which the true coefficient value likely lies
Example:
Slope = 2.5
95% CI = [2.1, 2.9]


➡️ You’re 95% confident the real effect is between 2.1 and 2.9
🔹 6. Residual Analysis
What it does:
Calculates residuals (errors)
Helps check:
Linearity
Homoscedasticity
Normality of errors
This validates model assumptions.
🧪 Why statsmodels Is Powerful
👉 That’s why statsmodels is preferred for statistical analysis, while sklearn focuses on prediction.
📌 Multicollinearity
Definition:
Multicollinearity occurs when two or more independent variables are highly correlated with each other in a regression model.
🔴 Why Is Multicollinearity a Problem?
Coefficient estimates become unstable
Difficult to understand the individual effect of each feature
Standard errors increase
p-values become unreliable
📊 Example
These two are highly correlated → causes multicollinearity.
📌 Variance Inflation Factor (VIF)
Definition:
VIF measures how much the variance of a regression coefficient is inflated due to multicollinearity.
📐 VIF Formula
VIF =1 / (1 − R²)


Where:
R² = R-squared value obtained by regressing one feature against all other features
📊 VIF Interpretation
🧠 Key Point
If a feature is not correlated with others → R² = 0 → VIF = 1
🛠️ How to Handle Multicollinearity
Remove highly correlated features
Combine features
Use PCA
Use regularization (Ridge, Lasso)
📌 p-value
Definition:
The p-value tells whether an independent variable is statistically significant in predicting the target variable.
🧪 What Does p-value Measure?
It tests the null hypothesis:
The feature has no effect on the target variable.
📊 p-value Interpretation
🧠 Why p-value Is Important
Identifies relevant features
Helps remove irrelevant variables
Improves model interpretability
Avoids overfitting
📈 Example
➡️ Page Traffic significantly impacts sales
➡️ Ad Spend may not meaningfully contribute
Regression Diagnostics (statsmodels)
These statistics help validate the assumptions of linear regression.
📌 Omnibus
Meaning:
Omnibus test checks whether the residuals are normally distributed.
It tests skewness + kurtosis together.
Used as a normality check for errors.
Desired Value:
Loweris better


👉 Interpretation is done using Prob(Omnibus), not the Omnibus value itself.
📌 Prob(Omnibus)
Meaning:
This is the p-value of the Omnibus test.
Null hypothesis: residuals are normally distributed
Desired Value:
Prob(Omnibus) >0.05  ✅


📌 Jarque–Bera (JB)
Meaning:
Another test for normality of residuals, based on:
Skewness
Kurtosis
More sensitive for larger samples.
Desired Value:
Loweris better


👉 Interpretation is done using Prob(JB).
📌 Prob(JB)
Meaning:
p-value of the Jarque–Bera test.
Desired Value:
Prob(JB) >0.05  ✅


📌 Durbin–Watson
Meaning:
Tests for autocorrelation in residuals (especially important in time-series data).
Range:
0to4


Desired Value:
≈ 2  ✅


📌 Condition Number (Cond. No.)
Meaning:
Measures multicollinearity and numerical stability of the model.
High value → features are highly correlated
Can make coefficients unreliable
Desired Value:
< 30  ✅


🧠 Quick Summary Table
We will split the data set into different parts: 
Training, validation and Test
Training: Fir the model
Validation: compare the different model
Test: Test the model
Regularization: Technique to tackle the overfitting
L1 Regularization
L2 Regularization
Elastic Net
Loss Function

A loss function measures how wrong a model’s predictions are by calculating the difference between the actual values and the predicted values
Intuition
“Loss is the penalty the model gets for being wrong.”
Low loss → good predictions
High loss → poor predictions
The goal of training is to minimize the loss function.
🔗 Role in Machine Learning
Model makes predictions
Loss function computes error
Optimizer updates parameters
Loss decreases over time
📊 Common Loss Functions
🔹 1. Mean Squared Error (MSE) – Regression
Formula:
MSE = (1 / n) × Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )²


Why used?
Penalizes large errors heavily
Smooth and differentiable
🔹 2. Mean Absolute Error (MAE) – Regression
Formula:
MAE = (1 / n) × Σ | yᵢ − ŷᵢ |


Why used?
Robust to outliers
Easy to interpret
🔹 3. Log Loss (Binary Cross-Entropy) – Classification
Formula:
Loss = −[ ylog(p) + (1 − y)log(1 − p) ]


Why used?
Measures probability accuracy
Punishes confident wrong predictions
🔹 4. Hinge Loss – SVM
Formula:
Loss = max(0,1 − yŷ)


Used in Support Vector Machines.
L1 Regularization (Lasso)
📌 Definition
L1 Regularization adds the absolute value of coefficients as a penalty to the loss function.
📐 Formula
Linear Regression with L1
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ Σ |wᵢ|


🧠 Key Property
👉 Shrinks some coefficients exactly to zero
➡️ Performs feature selection automatically
✅ Advantages
Produces sparse models
Removes irrelevant features
Improves interpretability
❌ Disadvantages
Can be unstable when features are highly correlated
🧪 When to Use
High-dimensional data
When feature selection is important
🔹 2. L2 Regularization (Ridge)
📌 Definition
L2 Regularization adds the square of coefficients as a penalty.
📐 Formula
Linear Regression with L2
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ Σ wᵢ²


🧠 Key Property
👉 Shrinks coefficients close to zero but never exactly zero
➡️ Keeps all features
✅ Advantages
Handles multicollinearity well
Produces stable models
❌ Disadvantages
No feature elimination
🧪 When to Use
When all features are important
When multicollinearity exists
🔹 3. Elastic Net
📌 Definition
Elastic Net is a combination of L1 and L2 regularization.
📐 Formula
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ₁ Σ |wᵢ| + λ₂ Σ wᵢ²


🧠 Key Property
Performs feature selection (L1)
Maintains stability (L2)
✅ Advantages
Best of both worlds
Works well with correlated features
❌ Disadvantages
More hyperparameters to tune
🧪 When to Use
Large feature sets
Highly correlated features
When Lasso selects only one feature randomly
⚙️ Regularization Strength (λ)
Controls penalty intensity
Larger λ → simpler model
Smaller λ → complex model
👉 Tuned using cross-validation
📊 Comparison Table
Regularization:
Adds a penalty to reduce overfitting.
L1:
Performs feature selection by driving coefficients to zero.
L2:
Reduces coefficient magnitude and handles multicollinearity.
Elastic Net:
Combines L1 and L2 to balance sparsity and stability.
Article : https://www.analyticsvidhya.com/blog/2022/08/regularization-in-machine-learning/

=== PAGE: Deep Learning ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Deep-Learning-2fefca3384fc8074bd6bcd4c948a73a7


Deep Learning is a subset of Machine Learning that uses Neural Networks with multiple layers to learn patterns from data
This works both on structured and unstructured data
A neuron is the basic building block of a neural network.

Neuron and components of neurons
inputs : Features provided to the model
weights: Importance assigned to each input, Learned during training
Higher weight = more influence
Dot product = Σ (X · W) + b
b = bias (helps shift the output)
Activation function: 
Applies a transformation to decide whether the neuron should activate.
Introduces non-linearity.
Output: Final value passed to the next neuron or layer.
Flow 

inputs → weights →dot product(inputs and weights) → activation function → op
Dot Product in Neural Network
1. Linear Combination (Before Activation)
Formula:
z = Σ (X · W) + b
Vector form:
z = WᵀX + b
Where:
X → Input features
W → Weights
b → Bias
z → Linear output (also called logit)
This step is called the linear transformation.
2. Activation Function
The linear output is passed to an activation function:
ŷ = f(z)
Full equation:
ŷ = f(WᵀX + b)
Where:
f → Activation function
ŷ → Predicted output
Depending on the Problem Type
A. Regression
Usually, no activation (or linear activation).
ŷ = z
Output → Continuous value
B. Binary Classification
Use Sigmoid activation:
σ(z) = 1 / (1 + e^(-z))
So,
ŷ = σ(WᵀX + b)
Output → Probability between 0 and 1
C. Multi-Class Classification
Use Softmax activation:
Softmax(z_i) = e^(z_i) / Σ e^(z_j)
Output → Probability distribution across classes
Full Flow of a Single Neuron
InputX
   ↓
Dot Product (W · X)
   ↓
Add Bias (+ b)
   ↓
Activation Functionf()
   ↓
ŷ (Prediction)
Activation functions
1. Step Function
Definition:
Outputs either 0 or 1 based on a threshold.
Problem:
❌ Not differentiable → cannot use backpropagation effectively.
👉 Mostly historical.
🔹 2. Linear Function
f(x) = x


Problem:
Stacking linear layers still produces a linear model.
❌ Cannot learn complex relationships.
🔹 3. Sigmoid
Range: (0, 1)


Uses:
Binary classification
Output layer
Problems:
Vanishing gradient
Slow training
🔹 4. Tanh
Range: (-1, 1)


✅ Zero-centered → better than sigmoid
Still suffers from:
❌ Vanishing gradient
🔹 5. ReLU (Rectified Linear Unit)
f(x) =max(0, x)


👉 Most widely used activation.
neural Network

A Neural Network is a structured combination of neurons arranged in layers that work together to make predictions.


Nodes → neuron
The number of hidden layers determines the architecture 
🔁 Forward Propagation
📌 Definition
Forward propagation is the movement of data from input → hidden → output layer.
Steps:
Inputs enter network
Multiply by weights
Apply activation
Pass forward
Generate prediction
👉 Happens during inference and training.
🔄 Backpropagation
📌 Definition
Backpropagation is the process of updating weights by propagating the error backward through the network.
⚙️ How It Works
1️⃣ Calculate loss
2️⃣ Compute gradients
3️⃣ Update weights using optimizer
4️⃣ Reduce error
👉 Repeated many times → model improves.
🎯 Goal:
MinimizeLossFunction

Loss Functions in Neural Networks
📌 What is a Loss Function?
Definition:
A loss function measures the difference between the actual value and the neural network’s prediction.
👉 It tells the model:
“How wrong are your predictions?”
🎯 Goal of Training
MinimizeLoss →ImprovePredictions →BetterModel


Loss guides backpropagation, which updates weights to reduce errors.
🧠 Why Loss Functions Are Important
Directs the learning process
Helps optimize weights
Impacts model performance
Different problems require different loss functions
👉 Choosing the wrong loss function can ruin model accuracy.
🔹 Types of Loss Functions
👉 Mainly depend on the problem type:
🔥 1. Binary Cross-Entropy (Log Loss)
📌 What is It?
Used when the target has two classes:
0/1
Yes/No
Spam/NotSpam


📐 Formula
Loss = −[ ylog(p) + (1 − y)log(1 − p) ]


Where:
y = actual label (0 or 1)
p = predicted probability
🧠 Intuition
✅ Rewards confident correct predictions
❌ Punishes confident wrong predictions heavily
Example:
⭐ Why It Works Well
Perfect for probability outputs
Smooth gradient → stable training
Works great with sigmoid activation
⚠️ Important
👉 Output layer should use:
Sigmoid Activation


🔥 2. Categorical Cross-Entropy
📌 What is It?
Used for multi-class classification:
Example:
Cat/Dog/Horse
Red/Blue/Green


📐 Formula
Loss = − Σ yᵢ log(pᵢ)


Where:
yᵢ = actual class indicator
pᵢ = predicted probability
🧠 Intuition
The loss increases when the model assigns low probability to the correct class.
⭐ Works Best With
Softmax Activation


Softmax ensures:
Sumofprobabilities=1


⚠️ Types
✅ One-Hot Encoded Labels → Use:
CategoricalCrossentropy


✅ Integer Labels → Use:
SparseCategoricalCrossentropy


(Saves memory)
🔥 3. Mean Squared Error (MSE)
📌 What is It?
Used mostly for regression problems.
📐 Formula
MSE = (1 / n) × Σ ( y − ŷ )²


🧠 Intuition
Squares errors → large mistakes punished more
Encourages the model to avoid big errors
✅ Advantages
Smooth gradient
Easy to optimize
❌ Disadvantages
Very sensitive to outliers
Example:
Error =10 → Squared =100 😬


🔥 4. Mean Absolute Error (MAE)
📌 What is It?
Measures the average absolute difference between predictions and actual values.
📐 Formula
MAE = (1 / n) × Σ | y − ŷ |


🧠 Intuition
Treats all errors equally.
👉 No extra punishment for large errors.
✅ Advantages
Robust to outliers
Easy to interpret
❌ Disadvantages
Not smoothly differentiable
Training can be slower
Gradient Descent
📌 What is Gradient Descent?
Definition:
Gradient Descent is an optimization algorithm used to minimize the loss function by adjusting the model’s weights iteratively.
👉 It helps the model find the best parameters that reduce prediction error.
🧠 Intuition
Imagine standing on a mountain in heavy fog 🌫️
Your goal is to reach the lowest point (minimum).
👉 What do you do?
Check the slope around you
Take a small step downhill
Repeat until you reach the bottom
That is exactly how gradient descent works.
⚙️ How Gradient Descent Works
Steps:
1️⃣ Initialize weights randomly
2️⃣ Calculate loss
3️⃣ Compute gradient (slope of loss curve)
4️⃣ Update weights
5️⃣ Repeat until loss stops decreasing
📐 Weight Update Formula
W_new = W_old − α × (gradient)
Where:
α (alpha) = learning rate
gradient = direction of steepest increase
👉 Subtracting moves us toward the minimum.
📌 Learning Rate (VERY Important)
What is it?
Controls how big a step we take toward the minimum.
🚨 If Learning Rate is Too Large:
Overshoots minimum
Training becomes unstable
May never converge
🚨 If Learning Rate is Too Small:
Training becomes very slow
Might get stuck in local minima
✅ Ideal Learning Rate:
0.001 –0.01 (commonly used)
🔥 Types of Gradient Descent
🔹 1. Batch Gradient Descent
📌 Definition
Uses the entire dataset to compute the gradient before updating weights.
⚙️ How it Works:
Processall training examples → compute gradient →update weights
✅ Advantages
Stable convergence
Accurate gradient
❌ Disadvantages
Very slow for large datasets
High memory usage
🧠 Best For:
Small datasets
Convex optimization problems
🔹 2. Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD)
📌 Definition
Updates weights using one training example at a time.
⚙️ How it Works:
Pick1 sample → compute gradient →update weights
✅ Advantages
Very fast
Works well for large datasets
Escapes local minima due to randomness
❌ Disadvantages
Noisy updates
Loss fluctuates
Less stable convergence
🧠 Best For:
Deep learning
Online learning
Massive datasets
🔹 3. Mini-Batch Gradient Descent ⭐ (Most Used)
📌 Definition
A hybrid approach that uses small batches of data.
Example:
Batch size =32,64,128
⚙️ How it Works:
Split dataset → process small batch → update weights
✅ Advantages
Faster than batch GD
More stable than SGD
Efficient on GPUs
👉 Industry standard
❌ Disadvantages
Requires tuning batch size
📊 Quick Comparison

Learning rate is a hyperparameter that controls how big a step the model takes while updating weights during training.


What is an Optimizer?
An optimizer is an algorithm that updates model weights to minimize the loss function.
Gradient Descent with Momentum
📌 Why Momentum is Needed
Basic GD problems:
Slow convergence
Oscillates in narrow valleys
Can get stuck in local minima
👉 Momentum helps speed up learning and smooth oscillations.
🧠 Intuition
Imagine rolling a ball down a hill 🏔️
Without momentum:
The ball moves slowly.
It zig-zags.
With momentum:
The ball builds speed.
It moves smoothly.
It resists small bumps.
📐 Formula
Step 1: Update Velocity
V = βV − α × gradient
Step 2: Update Weights
W = W + V
Where:
V = velocity (memory of past gradients)
β = momentum coefficient (usually 0.9)
α = learning rate
📌 Momentum Parameter (β)
Controls how much past gradients influence current update
Typical value:
β = 0.9
If β is:
Too small → behaves like normal GD
Too large → may overshoot
🧠 What Momentum Does
✅ Accelerates learning in correct direction
✅ Reduces oscillations
✅ Escapes shallow local minima
✅ Faster convergence
Comparison
AdaGrad (Adaptive Gradient Algorithm)
📌 What is AdaGrad?
AdaGrad is an optimization algorithm that adapts the learning rate for each parameter individually.
👉 Parameters that get large gradients receive smaller learning rates.
👉 Parameters with small gradients receive larger learning rates.
🧠 Why AdaGrad Was Introduced
Problem with normal Gradient Descent:
Uses the same learning rate for all parameters.
Some features may update too much.
Some may update too slowly.
👉 AdaGrad solves this by adjusting learning rate automatically.
⚙️ How AdaGrad Works
Instead of using:
W = W − α × gradient
AdaGrad modifies it to:
W = W − (α / √G + ε) × gradient
Where:
G = sum of squared past gradients
α = learning rate
ε = small constant (e.g., 1e-8) to avoid division by zero
📐 Step-by-Step Formula
1️⃣ Accumulate squared gradients:
G = G + (gradient)²
2️⃣ Update weights:
W = W − (α / √G + ε) × gradient
🧠 Intuition
If a parameter keeps receiving large gradients:
👉 G becomes large
👉 Learning rate becomes smaller
👉 Updates slow down
If a parameter receives small gradients:
👉 G remains small
👉 Learning rate remains relatively large
📊 Why AdaGrad Is Useful
✅ Works well for sparse data
✅ Good for NLP problems
✅ No need to manually tune learning rate aggressively
Vanishing gradient 
This occurs during the process of minimizing the loss function, when gradients shrink so much during backpropagation that weight updates become negligible, preventing effective learning
Full Picture of What’s Happening
When we train a neural network:
Step 1️⃣ — Forward Pass
Compute prediction.
Step 2️⃣ — Compute Loss
Measure error.
Step 3️⃣ — Backpropagation
Compute gradients:
∂Loss∂Weight\frac{\partial Loss}{\partial Weight}
∂Weight∂Loss
Step 4️⃣ — Update Weights
W=W−α⋅gradientW = W - \alpha \cdot gradient
W=W−α⋅gradient
🚨 Where Vanishing Gradient Happens
It happens in Step 3 (Backpropagation).
When gradients are computed, they are calculated using the chain rule:
gradient=g1×g2×g3×...×gngradient = g_1 \times g_2 \times g_3 \times ... \times g_n
gradient=g1×g2×g3×...×gn
If each gi<1g_i < 1gi<1, then:
small × small × small × small
→ becomes extremely small.
🔴 What Happens Then?
Gradient becomes:
≈0\approx 0
≈0
So update becomes:
W=W−α⋅0W = W - \alpha \cdot 0
W=W−α⋅0
W=WW = W
W=W
👉 The weight stops changing.
👉 The model stops learning in earlier layers.
👉 Loss stops decreasing effectively.
Full Picture of What’s Happening
When we train a neural network:
Step 1️⃣ — Forward Pass
Compute prediction.
Step 2️⃣ — Compute Loss
Measure error.
Step 3️⃣ — Backpropagation
Compute gradients:
∂Loss∂Weight\frac{\partial Loss}{\partial Weight}
∂Weight∂Loss
Step 4️⃣ — Update Weights
W=W−α⋅gradientW = W - \alpha \cdot gradient
W=W−α⋅gradient
🚨 Where Vanishing Gradient Happens
It happens in Step 3 (Backpropagation).
When gradients are computed, they are calculated using the chain rule:
gradient=g1×g2×g3×...×gngradient = g_1 \times g_2 \times g_3 \times ... \times g_n
gradient=g1×g2×g3×...×gn
If each gi<1g_i < 1gi<1, then:
small × small × small × small
→ becomes extremely small.
🔴 What Happens Then?
Gradient becomes:
≈0\approx 0
≈0
So update becomes:
W=W−α⋅0W = W - \alpha \cdot 0
W=W−α⋅0
W=WW = W
W=W
👉 The weight stops changing.
👉 The model stops learning in earlier layers.
👉 Loss stops decreasing effectively.
What is Overfitting?
Overfitting happens when:
Model performs very well on training data
Performs poorly on validation/test data
It memorizes patterns instead of learning generalizable features.
📊 Signs of Overfitting
Training loss ↓
Validation loss ↑
Training accuracy very high
Validation accuracy much lower
🔥 How to Fix Overfitting in Deep Learning
✅ 1️⃣ Regularization (L1 / L2)
Add penalty to loss function.
L2 (Most Common in DL)
Loss=OriginalLoss+λ∑W2Loss = OriginalLoss + \lambda \sum W^2
Loss=OriginalLoss+λ∑W2
Effect:
Keeps weights small
Reduces model complexity
Prevents memorization
In practice:
weight_decay (PyTorch)
kernel_regularizer (Keras)
✅ 2️⃣ Dropout (Very Powerful)
📌 What It Does
Randomly turns off neurons during training.
Example:
Dropoutrate=0.5
Means:
50% neurons randomly ignored each iteration
🔥 Why It Works
Forces network to:
Not depend on specific neurons
Learn robust distributed features
Prevents co-adaptation.
🎯 Most Common Fix in Deep Learning
Dense → ReLU → Dropout → Dense
✅ 3️⃣ Early Stopping
Stop training when validation loss starts increasing.
Instead of training for fixed epochs:
Monitor validation loss
Stop when it stops improving
Prevents memorization phase.
✅ 4️⃣ Data Augmentation (Very Effective for Images)
Artificially increase dataset size.
Examples:
Flip
Rotate
Crop
Add noise
Color jitter
More data → better generalization.
✅ 5️⃣ Reduce Model Complexity
If model is too large:
Too many layers
Too many neurons
Then:
Reduce hidden units
Reduce layers
Simpler model → less overfitting.
✅ 6️⃣ Batch Normalization
Though mainly for gradient stability, it also:
Adds slight regularization
Reduces overfitting
✅ 7️⃣ Increase Training Data
Best solution if possible.
More data → model learns true distribution.
✅ 8️⃣ Cross-Validation
Helps detect overfitting early.
🧠 Deep Learning Specific Tricks

URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Regularization-2f9fca3384fc80f3b5d4fadf1ca2b320

We will split the data set into different parts: 
Training, validation and Test
Training: Fir the model
Validation: compare the different model
Test: Test the model
Regularization: Technique to tackle the overfitting
L1 Regularization
L2 Regularization
Elastic Net
Loss Function

A loss function measures how wrong a model’s predictions are by calculating the difference between the actual values and the predicted values
Intuition
“Loss is the penalty the model gets for being wrong.”
Low loss → good predictions
High loss → poor predictions
The goal of training is to minimize the loss function.
🔗 Role in Machine Learning
Model makes predictions
Loss function computes error
Optimizer updates parameters
Loss decreases over time
📊 Common Loss Functions
🔹 1. Mean Squared Error (MSE) – Regression
Formula:
MSE = (1 / n) × Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )²


Why used?
Penalizes large errors heavily
Smooth and differentiable
🔹 2. Mean Absolute Error (MAE) – Regression
Formula:
MAE = (1 / n) × Σ | yᵢ − ŷᵢ |


Why used?
Robust to outliers
Easy to interpret
🔹 3. Log Loss (Binary Cross-Entropy) – Classification
Formula:
Loss = −[ ylog(p) + (1 − y)log(1 − p) ]


Why used?
Measures probability accuracy
Punishes confident wrong predictions
🔹 4. Hinge Loss – SVM
Formula:
Loss = max(0,1 − yŷ)


Used in Support Vector Machines.
L1 Regularization (Lasso)
📌 Definition
L1 Regularization adds the absolute value of coefficients as a penalty to the loss function.
📐 Formula
Linear Regression with L1
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ Σ |wᵢ|


🧠 Key Property
👉 Shrinks some coefficients exactly to zero
➡️ Performs feature selection automatically
✅ Advantages
Produces sparse models
Removes irrelevant features
Improves interpretability
❌ Disadvantages
Can be unstable when features are highly correlated
🧪 When to Use
High-dimensional data
When feature selection is important
🔹 2. L2 Regularization (Ridge)
📌 Definition
L2 Regularization adds the square of coefficients as a penalty.
📐 Formula
Linear Regression with L2
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ Σ wᵢ²


🧠 Key Property
👉 Shrinks coefficients close to zero but never exactly zero
➡️ Keeps all features
✅ Advantages
Handles multicollinearity well
Produces stable models
❌ Disadvantages
No feature elimination
🧪 When to Use
When all features are important
When multicollinearity exists
🔹 3. Elastic Net
📌 Definition
Elastic Net is a combination of L1 and L2 regularization.
📐 Formula
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )² + λ₁ Σ |wᵢ| + λ₂ Σ wᵢ²


🧠 Key Property
Performs feature selection (L1)
Maintains stability (L2)
✅ Advantages
Best of both worlds
Works well with correlated features
❌ Disadvantages
More hyperparameters to tune
🧪 When to Use
Large feature sets
Highly correlated features
When Lasso selects only one feature randomly
⚙️ Regularization Strength (λ)
Controls penalty intensity
Larger λ → simpler model
Smaller λ → complex model
👉 Tuned using cross-validation
📊 Comparison Table
Regularization:
Adds a penalty to reduce overfitting.
L1:
Performs feature selection by driving coefficients to zero.
L2:
Reduces coefficient magnitude and handles multicollinearity.
Elastic Net:
Combines L1 and L2 to balance sparsity and stability.
Article : https://www.analyticsvidhya.com/blog/2022/08/regularization-in-machine-learning/
