KNN
Definition:
K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) is a supervised, non-parametric algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It makes predictions based on the K closest data points to a new input.
How Does KNN Work?
Step-by-Step Process
Choose K
Number of nearest neighbors (e.g., K = 3, 5)
Calculate Distance
Compute distance between the new point and all training points
(commonly Euclidean distance)
Select K Nearest Neighbors
Pick the K points with the smallest distances
Make Prediction
Classification → majority vote
Regression → average of values
Distance Formula (Most Common)
Euclidean Distance
d = √[ (x₁ − y₁)² + (x₂ − y₂)² + ... + (xₙ − yₙ)² ]


📊 KNN for Classification
Example:
K = 5
3 neighbors → Class A
2 neighbors → Class B
➡️ Predicted class = A
📈 KNN for Regression
Example:
K = 4
Neighbor values:
10, 12, 14, 16


➡️ Prediction = (10 + 12 + 14 + 16) / 4 = 13
🔢 Choosing the Value of K
👉 Usually found using cross-validation
⭐ Advantages of KNN
Simple to understand
No training phase
Works well with small datasets
⚠️ Disadvantages of KNN
Slow for large datasets
Sensitive to noise
Requires feature scaling
High memory usage
❗ Why Scaling is Important in KNN
KNN is distance-based, so features with large values dominate.
➡️ Always use StandardScaler or MinMaxScaler
🧠 When to Use KNN
✅ Small to medium datasets
✅ Low-dimensional data
❌ Very large datasets
❌ High-dimensional data (curse of dimensionality)
Logistic Regression:
Used for a classification problem based on linear regression
odds ratio: 
log loss function -? perfect model will be 0
Logistic Regression (statsmodels) — Interpretation & Diagnostics
Logistic Regression is used when the target variable is categorical (usually binary: 0/1, Yes/No).
Instead of predicting a value, it predicts a probability using the log-odds (logit) function.
📌 Logistic Regression Equation
log(p / (1 − p)) = β₀ + β₁X₁ + β₂X₂ + ... + βₙXₙ


Where:
p = probability of class 1
β = coefficients
X = features
🔍 statsmodels Logistic Regression Summary — Key Metrics
When you run:
model = sm.Logit(y, X).fit()


You get the following diagnostics 👇
📌 Coefficients (β)
Meaning:
Represents the change in log-odds for a 1-unit increase in the feature.
Interpretation Tip:
Positive β → increases probability of class 1
Negative β → decreases probability of class 1
📌 Odds Ratio (exp(β))
Meaning:
Exponentiating coefficients gives odds ratios.
OddsRatio= e^β


Interpretation:
OR > 1 → feature increases odds
OR < 1 → feature decreases odds
📌 p-value (Very Important)
Meaning:
Tests whether a feature is statistically significant in predicting the target class.
Null Hypothesis:
Feature has no effect on the outcome.
Desired Value:
p-value <0.05  ✅


📌 Log-Likelihood (LL)
Meaning:
Measures how well the model fits the data.
Higher (less negative) → better fit
Used for model comparison, not absolute interpretation.
📌 Log-Likelihood Ratio (LLR)
Meaning:
Compares the fitted model against a null model (no predictors).
Desired:
Large value + smallp-value


📌 LLR p-value
Meaning:
Tests whether at least one predictor significantly improves the model.
Desired Value:
LLRp-value <0.05  ✅


➡️ Confirms the model is better than guessing.
📌 Pseudo R² (McFadden’s R²)
Meaning:
Indicates how well the model explains the outcome
(Not the same as linear regression R²)
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
👉 Even 0.3 is considered very good in logistic regression.
📌 Convergence
Meaning:
Indicates whether the optimization algorithm successfully found optimal coefficients.
Desired:
converged:True  ✅


❌ False → model may be unreliable.
🧪 Assumption Checks for Logistic Regression
📌 Multicollinearity (VIF)
Same as Linear Regression
High correlation between features inflates variance
Check using VIF
Desired:
VIF <5 (preferably <3)


📌 Linearity of Log-Odds
Meaning:
Logistic regression assumes a linear relationship between predictors and log-odds, not the target itself.
❌ Violations → poor predictions
📌 Independence of Observations
Each data point must be independent
No repeated or grouped observations
🧠 What Logistic Regression Does NOT Use
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics (After Training)
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Logistic Regression:
Predicts probabilities using log-odds for classification problems.
p-value:
Tells whether a feature significantly affects the probability of the outcome.
Pseudo R²:
Measures model fit but is not interpreted like linear regression R².
Decision Tree
A Decision Tree is a supervised learning algorithm used for:
Classification
Regression
It works by splitting data into smaller subsets based on feature conditions, forming a tree-like structure.
🧠 Core Idea (Intuition)
“Ask a sequence of yes/no questions to reach a decision.”
Each internal node = question
Each branch = answer
Each leaf node = final prediction
⚙️ How Decision Tree Works
Choose the best feature to split the data
Split data to maximize purity
Repeat recursively
Stop when:
Node is pure
Max depth reached
Minimum samples reached
📐 Splitting Criteria (Very Important)
📌 1. Gini Impurity (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures how impure a node is.
Formula:
Gini =1 − Σ (pᵢ)²


Desired Value:
Lower isbetter (0 = pure node)


📌 2. Entropy (Classification)
Meaning:
Measures disorder or randomness in a node.
Formula:
Entropy = − Σ pᵢ log₂(pᵢ)


Desired Value:
Loweris better


📌 3. Information Gain
Meaning:
Measures how much entropy is reduced after a split.
Formula:
InformationGain=Entropy(parent) −WeightedEntropy(children)


Desired Value:
Higheris better


📌 4. Variance Reduction (Regression Trees)
Meaning:
Used in Decision Tree Regression.
Tree splits data to minimize variance in target values.
Desired:
Lower variance in leaf nodes


📊 Example (Classification)
Dataset
Tree Rule
IFStudent=Yes→Buy=Yes
ELSE→Buy=No


➡️ Final prediction comes from leaf node
📌 Important Hyperparameters (Control Overfitting)
🔹 Max Depth
Maximum depth of tree
Desired:
Small–moderate (e.g.,3–10)


🔹 Min Samples Split
Minimum samples required to split a node
Higher value → simpler tree
🔹 Min Samples Leaf
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node
Helps smooth predictions
🔹 Max Features
Number of features considered at each split
⚠️ Overfitting in Decision Trees
Problem:
Trees can memorize data
Very deep trees → poor generalization
Solution:
Pruning
Limiting depth
Ensemble methods (Random Forest)
📊 Model Evaluation Metrics
📌 For Classification
📌 For Regression
🧠 Advantages of Decision Tree
Easy to interpret
No feature scaling required
Handles non-linear data
Works with categorical & numerical data
❌ Disadvantages
Overfitting
Sensitive to small data changes
Lower accuracy than ensembles
📌 Assumptions (Minimal!)
👉 Decision Trees are assumption-light
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Decision Tree:
A tree-based model that splits data using feature conditions to make predictions.
Gini / Entropy:
Measures node impurity; lower values mean purer splits.
Overfitting:
Deep trees overfit; pruning and depth control fix this.
🔁 Comparison (Quick)
Decision Tree Hyperparameters
Hyperparameters control how the tree grows and help prevent overfitting.
📌 random_state
What it is:
Controls the randomness in the tree-building process.
Why it matters:
Ensures reproducible results
Same data + same random_state → same tree
Desired Practice:
Settoany fixed number (e.g.,42)


👉 Does not affect accuracy directly — only reproducibility.
📌 criterion
What it is:
Metric used to decide how to split nodes.
Classification:
gini (default)
entropy
Regression:
squared_error
Desired:
gini → faster
entropy → more information-theoretic
📌 max_depth (Most Important)
What it is:
Maximum depth of the tree.
Effect:
Large depth → overfitting
Small depth → underfitting
Desired Values (Rule of Thumb):
3 – 10


📌 min_samples_split
What it is:
Minimum number of samples required to split a node.
Effect:
Higher value → simpler tree
Lower value → complex tree
Typical Values:
2 (default),5,10


📌 min_samples_leaf
What it is:
Minimum samples allowed in a leaf node.
Why important:
Smooths predictions
Reduces overfitting
Typical Values:
1, 2, 5


📌 max_features
What it is:
Number of features considered when looking for the best split.
Options:
None → all features
sqrt → √features (common for classification)
log2
Integer or float
Effect:
Smaller value → more randomness
Helps reduce overfitting
📌 max_leaf_nodes
What it is:
Maximum number of leaf nodes.
Effect:
Controls model complexity
Smaller value → simpler tree
✂️ Pruning (Very Important)
📌 What is Pruning?
Definition:
Pruning removes unnecessary branches to prevent overfitting.
Two types:
Pre-pruning
Post-pruning
🔹 Pre-Pruning (Early Stopping)
Done while building the tree using hyperparameters:
🔹 Post-Pruning (Cost Complexity Pruning)
📌 ccp_alpha
What it is:
Controls cost-complexity pruning.
How it works:
Penalizes complex trees
Removes branches that add little value
Effect:
Larger ccp_alpha → more pruning
Smaller ccp_alpha → deeper tree
Desired Practice:
Tuneusing cross-validation


📊 Example (Intuition)
DecisionTreeClassifier(
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Balanced tree
➡️ Less overfitting
➡️ Reproducible results
🧠 Key Takeaways
max_depth is the most important hyperparameter
Pruning controls overfitting
Decision Trees do not need scaling
Always tune hyperparameters using cross-validation
🎯 Interview One-Liners
random_state:
Ensures reproducibility of results.
Pruning:
Reduces overfitting by removing unnecessary branches.
max_depth:
Controls the complexity of the tree.
Handling Imbalanced Data in Decision Trees
An imbalanced dataset occurs when one class appears much more frequently than the other(s).
Example:
Class0:95%
Class1:5%


➡️ A naive model may always predict the majority class and still get high accuracy.
🚨 Why Imbalance Is a Problem
Model becomes biased toward majority class
Minority class is poorly predicted
Accuracy becomes misleading
🛠️ Techniques to Handle Imbalance in Decision Trees
📌 1. class_weight (Most Important)
What it is:
Assigns higher importance (penalty) to the minority class during splitting.
📐 How It Works
Misclassifying minority class is penalized more
Tree learns splits that focus on minority samples
✅ Common Options
class_weight="balanced"


Automatically assigns:
weight = total_samples / (n_classes × class_frequency)


🧠 When to Use
Strong class imbalance
Classification problems
When recall on minority class matters
📌 2. Frequency / Sample Weights (sample_weight)
What it is:
Assigns a weight to each training sample.
Minority class samples get higher weights
Similar effect to class_weight, but more flexible
📐 Example
sample_weight = [5if y==1else1for yin y_train]
model.fit(X_train, y_train, sample_weight=sample_weight)


🧠 When to Use
You want custom control
Unequal importance per observation
📌 3. Resampling Techniques
🔹 Oversampling (Increase Minority Class)
Duplicate minority samples
Or generate synthetic samples (SMOTE)
Pros:
Improves recall
Cons:
Risk of overfitting
🔹 Undersampling (Reduce Majority Class)
Remove majority samples
Pros:
Faster training
Cons:
Loss of information
📌 4. Adjust Tree Hyperparameters
🔹 min_samples_leaf
Larger value prevents tree from ignoring minority class
min_samples_leaf =5or10


🔹 max_depth
Restricts overfitting to majority class patterns
max_depth =4 –8


📌 5. Change Evaluation Metrics (Very Important)
❌ Avoid accuracy
✅ Use:
Recall
Precision
F1-score
ROC–AUC
PR–AUC (very good for imbalance)
📌 6. Threshold Tuning
Instead of default:
0.5


Use:
Lower threshold → higher recallfor minorityclass


📊 Example (Decision Tree with Imbalance Handling)
from sklearn.treeimport DecisionTreeClassifier

model = DecisionTreeClassifier(
    class_weight="balanced",
    max_depth=5,
    min_samples_leaf=10,
    random_state=42
)


➡️ Better minority class detection
➡️ Reduced bias
🧠 Comparison: class_weight vs Resampling
🎯 Interview One-Liners
class_weight:
Assigns higher penalty to minority class misclassification.
sample_weight:
Adjusts importance of individual samples.
Best practice:
Combine class weighting with proper evaluation metrics.
✅ Best Strategy (Real Projects)
Start with class_weight="balanced"
Tune tree depth & leaf size
Use Recall / F1 instead of Accuracy
Try SMOTE if needed
Validate with cross-validation
If you want next:
Imbalanced data in Random Forest
SMOTE explained simply
Confusion matrix interpretation
End-to-end example
Just say 👍
tell about over and under sampling with SMOTE algo
Got it 👍
Here are clear, Notion-ready notes on Over-sampling, Under-sampling, and the SMOTE algorithm, explained step-by-step with intuition, example, pros/cons, and best practices.
⚖️ Handling Imbalanced Data
Over-Sampling, Under-Sampling & SMOTE
📌 Imbalanced Dataset
Definition:
An imbalanced dataset is one where one class heavily dominates the others.
Example:
Class0 (Majority):950
Class1 (Minority):50


🔹 Under-Sampling
📌 What is Under-Sampling?
Definition:
Under-sampling reduces the majority class samples to balance the dataset.
📊 Example
Before:
Class0:950
Class1:50


After under-sampling:
Class0:50
Class1:50


✅ Advantages
Faster training
Simple to apply
Useful for very large datasets
❌ Disadvantages
Loss of information
Important patterns may be removed
🧠 When to Use
Extremely large datasets
Majority class is redundant
🔹 Over-Sampling
📌 What is Over-Sampling?
Definition:
Over-sampling increases the minority class samples to balance the dataset.
📊 Example (Naive Over-Sampling)
Before:
Class0:950
Class1:50


After over-sampling:
Class0:950
Class1:950 (duplicates)


✅ Advantages
No loss of majority data
Improves minority class recall
❌ Disadvantages
Overfitting due to duplicate samples
Increased training time
🧠 When to Use
Small to medium datasets
Minority class is very important
🤖 SMOTE (Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique)
📌 What is SMOTE?
Definition:
SMOTE is an advanced over-sampling technique that creates synthetic (new) minority samples, instead of duplicating existing ones.
🧠 Core Idea
“Create new minority points between existing ones.”
⚙️ How SMOTE Works (Step-by-Step)
Select a minority class sample
Find its K nearest minority neighbors
Pick one neighbor randomly
Create a new point along the line between them
📐 SMOTE Formula (Conceptual)
NewSample= x + rand(0,1) × (x_neighbor − x)


📊 Example
Minority samples:
(2,3), (3,4)


Synthetic sample:
(2.6,3.6)


➡️ Lies naturally between real samples
✅ Advantages of SMOTE
Avoids duplicate data
Reduces overfitting
Creates realistic minority samples
❌ Disadvantages of SMOTE
Can create noisy samples
Not suitable for categorical features (without variants)
Can cause class overlap
🧠 When to Use SMOTE
Minority class is very small
Feature space is continuous
Tree / Logistic / SVM models
⚠️ Important Rules (Very Important)
🚫 Never Apply SMOTE on Test Data
Correct Workflow:
1. Split data into train & test
2. Apply SMOTE only on training data
3. Train model
4. Evaluate on original test data


📊 Comparison Summary
🧪 SMOTE vs class_weight
👉 Often class_weight + SMOTE gives best results.
🎯 Interview One-Liners
Under-sampling:
Reduces majority class to balance data.
Over-sampling:
Increases minority class using duplication or synthesis.
SMOTE:
Creates synthetic minority samples using nearest neighbors.
✅ Best Practice (Real Projects)
Start with class_weight="balanced"
Try SMOTE on training data
Tune decision threshold
Use Recall / F1 / PR-AUC
Validate using cross-validation

=== PAGE: Regression Models ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Regression-Models-2f3fca3384fc8009b8cfd16c95ce3469

Linear Regression 
Linear regression has single and multiple regression
Y = mx+c
y is the predicted value
m is slope 
c is the intercept
x is a feature
best fit line:
It minimises the errors between the predicted and actual values of the dependent variable
Optimization technique: They are used to find the best parameters for the model that minimizes the loss function 
Loss function calculates the errors of the model prediction - ordinary least squares
What is Linear Regression?
Linear Regression is a supervised learning algorithm used to model the relationship between:
Independent variable(s) (features)
Dependent variable (target)
It predicts continuous values.
🔹 Types of Linear Regression
1️⃣ Simple Linear Regression
Uses one feature
Y = mX + c


2️⃣ Multiple Linear Regression
Uses multiple features
Y = m₁X₁ + m₂X₂ + ... + mₙXₙ + c


📐 Equation Explanation
Y = mX + c


📊 What is Slope (m)?
Definition:
Slope represents how much Y changes when X increases by 1 unit.
Positive slope → Y increases with X
Negative slope → Y decreases with X
📍 What is Intercept (c)?
Definition:
Intercept is the value of Y when X = 0.
It shows where the regression line cuts the Y-axis.
📈 Best Fit Line
Definition:
The best fit line is the line that minimizes the total error between:
Actual values
Predicted values
This error is measured using a loss function.
📉 Error (Residual)
Error = Actual Value − Predicted Value


Each data point has a residual, and the goal is to make these as small as possible.
⚙️ Optimization Technique
Definition:
Optimization techniques are used to find the best values of m and c that minimize the loss function.
Common techniques:
Gradient Descent
Normal Equation
📌 Loss Function (Ordinary Least Squares – OLS)
Definition:
Loss function measures how wrong the model predictions are.
Linear Regression uses Ordinary Least Squares (OLS), which minimizes the sum of squared errors.
📐 OLS Formula
Loss = Σ ( yᵢ − ŷᵢ )²


Where:
yᵢ = actual value
ŷᵢ = predicted value
➡️ Squaring:
Removes negative signs
Penalizes large errors
🧮 Example (Simple Linear Regression)
Dataset
Assume the model learns:
m =10
c =30


Prediction Equation
Y =10X +30


Predict for X = 4
Y =10(4) +30
Y =70


➡️ Predicted Marks = 70
How statsmodels Handles Linear Regression (OLS)
statsmodels does the heavy mathematical lifting to find the optimal slope (m) and intercept (c) using Ordinary Least Squares (OLS).
But it doesn’t just stop there.
👉 Instead of only drawing a best-fit line, statsmodels gives a complete statistical diagnosis of your model — like a medical check-up for your data.
🧠 What Happens Behind the Scenes?
When you run:
model = sm.OLS(y, X).fit()


statsmodels performs the following steps:
🔹 1. Calculates the Coefficients (m and c)
What it does:
Finds the exact values of:
Slope (m) → effect of the feature on the target
Intercept (c) → baseline value when X = 0
Interpretation Example:
A slope of 2.5 means:
For every 1-unit increase in page traffic, sales increase by 2.5 units.
🔹 2. Measures Error (Standard Error)
What it does:
Calculates the Standard Error of each coefficient
Shows how much the coefficient would vary if the dataset changed
Why it matters:
Smaller standard error → more reliable estimate
Larger standard error → unstable coefficient
Intuition:
“How much does this slope wiggle if I collect new data?”
🔹 3. Tests Statistical Significance (p-value)
What it does:
Calculates a p-value for each coefficient
Tests whether the relationship is real or due to random noise
Rule of Thumb:
p-value <0.05  → statistically significant
p-value ≥0.05 → not significant


Interpretation Example:
A low p-value for page traffic means traffic genuinely impacts sales.
🔹 4. Quantifies Goodness of Fit (R² Score)
What it does:
Calculates R-squared
Measures how well the model explains the target variable
Meaning:
R² =0.80


➡️ 80% of the variation in sales is explained by page traffic
🔹 5. Computes Confidence Intervals
What it does:
Provides a range within which the true coefficient value likely lies
Example:
Slope = 2.5
95% CI = [2.1, 2.9]


➡️ You’re 95% confident the real effect is between 2.1 and 2.9
🔹 6. Residual Analysis
What it does:
Calculates residuals (errors)
Helps check:
Linearity
Homoscedasticity
Normality of errors
This validates model assumptions.
🧪 Why statsmodels Is Powerful
👉 That’s why statsmodels is preferred for statistical analysis, while sklearn focuses on prediction.
📌 Multicollinearity
Definition:
Multicollinearity occurs when two or more independent variables are highly correlated with each other in a regression model.
🔴 Why Is Multicollinearity a Problem?
Coefficient estimates become unstable
Difficult to understand the individual effect of each feature
Standard errors increase
p-values become unreliable
📊 Example
These two are highly correlated → causes multicollinearity.
📌 Variance Inflation Factor (VIF)
Definition:
VIF measures how much the variance of a regression coefficient is inflated due to multicollinearity.
📐 VIF Formula
VIF =1 / (1 − R²)


Where:
R² = R-squared value obtained by regressing one feature against all other features
📊 VIF Interpretation
🧠 Key Point
If a feature is not correlated with others → R² = 0 → VIF = 1
🛠️ How to Handle Multicollinearity
Remove highly correlated features
Combine features
Use PCA
Use regularization (Ridge, Lasso)
📌 p-value
Definition:
The p-value tells whether an independent variable is statistically significant in predicting the target variable.
🧪 What Does p-value Measure?
It tests the null hypothesis:
The feature has no effect on the target variable.
📊 p-value Interpretation
🧠 Why p-value Is Important
Identifies relevant features
Helps remove irrelevant variables
Improves model interpretability
Avoids overfitting
📈 Example
➡️ Page Traffic significantly impacts sales
➡️ Ad Spend may not meaningfully contribute
Regression Diagnostics (statsmodels)
These statistics help validate the assumptions of linear regression.
📌 Omnibus
Meaning:
Omnibus test checks whether the residuals are normally distributed.
It tests skewness + kurtosis together.
Used as a normality check for errors.
Desired Value:
Loweris better


👉 Interpretation is done using Prob(Omnibus), not the Omnibus value itself.
📌 Prob(Omnibus)
Meaning:
This is the p-value of the Omnibus test.
Null hypothesis: residuals are normally distributed
Desired Value:
Prob(Omnibus) >0.05  ✅


📌 Jarque–Bera (JB)
Meaning:
Another test for normality of residuals, based on:
Skewness
Kurtosis
More sensitive for larger samples.
Desired Value:
Loweris better


👉 Interpretation is done using Prob(JB).
📌 Prob(JB)
Meaning:
p-value of the Jarque–Bera test.
Desired Value:
Prob(JB) >0.05  ✅


📌 Durbin–Watson
Meaning:
Tests for autocorrelation in residuals (especially important in time-series data).
Range:
0to4


Desired Value:
≈ 2  ✅


📌 Condition Number (Cond. No.)
Meaning:
Measures multicollinearity and numerical stability of the model.
High value → features are highly correlated
Can make coefficients unreliable
Desired Value:
< 30  ✅


🧠 Quick Summary Table

=== PAGE: Good to know  ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Good-to-know-2f0fca3384fc807b94b7c6ab18397115

RLM
Conceptually, RLM gives an LLM external memory and a way to operate on it. Here is how it works:
The prompt gets loaded in a variable.
This variable is spliced depending upon the memory or a hard-coded number.
That data gets sent to the LLM and its output is saved for reference.
Similarly all the chunks of the prompt are processed individually and their outputs are recorded.
This list of outputs is used to produce the final response of the model.
Context ROT
Even when a prompt fits inside the token window, model quality quietly degrades as the input grows longer. Attention becomes diffuse, earlier information loses influence, and the model starts missing connections across distant parts of the text. This phenomenon is known as context rot
Context window is how much information the model can actually use well before performance collapses.
This number changes based on the complexity of the prompt and the type of data that is processed. The effective context window of an LLM is much smaller than the token window

=== PAGE: Design Pattern ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Design-Pattern-238fca3384fc8079a526c4020bd7c3a4

It is a description or model for problem-solving that may be applied in a variety of contexts.
Creational design patterns are a subset of design patterns in software development. They deal with the process of object creation, trying to make it more flexible and efficient. It makes the system independent and how its objects are created, composed, and represented.

Structural design patterns are a subset of design patterns in software development that focus on the composition of classes or objects to form larger, more complex structures. They help in organizing and managing relationships between objects to achieve greater flexibility, reusability, and maintainability in a software system.

Behavioural design patterns are a subset of design patterns in software development that deal with the communication and interaction between objects and classes. They focus on how objects and classes collaborate and communicate to accomplish tasks and responsibilities.
Creational design patterns:
Factory method:
 Goal: Create objects without specifying the exact class.
class Parser:
    def Parse(self,data):
        raise NotImplementedError("This must be implemented")

class xmlParser(Parser):
    def Parse(self, data):
        print("parse XML")

class JSONParser(Parser):
    def Parse(self, data):
        print("Json Parser")

class ParseFactory:
    def get_parser(file_type):
        if file_type == 'json':
            return JSONParser()
        elif file_type == 'XML':
            return xmlParser()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    file_type  ="XML"
    obj = ParseFactory.get_parser(file_type)
    obj.Parse("data")
    

Abstract Factory 
is a creational design pattern that lets you create families of related objects without specifying their concrete classes.
When you want to ensure that a group of products (like Button, Checkbox) work together, and you want to switch the whole group at once (like switching from a Light Theme UI to a Dark Theme UI).


from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
class Button(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def render(self):
        pass

class Checkbox(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def render(self):
        pass

class LightButton(Button):
    def render(self):
        return "Rendering Light Button"

class LightCheckbox(Checkbox):
    def render(self):
        return "Rendering Light Checkbox"

# Dark Theme Components
class DarkButton(Button):
    def render(self):
        return "Rendering Dark Button"

class DarkCheckbox(Checkbox):
    def render(self):
        return "Rendering Dark Checkbox"

class UIFactory(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def create_button(self) -> Button:
        pass

    @abstractmethod
    def create_checkbox(self) -> Checkbox:
        pass

class LightUIFactory(UIFactory):
    def create_button(self):
        return LightButton()
    
    def create_checkbox(self):
        return LightCheckbox()
    
class DarkUIFactory(UIFactory):
    def create_button(self) -> Button:
        return DarkButton()

    def create_checkbox(self) -> Checkbox:
        return DarkCheckbox() 

def render_UI(factory):
    btn = factory.create_button()
    chk = factory.create_checkbox()
    print(btn.render())
    print(chk.render())

if __name__ == "__main__":
    render_UI(LightUIFactory())

Builder Design patterns
Goal: Step-by-step creation of complex objects.
📌 Use Case: Creating API request bodies or test data
class ReportBuilder:
    def __init__(self):
        self.report = {}
    
    def set_title(self, title):
        self.report['title'] = title
        return self
    def add_content(self,content):
        self.report['content'] = content
        return self
    def build(self):
        return self.report

report = ReportBuilder().set_title('AM').add_content('Content').build()
print(report['title'])
Prototype 

Create new objects by copying (cloning) an existing object, instead of creating from scratch.
It's useful when:
Object creation is costly (e.g., reading from DB, file, network)
You need to duplicate objects with slight changes
You open a document (object)
Click “Save As” → clone the file
You tweak the copy (title, text) without touching the original
import copy
class TestTemplate:
    def __init__(self, name, case):
        self.name = name
        self.steps = case
    def run(self):
        for i in self.steps:
            print(i)
    def clone(self):
        return copy.deepcopy(self)

test1 = TestTemplate('login', [1,3,4])
test2 = test1.clone()
test2.name = "test1 copy"
print(test1.name, test2.name)
Singleton
Structural Design Patterns 
They help in organizing and managing relationships between objects to achieve greater flexibility, reusability, and maintainability in a software system.

Adapter
 Let's incompatible interfaces work together by converting one interface into another the client expects.
class LegacyLogerr:
    def write(self, message):
        print(message)

class NewLogger:
    def __init__(self, legacy_logger):
        self.legacy_logger = legacy_logger

    def log(self, message):
        self.legacy_logger.write(message)

def log_system(logger):
    logger.log("This is and adapater")

legacy = LegacyLogerr()
adapter = NewLogger(legacy)

log_system(adapter)




Bridge Pattern 
a structural design pattern that decouples abstraction from implementation, so they can evolve independently.
Decorator Pattern 
lets you dynamically add behaviour or responsibilities to an object without modifying its structure or class.

def logger(func):
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        print(f"func {func.__name__}, {args}")
    return wrapper

@logger
def add(a,b):
    print(a+b)

add(1,3)

Facade Pattern 
provides a simplified, unified interface to a complex system of classes, libraries, or APIs.


class CPU:
    def freeze(self):
        print("Freezing processor...")

    def jump(self, position):
        print(f"Jumping to position {position}...")

    def execute(self):
        print("Executing instructions...")


class Memory:
    def load(self, position, data):
        print(f"Loading data '{data}' to memory at position {position}...")


class HardDrive:
    def read(self, lba, size):
        print(f"Reading {size} bytes from sector {lba}...")
        return "OS Bootloader"
    
class ComputerFacade:
    def __init__(self):
        self.cpu = CPU()
        self.memory = Memory()
        self.hard_drive = HardDrive()
    def start_computer(self):
        print("Starting computer...")
        self.cpu.freeze()
        data = self.hard_drive.read(0, 1024)
        self.memory.load(0, data)
        self.cpu.jump(0)
        self.cpu.execute()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    computer = ComputerFacade()
    computer.start_computer()

Simplifies client usage.
Hides internal complexity.
Promotes loose coupling.
Easy to refactor internals without affecting the interface.
Behavioural Design Patterns
Strategy Pattern 
Definition: Strategy Pattern lets you choose the algorithm/behavior at runtime by encapsulating each strategy in a separate class or function.


# Step 1: Define Strategy Interface
class PaymentStrategy:
    def pay(self, amount):
        pass

class CreditCardPayment(PaymentStrategy):
    def pay(self, amount):
        print(f"Paid ₹{amount} using Credit Card")

class UpiPayment(PaymentStrategy):
    def pay(self, amount):
        print(f"Paid ₹{amount} using UPI")

class PaymentProcessor:
    def __init__(self, strategy: PaymentStrategy):
        self.strategy = strategy

    def make_payment(self, amount):
        self.strategy.pay(amount)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    processor = PaymentProcessor(UpiPayment())
    processor.make_payment(500)


Observer Pattern 
Definition: The Observer Pattern defines a one-to-many dependency between objects, so when one object (the Subject) changes state, all its Observers are notified and updated automatically.
class Subscriber:
    def get_update(self,message):
        print(message)

class Channel:
    def __init__(self):
        self.subsriber =[]
    
    def add_subscriber(self,obj):
        self.subsriber.append(obj)

    def notify_users(self):
        for sub in self.subsriber:
            sub.get_update("you have a message")

s1 = Subscriber()
s2 = Subscriber()
ch = Channel()
ch.add_subscriber(s1)
ch.add_subscriber(s2)
ch.notify_users()
State Pattern
The State Pattern allows an object to change its behavior when its internal state changes. It looks as if the object has changed its class.
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
class DocumentState(ABC):
    @abstractmethod
    def apply_action(self):
        pass

class EditingMode(DocumentState):
    def apply_action(self):
        # return super().apply_action()
        print("editing mode")

class ViewMode(DocumentState):
    def apply_action(self):
        # return super().apply_action()
        print("view mode")

class Document:
    def __init__(self):
        self.state = EditingMode()

    def set_state(self, state):
        self.state = state
    
    def actions(self):
        self.state.apply_action()

if __name__ == '__main__':
    d = Document()
    d.actions()
    d.set_state(ViewMode())
    d.actions()
 Chain of Responsibility Pattern
The Chain of Responsibility pattern lets you pass a request along a chain of handlers. Each handler decides:
If it can handle the request
Or pass it to the next handler in the chain
Think of tech support:
Level 1 support → Level 2 support → Level 3 support
If Level 1 can't solve it, it forwards it to the next level
class Handler:
    def __init__(self, next_handler=None):
        self.next_handler = next_handler

    def handle(self, level, message):
        if self.next_handler:
            self.next_handler.handle(level, message)

class DebugHandler(Handler):
    def handle(self, level, message):
        if level == "DEBUG":
            print(f"[DEBUG] {message}")
        else:
            super().handle(level, message)

class InfoHandler(Handler):
    def handle(self, level, message):
        if level == "INFO":
            print(f"[INFO] {message}")
        else:
            super().handle(level, message)

class ErrorHandler(Handler):
    def handle(self, level, message):
        if level == "ERROR":
            print(f"[ERROR] {message}")
        else:
            super().handle(level, message)

# Chain: Debug → Info → Error
chain = DebugHandler(InfoHandler(ErrorHandler()))
# chain.handle("DEBUG", "This is a debug message")
chain.handle("INFO", "This is an info message")
# chain.handle("ERROR", "This is an error message")
# chain.handle("FATAL", "This level is not handled")


DebugHandler.handle() is called first.
It checks: is "INFO" equal to "DEBUG"?
 No → it doesn’t handle it.
It calls InfoHandler.handle().
InfoHandler.handle() checks:
Is "INFO" equal to "INFO"?
Yes → it handles the message:
python
CopyEdit
print("[INFO] This is an info message")

# Create a prompt template from the template string
template = "You are an artificial intelligence assistant, answer the question. {question}"
prompt = PromptTemplate.from_template(
    template=template
)
