=== PAGE: 100 days of AI ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/100-days-of-AI-1f4fca3384fc808eb8c8eb268a822b0f

DAY: 1
Topics Covered:
--------------
1. Definition of AI
2. Key Concepts of AI
3. Linear Regression: Theory, Formulas & Code
4. Drawbacks of Linear Regression
1. What is Artificial Intelligence?
----------------------------------
AI (Artificial Intelligence) is the simulation of human intelligence by machines. It enables systems to perform tasks like understanding language, recognizing patterns, learning from data, and making decisions.
In Software Engineering, AI helps automate testing, make predictions, optimize code, and build intelligent features like recommendation systems or chatbots.
2. Key Concepts of AI
Data: The most essential component. AI learns patterns from it.
Features: Input variables used for prediction.
Target: Output variable the model tries to predict.
Supervised Learning: Training the model using labeled data.
Model: A function that maps inputs to outputs.
Loss Function: A way to measure prediction error (e.g., MSE).
Machine Learning Types (3 Main Categories)
Supervised Learning
The model learns from labeled data (input + correct output).
Example: Spam detection (emails labeled as spam or not spam).
Algorithms: Linear Regression, Decision Trees, Neural Networks.
Unsupervised Learning
The model learns from unlabeled data — it finds hidden patterns or structure.
Example: Customer segmentation, anomaly detection.
Algorithms: K-Means, PCA, Autoencoders.
Reinforcement Learning
The model learns by interacting with an environment, receiving rewards or penalties.
Example: Teaching a robot to walk or an AI to play chess.
Algorithms: Q-Learning, Deep Q-Networks (DQN).

In Supervised Learning, we mainly have two core types of problems
1. Classification
Goal: Predict a category or label.
Output: Discrete (e.g., “spam” or “not spam”).
Examples:
Email spam detection 📧
Disease diagnosis 🩺
Sentiment analysis (positive/negative review) 💬
Algorithms:
Logistic Regression
Decision Trees / Random Forest
Support Vector Machines (SVM)
Neural Networks
📈 2. Regression (Linear or Nonlinear)
Goal: Predict a numerical value.
Output: Continuous (e.g., “price = ₹35.7 lakhs”).
Examples:
Predicting house prices 🏠
Stock price prediction 📊
Temperature forecasting 🌡️
Algorithms:
Linear Regression (most basic one)
Polynomial Regression
Ridge / Lasso Regression
Neural Networks
3. Linear Regression
Definition:
Linear Regression is a supervised learning algorithm used to predict continuous values based on the relationship between input(s) and output. It finds the best-fit line that minimizes the error between actual and predicted values.
Simple Linear Regression Formula:
y = mx + b
Where:
y: Predicted output
x: Input feature
m: Slope (weight)
b: Intercept (bias)
How to calculate m and b:
Using raw sums:
m = (n * Σ(xy) - Σx * Σy) / (n * Σx^2 - (Σx)^2)
b = (Σy - m * Σx) / n
Using means:
m = Σ((x - x̄)(y - ȳ)) / Σ((x - x̄)^2)
b = ȳ - m * x̄
Multiple Linear Regression
-------------------------
Definition:
Multiple Linear Regression is used when there are **two or more input features (independent variables)**.
Formula:
y = b0 + b1*x1 + b2*x2 + ... + bn*xn
Where:
y: Predicted output
x1, x2, ..., xn: Input features
b0: Intercept
b1, ..., bn: Coefficients (slopes for each feature)
Mean Squared Error (MSE):
MSE tells you how well your model is performing.
Formula:
MSE = (1/n) * Σ(y - ŷ)^2
Where:
y: actual value
ŷ: predicted value
n: number of data points
Lower MSE = better model performance.
Drawbacks of Linear Regression:
Assumes a linear relationship
Sensitive to outliers
Doesn’t handle complex or curved data
Doesn’t account for correlated inputs (multicollinearity)
LOSS:
Loss is a numerical metric that describes how wrong a model's predictions are. Loss measures the distance between the model's predictions and the actual labels. The goal of training a model is to minimize the loss, reducing it to its lowest possible value.
Polynomial Regression
 is just an extension of Linear Regression 
But it allows the model to learn curved (non-linear) relationships between input and output.
In Linear Regression, the model tries to fit a straight line:
y=b0+b1xy = b_0 + b_1x
y=b0+b1x
But sometimes data is not linear (it curves).
So we add higher powers of xxx — that’s Polynomial Regression:
y=b0+b1x+b2x2+b3x3+...+bnxny = b_0 + b_1x + b_2x^2 + b_3x^3 + ... + b_nx^n
y=b0+b1x+b2x2+b3x3+...+bnxn
This lets the model bend the line to fit the data better
Data bias is when the data used to train an AI or ML model is not representative of the real-world population or is skewed, leading the model to make unfair or inaccurate predictions.
Gradient descent 
It is a mathematical technique that iteratively finds the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss. Gradient descent finds the best weight and bias by repeating the following process for a number of user-defined iterations.
The model begins training with randomized weights and biases near zero, and then repeats the following steps:
Calculate the loss with the current weight and bias.
Determine the direction to move the weights and bias that reduces loss.
Move the weight and bias values a small amount in the direction that reduces loss.
Return to step one and repeat the process until the model can't reduce the loss any further.
The diagram below outlines the iterative steps gradient descent performs to find the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss.
Figure 12. Gradient descent is an iterative process that finds the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss
Hyperparameters are variables that control different aspects of training. Three common hyperparameters are:
Learning rate
Batch size
Epochs
Day 2
Logistic regression
type of regression model that predicts a probability
The term logistic regression usually refers to binary logistic regression, that is, to a model that calculates probabilities for labels with two possible values. A less common variant, multinomial logistic regression, calculates probabilities for labels with more than two possible values.
A logistic regression model uses the following two-step architecture:
The model generates a raw prediction (y') by applying a linear function of input features.
The model uses that raw prediction as input to a sigmoid function, which converts the raw prediction to a value between 0 and 1, exclusive.
Sigmoid Function:
A mathematical function that "squishes" an input value into a constrained range, typically 0 to 1 or -1 to +1. That is, you can pass any number (two, a million, negative billion, whatever) to a sigmoid and the output will still be in the constrained range. A plot of the sigmoid activation function looks as follows:
The sigmoid function has several uses in machine learning, including:
Converting the raw output of a logistic regression or multinomial regression model to a probability.
Acting as an activation function in some neural networks.
The sigmoid function over an input number x has the following formula:
𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑑(𝑥)=1/(1+𝑒−x)
In machine learning, x is generally a weighted sum.
As the input, x, increases, the output of the sigmoid function approaches but never reaches 1. Similarly, as the input decreases, the sigmoid function's output approaches but never reaches 0
Transforming linear output using the sigmoid function
The following equation represents the linear component of a logistic regression model:
𝑧=𝑏+𝑤1𝑥1+𝑤2𝑥2+…+𝑤𝑁𝑥𝑁
where:
z is the output of the linear equation, also called the log odds.
b is the bias.
The w values are the model's learned weights.
The x values are the feature values for a particular example.
To obtain the logistic regression prediction, the z value is then passed to the sigmoid function, yielding a value (a probability) between 0 and 1:
𝑦′=1/1+𝑒−𝑧
where:
y' is the output of the logistic regression model.
z is the linear output (as calculated in the preceding equation).
Day 3
Classification
Classification is the task of predicting which of a set of classes (categories) an example belongs to. In this module, you'll learn how to convert a logistic regression model that predicts a probability into a binary classification model that predicts one of two classes
Confusion matrix
The probability score is not reality, or ground truth. There are four possible outcomes for each output from a binary classifier.
Accuracy is the proportion of all classifications that were correct, whether positive or negative. It is mathematically defined as:
Accuracy=correct classifications / total classifications=𝑇𝑃+𝑇𝑁/(𝑇𝑃+𝑇𝑁+𝐹𝑃+𝐹𝑁)
In the spam classification example, accuracy measures the fraction of all emails correctly classified.
Recall, or true positive rate
The true positive rate (TPR), or the proportion of all actual positives that were classified correctly as positives, is also known as recall.
Recall is mathematically defined as:
Recall (or TPR)=correctly classified actual positivesall actual positives=𝑇𝑃/(𝑇𝑃+𝐹𝑁)
False negatives are actual positives that were misclassified as negatives, which is why they appear in the denominator.
False positive rate
The false positive rate (FPR) is the proportion of all actual negatives that were classified incorrectly as positives, also known as the probability of false alarm. It is mathematically defined as:
FPR=incorrectly classified actual negativesall actual negatives=𝐹𝑃/(𝐹𝑃+𝑇𝑁)
False positives are actual negatives that were misclassified, which is why they appear in the denominator
Precision is the proportion of all the model's positive classifications that are actually positive. It is mathematically defined as:
Precision=correctly classified actual positives, everything classified as positive=𝑇𝑃/(𝑇𝑃+𝐹𝑃)
In the spam classification example, precision measures the fraction of emails classified as spam that were actually spam.
ROC Curve
The ROC curve is a graphical plot that illustrates the performance of a binary classifier as the discrimination threshold is varied.
X-axis: False Positive Rate (FPR) = FP / (FP + TN)
Y-axis: True Positive Rate (TPR) = TP / (TP + FN) = Recall
Each point on the curve corresponds to a different threshold. A good model will have a curve that bows towards the top-left corner.
AUC (Area Under the Curve)
The AUC is the area under the ROC curve, giving a single number summary of the model's performance.
AUC = 1.0 → Perfect model
AUC = 0.5 → No discrimination (random guessing)
AUC < 0.5 → Worse than random (e.g., systematically wrong predictions)
Day 4:
Types of data: Numerical data, Categorical data.
Feature vector: The input feature is represented as a vector during training and inference
Representing raw dataset values as trainable values in the feature vector. This process is called feature engineering, and it is a vital part of machine learning. The most common feature engineering techniques are:
Normalization: Converting numerical values into a standard range.
Binning (also referred to as bucketing): Converting numerical values into buckets of ranges.
three popular normalization methods:
linear scaling
Z-score scaling
log scaling
Linear scaling (more commonly shortened to just scaling) means converting floating-point values from their natural range into a standard range—usually 0 to 1 or -1 to +1.
Use the following formula to scale to the standard range 0 to 1, inclusive:
𝑥′=(𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛)/(𝑥𝑚𝑎𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛)
where:
𝑥′ is the scaled value.
𝑥 is the original value.
𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛 is the lowest value in the dataset of this feature.
𝑥𝑚𝑎𝑥 is the highest value in the dataset of this feature.
Linear scaling is a good choice when all of the following conditions are met:
The lower and upper bounds of your data don't change much over time.
The feature contains few or no outliers, and those outliers aren't extreme.
The feature is approximately uniformly distributed across its range. That is, a histogram would show roughly even bars for most values.
Z-score scaling
A Z-score is the number of standard deviations a value is from the mean. For example, a value that is 2 standard deviations greater than the mean has a Z-score of +2.0. A value that is 1.5 standard deviations less than the mean has a Z-score of -1.5.
Use the following formula to normalize a value,
𝑥′=(𝑥−μ)/σ
where:
𝑥′ is the Z-score.
μ - is the mean.
σ is the standard deviation.
Day 5
Categorical data has a specific set of possible values. For example:
The different species of animals in a national park
The names of streets in a particular city
Whether or not an email is spam
Encoding means converting categorical or other data to numerical vectors that a model can train on. This conversion is necessary because models can only train on floating-point values;
Representing categorical data as a vector in which:
One element is set to 1.
All other elements are set to 0.
Day 6
Overfitting:
The model performs well on the training set but poorly on test data or real-world data.
It has memorized the training data instead of learning patterns.
Underfitting:
The model performs poorly on both training and test data.
It hasn’t learned enough from the data—too simple or not trained well.
Regularization:
A technique where we add a penalty to the loss function (based on model weights) to prevent overfitting by discouraging overly complex models.
Generalization is the ability of a machine learning model to perform well on new, unseen data—not just the data it was trained on.
1. L1 Regularization (Lasso)
Adds the absolute value of weights to the loss function.
Can shrink some weights to zero, effectively performing feature selection.
Used in regression and neural networks.
📌 Loss:
Loss = Original Loss + λ * Σ|weights|
🔢 2. L2 Regularization (Ridge)
Adds the squared value of weights to the loss function.
Shrinks weights but doesn’t zero them out.
Encourages small, distributed weights.
📌 Loss:
Loss = Original Loss + λ * Σ(weights²)
Day 7:
Neural networks are a family of model architectures designed to find nonlinear patterns in data. During training of a neural network, the model automatically learns the optimal feature crosses to perform on the input data to minimize loss.
📌 1. What is a Neural Network?
A neural network is a machine learning model inspired by the human brain. It consists of neurons (also called nodes) organized in layers.
Input Layer – Takes features from data
Hidden Layers – Process the inputs through weights and activation
Output Layer – Produces final predictions
🧮 2. Key Components
One Neuron Model (Single Input)
Let’s solve this example manually to understand how NN works.
🔢 Problem
We want to predict:
Input: x = 2
Actual Output: y = 1
Initial parameters:
Weight w = 0.5
Bias b = 0.0
Learning rate η = 0.1
🔁 Iteration 1: Forward Pass
1️⃣ Compute Weighted Sum:
z = w * x + b = 0.5 * 2 + 0 = 1.0
2️⃣ Apply Activation (Sigmoid):
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-z)) = 1 / (1 + e^(-1)) ≈ 0.7311
3️⃣ Compute Loss (MSE):
Loss = 0.5 * (y - ŷ)^2 = 0.5 * (1 - 0.7311)^2 ≈ 0.0362
🔁 Iteration 1: Backward Pass (Gradient Descent)
Compute Gradients:
dL/dŷ = ŷ - y = 0.7311 - 1 = -0.2689
dŷ/dz = ŷ * (1 - ŷ) = 0.7311 * (1 - 0.7311) = 0.1966
dz/dw = x = 2
dL/dw = -0.2689 * 0.1966 * 2 ≈ -0.1057
Update Weight:
w = w - η * dL/dw = 0.5 - 0.1 * (-0.1057) = 0.5106
🔁 Iteration 2: Repeat
1️⃣ Forward Pass:
z = 0.5106 * 2 + 0 = 1.0211
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-1.0211)) ≈ 0.7342
2️⃣ Loss:
Loss = 0.5 * (1 - 0.7342)^2 ≈ 0.0353
3️⃣ Backward Pass:
dL/dw ≈ -0.1022
w = 0.5106 + 0.0102 = 0.5208
✅ Final Prediction After 2 Iterations
w = 0.5208, b = 0.0
z = 0.5208 * 2 = 1.0416
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-1.0416)) ≈ 0.7411
So, the model predicts ŷ ≈ 0.7411 for input x = 2 after training.
🛡️ 4. Regularization (L2)
Add a penalty to avoid overfitting:
Loss_total = Loss + (λ / 2) * w^2
Example:
λ = 0.1, w = 0.5208
L2 Penalty ≈ 0.0136
Total Loss ≈ 0.0353 + 0.0136 = 0.0489
This penalizes large weights and helps in better generalization.
Day 8:
Embeddings:
Embeddings are dense vector representations of discrete data (like words, categories, etc.).
They transform high-dimensional or symbolic inputs into low-dimensional numeric vectors that capture meaning or relationships.



How is it different from One-Hot Encoding?



One-Hot Encoding Example:
Say we have categories: ["cat", "dog", "fish", "horse"]
"dog" → [0, 1, 0, 0]
No meaning, just position
🌈 Embedding Example (after training):
"dog" → [0.12, -0.45, 0.77]
"cat" → [0.13, -0.40, 0.79]
These vectors are close in space, meaning they're semantically similar
This is very useful in NLP, recommendation systems, and more.
Common Models for Creating Embeddings:
🔤 For Text (Word Embeddings)

=== PAGE: Azure Devops ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Azure-Devops-204fca3384fc8068b647ed9735ee509e

Creating an env: Python-m venv env 
Activate the virtual environment: env\Scripts\activate
Get requirement.txt: pip freeze > requirements.txt
Here is a sample YAML file (azure-pipelines.yml) that demonstrates how to schedule and run a Python script using a cron trigger.
# azure-pipelines.yml
# This YAML pipeline schedules and runs a Python script.

trigger: none  # Prevents the pipeline from running on every code commit.

pr: none       # Disables pipeline runs on pull requests.

schedules:
- cron: "0 8 * * *"  # A cron expression for running at 8:00 AM every day
  displayName: 'Daily Python Script Execution'
  branches:
    include:
    - main           # The branch on which the schedule will run.
  always: true       # Run the pipeline even if there are no code changes since the last run.

pool:
  vmImage: 'ubuntu-latest'  # Use a hosted agent with Ubuntu.

steps:
- task: UsePythonVersion@0
  inputs:
    versionSpec: '3.9'    # Specify the Python version to use.
  displayName: 'Install Python'

- script: |
    python -m pip install --upgrade pip
    pip install -r requirements.txt  # Install required Python packages.
  displayName: 'Install dependencies'

- script: |
    python your_script.py  # Execute your Python script.
  displayName: 'Run Python script'
Breakdown of the YAML Configuration
trigger and pr: Setting both to none ensures the pipeline won't run automatically on code pushes or pull requests. This is crucial for a scheduled-only job.
schedules: This is the core of the scheduling.
cron: The cron expression "0 8 * * *" schedules the pipeline to run at 8:00 AM every day. The format is minute hour day_of_month month day_of_week. You can use a cron generator to create the exact time you need.
displayName: A descriptive name for the scheduled task.
branches: Specifies the branch on which the schedule should run. This prevents the job from running on all branches.
always: If set to true, the pipeline runs on schedule regardless of whether there have been any new code changes since the last run.
pool: Defines the virtual machine (VM) image for the agent that will run the job. ubuntu-latest is a good choice for most Python projects.
steps: A list of tasks to be executed sequentially.
UsePythonVersion@0: This task installs the specified Python version on the build agent.
pip install: This script block uses pip to install the dependencies listed in your requirements.txt file. Make sure this file is committed to your repository.
python your_script.py: This is the final step where your Python script is executed. Replace your_script.py with the actual name of your script file.
1. trigger
The trigger section determines what events will automatically start your pipeline. This is a fundamental part of continuous integration (CI).
Example: To run the pipeline on every commit to the main branch:YAML
trigger:
  - main
No trigger: You can set trigger: none to disable automatic runs and only allow manual execution.
2. pool
The pool section specifies the agent (a machine that runs the job) where your pipeline's tasks will be executed.
Example: To use a Microsoft-hosted agent running Ubuntu:YAML
pool:
  vmImage: 'ubuntu-latest'
3. steps
The steps section is the heart of the pipeline, containing the sequence of tasks and scripts that the agent will run.
script: This is a simple command-line script. It's often used for tasks like installing dependencies or running tests.
task: A task is a pre-built, reusable action from the Azure DevOps Marketplace. Tasks are more structured and provide specific functionality, such as installing a Python version or building a Docker image.
Example of a simple pipeline with steps:YAML
steps:
- script: echo 'Hello, World!'
  displayName: 'A simple script'

- task: UsePythonVersion@0
  inputs:
    versionSpec: '3.9'
  displayName: 'Install Python'
Creating a webapp 
Once created, go to the deployment center and give the source code
In settings, add the Startup command in the portal  gunicorn --bind=0.0.0.0 --timeout 600 main:app


=== PAGE: Transformers ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/Transformers-237fca3384fc803ba69ace1e11093e3a

A transformer model is a neural network that learns the context of sequential data and generates new data out of it.
To put it simply:
A transformer is a type of artificial intelligence model that learns to understand and generate human-like text by analyzing patterns in large amounts of text data.
Word embedding 
is a technique used in natural language processing (NLP) to represent words as vectors of real numbers, so that machines can understand and process human language more effectively.
king   → [0.52, 0.81, ..., -0.14]
queen  → [0.48, 0.79, ..., -0.10]

These vectors are learned from text data, and they capture meaning and relationships.
Raw text consists of characters and words, which are not meaningful to a model. Word embeddings:
Allow mathematical operations on words
Capture semantic meaning (e.g., "good" is close to "great")
Help models generalize better
Raw text is just a sequence of characters and words, not meaningful to a model. Word embeddings:
Allow mathematical operations on words
Capture semantic meaning (e.g., "good" is close to "great")
Help models generalize better

Word embeddings capture semantic meaning because words with similar meanings tend to occur in similar contexts, and training adjusts vectors to reflect that.


A word embedding is a 1D vector like (300,), not (1 column with rows).
A sentence embedding is typically a 2D matrix: (num_words, embedding_dim)
What is Encoder-Decoder Architecture?
It’s a two-part neural network where:
 1. Encoder
Reads the input sequence (e.g., "I love you")
Encodes it into a fixed-length vector (called the context or hidden state)
Captures the semantic meaning of the input
2. Decoder
Takes the encoded context and generates the output sequence
Produces one token at a time (e.g., "Je", then "t'", then "aime")
Core Idea of Transformrs
Instead of processing words one by one (like RNNs), the Transformer processes entire sequences in parallel using a mechanism called self-attention.
🏗️ High-Level Structure
text
CopyEdit
               ┌─────────────┐
Input → Token →│  Encoder    │→ Context Representation
               └─────────────┘
                          ↓
               ┌─────────────┐
              ←│  Decoder    │→ Output Tokens
               └─────────────┘


🔍 Detailed View
1. Input Embedding + Positional Encoding
Words are converted to vectors (embeddings)
Since Transformers have no recurrence, they add positional encoding to track the order of tokens
2. Encoder
Each Encoder block (there are usually many) consists of:
pgsql
CopyEdit
[Input]
 ↓
Multi-Head Self-Attention  (token attends to all other tokens)
 ↓
Add & LayerNorm
 ↓
Feed Forward Network
 ↓
Add & LayerNorm


Self-Attention helps each word focus on other important words in the input
Multi-Head Attention = multiple attention operations in parallel
3. Decoder
Similar blocks to encoder, but adds cross-attention:
mathematica
CopyEdit
[Shifted Output Input]
 ↓
Masked Multi-Head Self-Attention (can’t look at future words)
 ↓
Cross-Attention (attends to encoder output)
 ↓
Feed Forward
 ↓
Output Token Prediction


🔁 Self-Attention (The Heart of It)
Each token looks at all other tokens and weighs them to decide what's important.
For example:
vbnet
CopyEdit
Sentence: "The cat sat on the mat"
→ Attention: "sat" pays more attention to "cat"


Attention Formula (simplified):
mathematica
CopyEdit
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q × Kᵗ / √d) × V


Where:
Q = Query
K = Key
V = Value
(All derived from input embeddings)
⚙️ Transformer Architecture Summary
📚 Real-World Examples
https://www.datacamp.com/tutorial/how-transformers-work

=== PAGE: AI ===
URL: https://app.notion.com/p/AI-125fca3384fc804a8ff8d3e19dd5b879

DAY: 1
Topics Covered:
--------------
1. Definition of AI
2. Key Concepts of AI
3. Linear Regression: Theory, Formulas & Code
4. Drawbacks of Linear Regression
1. What is Artificial Intelligence?
----------------------------------
AI (Artificial Intelligence) is the simulation of human intelligence by machines. It enables systems to perform tasks like understanding language, recognizing patterns, learning from data, and making decisions.
In Software Engineering, AI helps automate testing, make predictions, optimize code, and build intelligent features like recommendation systems or chatbots.
2. Key Concepts of AI
Data: The most essential component. AI learns patterns from it.
Features: Input variables used for prediction.
Target: Output variable the model tries to predict.
Supervised Learning: Training the model using labeled data.
Model: A function that maps inputs to outputs.
Loss Function: A way to measure prediction error (e.g., MSE).
Machine Learning Types (3 Main Categories)
Supervised Learning
The model learns from labeled data (input + correct output).
Example: Spam detection (emails labeled as spam or not spam).
Algorithms: Linear Regression, Decision Trees, Neural Networks.
Unsupervised Learning
The model learns from unlabeled data — it finds hidden patterns or structure.
Example: Customer segmentation, anomaly detection.
Algorithms: K-Means, PCA, Autoencoders.
Reinforcement Learning
The model learns by interacting with an environment, receiving rewards or penalties.
Example: Teaching a robot to walk or an AI to play chess.
Algorithms: Q-Learning, Deep Q-Networks (DQN).

In Supervised Learning, we mainly have two core types of problems
1. Classification
Goal: Predict a category or label.
Output: Discrete (e.g., “spam” or “not spam”).
Examples:
Email spam detection 📧
Disease diagnosis 🩺
Sentiment analysis (positive/negative review) 💬
Algorithms:
Logistic Regression
Decision Trees / Random Forest
Support Vector Machines (SVM)
Neural Networks
📈 2. Regression (Linear or Nonlinear)
Goal: Predict a numerical value.
Output: Continuous (e.g., “price = ₹35.7 lakhs”).
Examples:
Predicting house prices 🏠
Stock price prediction 📊
Temperature forecasting 🌡️
Algorithms:
Linear Regression (most basic one)
Polynomial Regression
Ridge / Lasso Regression
Neural Networks
3. Linear Regression
Definition:
Linear Regression is a supervised learning algorithm used to predict continuous values based on the relationship between input(s) and output. It finds the best-fit line that minimizes the error between actual and predicted values.
Simple Linear Regression Formula:
y = mx + b
Where:
y: Predicted output
x: Input feature
m: Slope (weight)
b: Intercept (bias)
How to calculate m and b:
Using raw sums:
m = (n * Σ(xy) - Σx * Σy) / (n * Σx^2 - (Σx)^2)
b = (Σy - m * Σx) / n
Using means:
m = Σ((x - x̄)(y - ȳ)) / Σ((x - x̄)^2)
b = ȳ - m * x̄
Multiple Linear Regression
-------------------------
Definition:
Multiple Linear Regression is used when there are **two or more input features (independent variables)**.
Formula:
y = b0 + b1*x1 + b2*x2 + ... + bn*xn
Where:
y: Predicted output
x1, x2, ..., xn: Input features
b0: Intercept
b1, ..., bn: Coefficients (slopes for each feature)
Mean Squared Error (MSE):
MSE tells you how well your model is performing.
Formula:
MSE = (1/n) * Σ(y - ŷ)^2
Where:
y: actual value
ŷ: predicted value
n: number of data points
Lower MSE = better model performance.
Drawbacks of Linear Regression:
Assumes a linear relationship
Sensitive to outliers
Doesn’t handle complex or curved data
Doesn’t account for correlated inputs (multicollinearity)
LOSS:
Loss is a numerical metric that describes how wrong a model's predictions are. Loss measures the distance between the model's predictions and the actual labels. The goal of training a model is to minimize the loss, reducing it to its lowest possible value.
Polynomial Regression
 is just an extension of Linear Regression 
But it allows the model to learn curved (non-linear) relationships between input and output.
In Linear Regression, the model tries to fit a straight line:
y=b0+b1xy = b_0 + b_1x
y=b0+b1x
But sometimes data is not linear (it curves).
So we add higher powers of xxx — that’s Polynomial Regression:
y=b0+b1x+b2x2+b3x3+...+bnxny = b_0 + b_1x + b_2x^2 + b_3x^3 + ... + b_nx^n
y=b0+b1x+b2x2+b3x3+...+bnxn
This lets the model bend the line to fit the data better
Data bias is when the data used to train an AI or ML model is not representative of the real-world population or is skewed, leading the model to make unfair or inaccurate predictions.
Gradient descent 
It is a mathematical technique that iteratively finds the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss. Gradient descent finds the best weight and bias by repeating the following process for a number of user-defined iterations.
The model begins training with randomized weights and biases near zero, and then repeats the following steps:
Calculate the loss with the current weight and bias.
Determine the direction to move the weights and bias that reduces loss.
Move the weight and bias values a small amount in the direction that reduces loss.
Return to step one and repeat the process until the model can't reduce the loss any further.
The diagram below outlines the iterative steps gradient descent performs to find the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss.
Figure 12. Gradient descent is an iterative process that finds the weights and bias that produce the model with the lowest loss
Hyperparameters are variables that control different aspects of training. Three common hyperparameters are:
Learning rate
Batch size
Epochs
Day 2
Logistic regression
type of regression model that predicts a probability
The term logistic regression usually refers to binary logistic regression, that is, to a model that calculates probabilities for labels with two possible values. A less common variant, multinomial logistic regression, calculates probabilities for labels with more than two possible values.
A logistic regression model uses the following two-step architecture:
The model generates a raw prediction (y') by applying a linear function of input features.
The model uses that raw prediction as input to a sigmoid function, which converts the raw prediction to a value between 0 and 1, exclusive.
Sigmoid Function:
A mathematical function that "squishes" an input value into a constrained range, typically 0 to 1 or -1 to +1. That is, you can pass any number (two, a million, negative billion, whatever) to a sigmoid and the output will still be in the constrained range. A plot of the sigmoid activation function looks as follows:
The sigmoid function has several uses in machine learning, including:
Converting the raw output of a logistic regression or multinomial regression model to a probability.
Acting as an activation function in some neural networks.
The sigmoid function over an input number x has the following formula:
𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑑(𝑥)=1/(1+𝑒−x)
In machine learning, x is generally a weighted sum.
As the input, x, increases, the output of the sigmoid function approaches but never reaches 1. Similarly, as the input decreases, the sigmoid function's output approaches but never reaches 0
Transforming linear output using the sigmoid function
The following equation represents the linear component of a logistic regression model:
𝑧=𝑏+𝑤1𝑥1+𝑤2𝑥2+…+𝑤𝑁𝑥𝑁
where:
z is the output of the linear equation, also called the log odds.
b is the bias.
The w values are the model's learned weights.
The x values are the feature values for a particular example.
To obtain the logistic regression prediction, the z value is then passed to the sigmoid function, yielding a value (a probability) between 0 and 1:
𝑦′=1/1+𝑒−𝑧
where:
y' is the output of the logistic regression model.
z is the linear output (as calculated in the preceding equation).
Day 3
Classification
Classification is the task of predicting which of a set of classes (categories) an example belongs to. In this module, you'll learn how to convert a logistic regression model that predicts a probability into a binary classification model that predicts one of two classes
Confusion matrix
The probability score is not reality, or ground truth. There are four possible outcomes for each output from a binary classifier.
Accuracy is the proportion of all classifications that were correct, whether positive or negative. It is mathematically defined as:
Accuracy=correct classifications / total classifications=𝑇𝑃+𝑇𝑁/(𝑇𝑃+𝑇𝑁+𝐹𝑃+𝐹𝑁)
In the spam classification example, accuracy measures the fraction of all emails correctly classified.
Recall, or true positive rate
The true positive rate (TPR), or the proportion of all actual positives that were classified correctly as positives, is also known as recall.
Recall is mathematically defined as:
Recall (or TPR)=correctly classified actual positivesall actual positives=𝑇𝑃/(𝑇𝑃+𝐹𝑁)
False negatives are actual positives that were misclassified as negatives, which is why they appear in the denominator.
False positive rate
The false positive rate (FPR) is the proportion of all actual negatives that were classified incorrectly as positives, also known as the probability of false alarm. It is mathematically defined as:
FPR=incorrectly classified actual negativesall actual negatives=𝐹𝑃/(𝐹𝑃+𝑇𝑁)
False positives are actual negatives that were misclassified, which is why they appear in the denominator
Precision is the proportion of all the model's positive classifications that are actually positive. It is mathematically defined as:
Precision=correctly classified actual positives, everything classified as positive=𝑇𝑃/(𝑇𝑃+𝐹𝑃)
In the spam classification example, precision measures the fraction of emails classified as spam that were actually spam.
ROC Curve
The ROC curve is a graphical plot that illustrates the performance of a binary classifier as the discrimination threshold is varied.
X-axis: False Positive Rate (FPR) = FP / (FP + TN)
Y-axis: True Positive Rate (TPR) = TP / (TP + FN) = Recall
Each point on the curve corresponds to a different threshold. A good model will have a curve that bows towards the top-left corner.
AUC (Area Under the Curve)
The AUC is the area under the ROC curve, giving a single number summary of the model's performance.
AUC = 1.0 → Perfect model
AUC = 0.5 → No discrimination (random guessing)
AUC < 0.5 → Worse than random (e.g., systematically wrong predictions)
Day 4:
Types of data: Numerical data, Categorical data.
Feature vector: The input feature is represented as a vector during training and inference
Representing raw dataset values as trainable values in the feature vector. This process is called feature engineering, and it is a vital part of machine learning. The most common feature engineering techniques are:
Normalization: Converting numerical values into a standard range.
Binning (also referred to as bucketing): Converting numerical values into buckets of ranges.
three popular normalization methods:
linear scaling
Z-score scaling
log scaling
Linear scaling (more commonly shortened to just scaling) means converting floating-point values from their natural range into a standard range—usually 0 to 1 or -1 to +1.
Use the following formula to scale to the standard range 0 to 1, inclusive:
𝑥′=(𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛)/(𝑥𝑚𝑎𝑥−𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛)
where:
𝑥′ is the scaled value.
𝑥 is the original value.
𝑥𝑚𝑖𝑛 is the lowest value in the dataset of this feature.
𝑥𝑚𝑎𝑥 is the highest value in the dataset of this feature.
Linear scaling is a good choice when all of the following conditions are met:
The lower and upper bounds of your data don't change much over time.
The feature contains few or no outliers, and those outliers aren't extreme.
The feature is approximately uniformly distributed across its range. That is, a histogram would show roughly even bars for most values.
Z-score scaling
A Z-score is the number of standard deviations a value is from the mean. For example, a value that is 2 standard deviations greater than the mean has a Z-score of +2.0. A value that is 1.5 standard deviations less than the mean has a Z-score of -1.5.
Use the following formula to normalize a value,
𝑥′=(𝑥−μ)/σ
where:
𝑥′ is the Z-score.
μ - is the mean.
σ is the standard deviation.
Day 5
Categorical data has a specific set of possible values. For example:
The different species of animals in a national park
The names of streets in a particular city
Whether or not an email is spam
Encoding means converting categorical or other data to numerical vectors that a model can train on. This conversion is necessary because models can only train on floating-point values;
Representing categorical data as a vector in which:
One element is set to 1.
All other elements are set to 0.
Day 6
Overfitting:
The model performs well on the training set but poorly on test data or real-world data.
It has memorized the training data instead of learning patterns.
Underfitting:
The model performs poorly on both training and test data.
It hasn’t learned enough from the data—too simple or not trained well.
Regularization:
A technique where we add a penalty to the loss function (based on model weights) to prevent overfitting by discouraging overly complex models.
Generalization is the ability of a machine learning model to perform well on new, unseen data—not just the data it was trained on.
1. L1 Regularization (Lasso)
Adds the absolute value of weights to the loss function.
Can shrink some weights to zero, effectively performing feature selection.
Used in regression and neural networks.
📌 Loss:
Loss = Original Loss + λ * Σ|weights|
🔢 2. L2 Regularization (Ridge)
Adds the squared value of weights to the loss function.
Shrinks weights but doesn’t zero them out.
Encourages small, distributed weights.
📌 Loss:
Loss = Original Loss + λ * Σ(weights²)
Day 7:
Neural networks are a family of model architectures designed to find nonlinear patterns in data. During training of a neural network, the model automatically learns the optimal feature crosses to perform on the input data to minimize loss.
📌 1. What is a Neural Network?
A neural network is a machine learning model inspired by the human brain. It consists of neurons (also called nodes) organized in layers.
Input Layer – Takes features from data
Hidden Layers – Process the inputs through weights and activation
Output Layer – Produces final predictions
🧮 2. Key Components
One Neuron Model (Single Input)
Let’s solve this example manually to understand how NN works.
🔢 Problem
We want to predict:
Input: x = 2
Actual Output: y = 1
Initial parameters:
Weight w = 0.5
Bias b = 0.0
Learning rate η = 0.1
🔁 Iteration 1: Forward Pass
1️⃣ Compute Weighted Sum:
z = w * x + b = 0.5 * 2 + 0 = 1.0
2️⃣ Apply Activation (Sigmoid):
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-z)) = 1 / (1 + e^(-1)) ≈ 0.7311
3️⃣ Compute Loss (MSE):
Loss = 0.5 * (y - ŷ)^2 = 0.5 * (1 - 0.7311)^2 ≈ 0.0362
🔁 Iteration 1: Backward Pass (Gradient Descent)
Compute Gradients:
dL/dŷ = ŷ - y = 0.7311 - 1 = -0.2689
dŷ/dz = ŷ * (1 - ŷ) = 0.7311 * (1 - 0.7311) = 0.1966
dz/dw = x = 2
dL/dw = -0.2689 * 0.1966 * 2 ≈ -0.1057
Update Weight:
w = w - η * dL/dw = 0.5 - 0.1 * (-0.1057) = 0.5106
🔁 Iteration 2: Repeat
1️⃣ Forward Pass:
z = 0.5106 * 2 + 0 = 1.0211
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-1.0211)) ≈ 0.7342
2️⃣ Loss:
Loss = 0.5 * (1 - 0.7342)^2 ≈ 0.0353
3️⃣ Backward Pass:
dL/dw ≈ -0.1022
w = 0.5106 + 0.0102 = 0.5208
✅ Final Prediction After 2 Iterations
w = 0.5208, b = 0.0
z = 0.5208 * 2 = 1.0416
ŷ = 1 / (1 + e^(-1.0416)) ≈ 0.7411
So, the model predicts ŷ ≈ 0.7411 for input x = 2 after training.
🛡️ 4. Regularization (L2)
Add a penalty to avoid overfitting:
Loss_total = Loss + (λ / 2) * w^2
Example:
λ = 0.1, w = 0.5208
L2 Penalty ≈ 0.0136
Total Loss ≈ 0.0353 + 0.0136 = 0.0489
This penalizes large weights and helps in better generalization.
Day 8:
Embeddings:
Embeddings are dense vector representations of discrete data (like words, categories, etc.).
They transform high-dimensional or symbolic inputs into low-dimensional numeric vectors that capture meaning or relationships.



How is it different from One-Hot Encoding?



One-Hot Encoding Example:
Say we have categories: ["cat", "dog", "fish", "horse"]
"dog" → [0, 1, 0, 0]
No meaning, just position
🌈 Embedding Example (after training):
"dog" → [0.12, -0.45, 0.77]
"cat" → [0.13, -0.40, 0.79]
These vectors are close in space, meaning they're semantically similar
This is very useful in NLP, recommendation systems, and more.
Common Models for Creating Embeddings:
🔤 For Text (Word Embeddings)

A vector is a list (or array) of numbers that represents a piece of data like text, image, audio, or a product, in numerical form. 

A vector space is a mathematical environment where vectors (lists of numbers) live and can be compared, measured, or transformed.

this form of data type comes a set of operations that are specifically designed for vector manipulation. 
These operations are fundamental to many AI and machine learning tasks, such as recommendation systems, image recognition, and natural language understanding.
To summarize, these operations include:
Similarity comparison: Seeing if a given vector is similar to a reference vector, in meaning
A common operation for this is called Cosine Similarity - measuring the cosine of the angle between two vectors to determine their similarity. Vectors close in meaning have cosine similarity close to 1 (max is 1 for identical vectors) and those that are far away have low cosine similarity.
Nearest Neighbor Search: Identifying multiple close vectors in the dataset to a given vector.
In vector embedding space we have something called ANN or Approximate Nearest Neighbors,
which gives an approximate result which for genAI applications is sufficient.
Vector Addition and Subtraction: Performing mathematical operations on vectors to discover relationships or create new representations. For instance one of the seminal papers that introduced such vector embeddings demonstrated a jaw dropping result which did vector addition to show that
Vector Embeddings
Word2Vec is a neural network model developed by Google (2013) that learns to represent individual words as dense vectors in such a way that:
Words with similar meaning are close together in vector space.
Two Main Training Architectures
CBOW (Continuous Bag of Words):
Predicts the current word from surrounding context.
Skip-gram:
Predicts surrounding context words given the current word (often performs better on small data
Doc2Vec (aka Paragraph Vector) is an extension of Word2Vec introduced by Le and Mikolov (2014). It allows you to:
Get a vector representation of an entire document, sentence, or paragraph — not just individual words.
This is essential when you want to compare or retrieve entire documents based on meaning.
Representation
Sparse embeddings dominated NLP through techniques like one-hot encoding and TF-IDF vectors. Imagine representing the word “cat” in a vocabulary of 100,000 words: its sparse representation would be a vector of length 100,000 containing all zeros except for a single ’1’ in the position corresponding to “cat”.
Dense embeddings, introduced through models like Word2Vec, revolutionized this approach by mapping words or documents into a much lower-dimensional space (typically 100-1000 dimensions) where every dimension carries meaningful information.
Vector embeddings form the fundamental building blocks of modern Large Language Models (LLMs), serving as the bridge between human language and machine understanding.
 Every word, sentence, or token that enters a language model must first be converted into a vector representation that the model can process.  
A transformer model is a neural network that learns the context of sequential data and generates new data out of it.
To put it simply:
A transformer is a type of artificial intelligence model that learns to understand and generate human-like text by analyzing patterns in large amounts of text data.
How Transformers Work: A Detailed Exploration of Transformer Architecture | DataCamp
RAG WORKING
Document Processing Pipeline
Chunking Documents
Large documents are split into smaller, manageable chunks.
Creating Embeddings
Each chunk is converted into a vector using an embedding model (e.g., OpenAI's text-embedding-ada, Sentence-BERT, etc.).
Storing Vectors
These vector representations are stored in a vector database (e.g., Pinecone, FAISS, Weaviate) for fast similarity search.
Query Processing
Query Embedding
User query is passed through the same embedding model to get a vector.
Similarity Search
The system retrieves similar vectors (and thus text chunks) from the vector DB.
Context Injection
Retrieved content is used as additional context in the prompt to the LLM (like GPT) for generating responses.
Hybrid Search Systems
Semantic Search
Embedding models enable meaning-based retrieval, not just exact keyword matches.
Keyword + Semantic Hybrid
Combines keyword search (e.g., BM25) with vector search for higher accuracy.
Multimodal Capabilities
Supports search across multiple data types, including text, images, and potentially video or audio, using appropriate embeddings.
Embedding models

Most popular text embedding models and some of their characteristics:
all-MiniLM-L6-v2
all-mpnet-base-v2
paraphrase-multilingual-mpnet-base-v2
A transformer model is a neural network that learns the context of sequential data and generates new data out of it.
To put it simply:
A transformer is a type of artificial intelligence model that learns to understand and generate human-like text by analyzing patterns in large amounts of text data.
Word embedding 
is a technique used in natural language processing (NLP) to represent words as vectors of real numbers, so that machines can understand and process human language more effectively.
king   → [0.52, 0.81, ..., -0.14]
queen  → [0.48, 0.79, ..., -0.10]

These vectors are learned from text data, and they capture meaning and relationships.
Raw text consists of characters and words, which are not meaningful to a model. Word embeddings:
Allow mathematical operations on words
Capture semantic meaning (e.g., "good" is close to "great")
Help models generalize better
Raw text is just a sequence of characters and words, not meaningful to a model. Word embeddings:
Allow mathematical operations on words
Capture semantic meaning (e.g., "good" is close to "great")
Help models generalize better

Word embeddings capture semantic meaning because words with similar meanings tend to occur in similar contexts, and training adjusts vectors to reflect that.


A word embedding is a 1D vector like (300,), not (1 column with rows).
A sentence embedding is typically a 2D matrix: (num_words, embedding_dim)
What is Encoder-Decoder Architecture?
It’s a two-part neural network where:
 1. Encoder
Reads the input sequence (e.g., "I love you")
Encodes it into a fixed-length vector (called the context or hidden state)
Captures the semantic meaning of the input
2. Decoder
Takes the encoded context and generates the output sequence
Produces one token at a time (e.g., "Je", then "t'", then "aime")
Core Idea of Transformrs
Instead of processing words one by one (like RNNs), the Transformer processes entire sequences in parallel using a mechanism called self-attention.
🏗️ High-Level Structure
text
CopyEdit
               ┌─────────────┐
Input → Token →│  Encoder    │→ Context Representation
               └─────────────┘
                          ↓
               ┌─────────────┐
              ←│  Decoder    │→ Output Tokens
               └─────────────┘


🔍 Detailed View
1. Input Embedding + Positional Encoding
Words are converted to vectors (embeddings)
Since Transformers have no recurrence, they add positional encoding to track the order of tokens
2. Encoder
Each Encoder block (there are usually many) consists of:
pgsql
CopyEdit
[Input]
 ↓
Multi-Head Self-Attention  (token attends to all other tokens)
 ↓
Add & LayerNorm
 ↓
Feed Forward Network
 ↓
Add & LayerNorm


Self-Attention helps each word focus on other important words in the input
Multi-Head Attention = multiple attention operations in parallel
3. Decoder
Similar blocks to encoder, but adds cross-attention:
mathematica
CopyEdit
[Shifted Output Input]
 ↓
Masked Multi-Head Self-Attention (can’t look at future words)
 ↓
Cross-Attention (attends to encoder output)
 ↓
Feed Forward
 ↓
Output Token Prediction


🔁 Self-Attention (The Heart of It)
Each token looks at all other tokens and weighs them to decide what's important.
For example:
vbnet
CopyEdit
Sentence: "The cat sat on the mat"
→ Attention: "sat" pays more attention to "cat"


Attention Formula (simplified):
mathematica
CopyEdit
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q × Kᵗ / √d) × V


Where:
Q = Query
K = Key
V = Value
(All derived from input embeddings)
⚙️ Transformer Architecture Summary
📚 Real-World Examples
https://www.datacamp.com/tutorial/how-transformers-work
